- vi - 
 
 
 
PREFACE 
 
In mid-2018, the Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and Range (MoLFR) of the Federal Government of 
Somalia (FGS) began preparation of the Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy (LSDS), with 
technical assistance from the World Bank and the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) of the 
United Nations. Under the leadership of MoLFR, an extensive consultative and participatory 
approach involving all Federal Member States (FMSs), other relevant stakeholders (public, private 
and civil society) and teams of specialists was adopted during all phases of the LSDS preparation. 
Consultation workshops were held with MoLFR and Benadir (October 14 th -15th, 2018), South-West 
(16th -17th), Somaliland (20th -21th), Puntland (23th-24th), Galmudug (28th-29th), Hirshabelle (30th -31th) 
and Jubaland (November 3th-4th, 2018), while a validation workshop was conducted in Mogadishu 
with all stakeholders (June 17th-19th, 2019). This LSDS therefore fully captures the livestock sector’s 
main challenges and opportunities as well as a common vision for its development by 2030 among 
all stakeholders. 
The economic and social development of Somalia lies in its ability to exploit sustainably its natural 
resource base, which is increasingly buffeted by a fragile natural environment and climate change, while 
at the same time fostering shared growth and lifting the health and educational status of its people. This 
requires the public sector to adopt enabling and effective policies, strategies, institutions, laws and 
regulations and to provide key public goods (including essential infrastructure and services) that can 
increase private investment across the productive sectors. Livestock remains the backbone of the Somali 
economy in terms of production value, value added, and foreign currency earnings, and the vast majority 
of Somalia’s population is dependent on livestock and its products for their livelihoods and their food and 
nutrition security. The failure to develop the country’s livestock sector, including strengthening its 
resilience to climate change and ensuring the quality and safety of its products would, thus, would greatly 
constrain the country’s prospects for economic growth and recovery. 
The FGS, led by H.E. Prime Minister Hassan Ali Khayre, and the MoLFR are committed to improving the 
livelihoods of millions of pastoralists and agro-pastoralists, traders, processors and rural communities 
across Somalia who are dependent on the livestock sector. It welcomes the collaboration of its domestic 
and external partners to maximize the available technical and financial support to realize its potential as a 
major contributor to the country’s sustainable, inclusive, and equitable socio-economic development and 
poverty reduction. An imperative to achieve these goals will be to establish partnerships with 
international organizations and academic institutions, including for North-South, South-South and 
triangular cooperation on scientific and research projects and programs, which can help put this sector’s 
strategy and other innovative and creative ideas into action, deliver the desired results and impacts for all 
the sector’s stakeholders, producers and consumers alike. 
MoLFR would like to thank the officials of all governmental and non-governmental organizations, the 
technical teams at the federal and state levels, and all other relevant stakeholders (public, private and 
civil society) who participated in formulating the Somalia LSDS. Furthermore, the FGS wishes to express 
its deep gratitude to the joint team of the World Bank and the FAO for their strong dedication, effective 
technical contribution, and sustained efforts in coordinating inputs from many Somali and external 
experts, while respecting and supporting the leadership and ownership of the process by MoLFR. A 
special appreciation is also extended to the international community and donors for their continuous 
support and partnership towards the development and sustainable growth of Somalia’s livestock sector, 
its economy, and its development objectives. 
 
 
Hon. Hussein Mohamud Sheikh-Hussein, MP. 
Minister, Livestock Forestry and Range 
Mogadishu, July 2019

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS 
 
This report has been conceptualized and prepared by the Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and Range 
(MoLFR) of the Federal Government of Somalia, with technical assistance from the World Bank and the 
Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO). Financial support for core staff, consultants, and for the in- 
country state-level consultation workshops during October-November 2018 and the validation workshop 
in June 2019 was provided by the World Bank and FAO. 
The Ministry would like to express its utmost gratitude to His Excellency Prime Minister Hassan Ali Khaire 
of the Federal Government of Somalia (FGS), who resolutely introduced the Economic Development 
Roadmap initiative, known as “Tubta” in the Somali language, of which MoLFR is a member. The Somalia 
Livestock Sector Development Strategy (LSDS) is a key part of the planned activities by MoLFR under this 
initiative and its preparation within a period of only one year is a miraculous achievement. It is a great 
pleasure, an honor and a privilege that Somalia LSDS will be implemented under the auspices of Hon. 
Hussein Mohamud Sh. Hussein (MP) the Minister, Livestock, Forestry & Range of the Federal Government 
of Somalia. As envisioned, the implementation of this LSDS holds strong scope to drive sustainable and 
resilient growth of Somalia’s livestock sector and to make an important contribution to the country’s 
socio-economic recovery, poverty reduction gains and long-term development.

- viii - 
 
 
 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
 
A Bold Vision to Tackle Constraints and Harness Potential of the Largest Economic Sector 
Somalia’s livestock sector has proved remarkably resilient amidst headwinds of fragile 
governance, social unrest and recurrent droughts. Despite absent or very weak public services, an 
increasingly narrow and fragile natural resource base, and an arid and semi-arid climate that has 
become drier, more extreme, and more variable in recent decades, the sector has until recent years 
posted strong growth, reaching 62 percent of GDP at its peak in 2013-2016 from 37 percent pre-war. 
Even at the height of a severe drought in 2017, the sector remained the largest contributor (43 
percent) to GDP, employment and livelihoods. 
The livestock sector remains key to economic growth and poverty reduction, but its potential is 
hampered by structural weaknesses and by acute exposure to downside risks. The sector’s high 
vulnerability to natural and economic shocks was only recently evidenced by an estimated $2 billion 
in damages and losses suffered during the 2016/17 severe drought period and a drastic drop by  
2018 of $183 million in live animal exports after Saudi Arabia and the UAE imposed an import ban on 
health grounds in December 2016 (from $430 million in 2016; the drop would be even larger if 
compared to the peak $494 million of 2015). Major production constraints related to poor natural 
resource management, health, nutrition, and genetic resources are compounded by other structural 
and institutional weaknesses that impede sustainable growth, diversification and value addition. 
Nonetheless, despite current challenges, there is strong scope for the sector’s recovery and  
growth in the medium- and long-terms. Sizeable areas of the country are suitable for grazing, 
browsing, and fodder production. At an estimated 56 million heads in 2018, the country has one of 
the largest animal populations in the world (ranking 1st for camels and 6th for goats in 2015). 
Production of milk and meat has grown steadily to satisfy a growing domestic demand in its urban 
centers, supported by large remittance inflows. And its close proximity to, and favorable consumer 
preferences in, Gulf markets provide Somalia with a competitive advantage for the export of live 
animals and animal products. 
Success in tackling the sector’s constraints and harnessing its potential will require a clear vision of 
the priority policy reforms and interventions needed to alleviate the most binding constraints and 
concerted action across federal and state levels to mobilize and guide public and private 
investment, with strong, coordinated support by its development partners. The shared vision by 
the Federal Government and Federal Member States and by the key stakeholders is for the livestock 
sector to make a major contribution by 2030 to: i) improve livelihoods of the majority of the rural 
population by strengthening the sector’s climate resilience and increasing incomes from livestock 
products; (ii) enhance the nutrition and food security of its people via an expanded supply of high- 
quality and hygienic animal proteins; and iii) fuel stronger economic growth via more efficient, 
inclusive and environmentally sustainable livestock production and expanded value-adding 
processing. 
The vision and its objectives, principles, and operational priorities as articulated above were first 
outlined in the Livestock Policy 2015-2020, embedded in the NDP 2017-2019, and were 
subsequently reconfirmed by key federal, state and private stakeholders during broad-based 
stakeholder consultations during October-November 2018. If such a vision is achieved, the sector 
will not only provide a major contribution to the country’s overall economic development, but will 
also help cement peace and security, alleviate poverty and malnutrition, and enhance health 
outcomes in both rural and urban areas. Among its key operational goals are: 
 
Preserving Animal Resources - Safeguarding the diversity and optimizing the use of existing 
animal resources is critical for both resilience and growth.
=== PAGE 10 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- ix - 
 
 
 
 
Improving the management of the livestock industry – Also critical is to improve productivity, 
including for the rapidly expanding dairy, meat and poultry farming subsectors;
 
Ensuring the health and quality of live animals and livestock products in domestic and 
international markets – Stronger food safety is needed to protect consumers, to regain lost 
export markets for live animals, and to expand into new ones, including for livestock products;
 
Increasing the processing of livestock products – The sector’s long-term growth is dependent on 
higher value addition, as the current trajectory of increasing animal populations, supplying 
domestic and export markets with live animals, and minimal processing has reached its limit;
 
Building public and private service and research institutions, and human capacity – Public and 
private investment in finding solutions to current and emerging problems is currently lacking, 
but is necessary for both resilience and sustainable growth of the sector; and
 
Ensuring security throughout the country – Without enhanced security in many areas of 
southern and central regions of Somalia, rehabilitation and development interventions and new 
business ventures would remain challenging and costly, thus attracting limited donor funding 
and private investment.
 
To inform and guide needed action, this Livestock Sector Development Strategy (LSDS) provides a 
coherent, nationwide framework for the medium- and long-term development and growth of the 
sector, reflecting the priorities and needs of its key stakeholders beyond those related to short- 
term recovery from the recent 2016/2017 drought. It is largely informed and benefits from 
extensive consultations during 2018 and 2019 with public and private stakeholders who shared their 
vision, challenges, and priority recommendations. The LSDS will also provide a timely contribution to 
the National Development Plan II that will guide in 2020-2024 public and private investments, as well 
as external assistance from external donors and development agencies. 
 
Institutional Strengthening to Support Public and Private Interventions 
This LSDS supports a co-management framework based on collaboration among public and private 
institutions. This is needed to fill knowledge gaps, improve the management of the rangelands, 
deliver core animal health services, rebuild and expand production and trade infrastructure, and 
promote investment in processing and value-addition. To render PPP arrangements effective, 
efficient and accountable, however, the roles and responsibilities of public and private agents must 
be more clearly defined and coordinated. Furthermore, a consultative and participatory approach 
requires that livestock stakeholders have a voice in policy formulation and project design and 
implementation through producer/pastoralist associations, civil society organizations and/or other 
sector interest groups. 
The livestock sector falls under the responsibility of the Federal Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and 
Range (MoLFR), with the engagement of the livestock ministries of the Federal Member States 
(FMSs). All ministries, however, have major capacity and resource gaps, which inhibit their ability to 
deliver even the most basic services. Furthermore, budget allocations and physical facilities are very 
limited, with most staff lacking updated training and needed specializations. The most recently 
established states in southcentral Somalia, in particular, lack any physical infrastructure and receive 
virtually no budgetary resources and external funding, with most staff working as unpaid volunteers. 
The capacity needs of the public sector to effectively support the sustainable growth of production 
and trade by the private sector are extensive. They start with lack of pay for virtually all ministry 
staff in the recently established FMS, who are volunteering their time and operating without any 
vehicles and other supplies. They extend to policy formulation and review, planning, coordination 
and implementation of emergency and development programs and projects, drafting and 
enforcement of laws and regulations, and data collection and analyses. The list continues with
=== PAGE 11 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- x - 
 
 
 
research and extension; monitoring and compliance of, and service provision to, private businesses; 
and strengthening of public veterinary services. Furthermore, the MoFLR requires technical and 
financial support from external development partners to establish, with engagement of the FMS line 
ministries, forest and range reservation lands and sustainable range land management; to 
rehabilitate, improve and expand water infrastructures and, under public-private partnerships, 
wholesale and retail market facilities. The relevant ministries need to ensure quality higher 
education in veterinary science, animal husbandry, and food processing. The FGS, moreover, needs 
to provide adequate security to facilitate access to finance for the private sector so that it is able to 
invest in value added processing of livestock products. All stakeholders need also training along the 
main input and product value chains (feed and fodder, milk, meat, hides and skins, and poultry). 
Particular attention in the design and implementation of this strategy has been given to women 
and youth, whose engagement in livestock trading and marketing has been traditionally at the 
local and subsistence levels and whose equitable benefits and participation in the future 
development of the sector should be ensured. The live animal export value chain provides at 
present the bulk of the employment opportunities, including for traders, both small- and large-scale, 
brokers, trekkers, feedlot operators, lorry drivers, loaders and others providing ancillary services. 
However, large employment (and income) opportunities, especially for women and youth, lie in the 
collection, processing and distribution of livestock products (outputs, such as dairy and meat, and 
inputs such as fodder). 
 
Modernizing Traditional Systems for Production and Marketing of Live Animals 
Both resilience to climate shocks and expansion of livestock production are constrained by several 
factors linked to the nature of traditional production systems. The key constraints include: (i) 
degradation and deforestation of the rangelands; (ii) shortages of water, largely due to insufficient 
rainfall harvesting facilities; (iii) high incidence of diseases and weak systems for the monitoring and 
response to disease threats; and (iv) inadequate availability of natural feed and commercial fodder. 
Another set of institutional and structural constraints and gaps is related to the lack of and/or 
inconsistent implementation of regulations and policies, and insufficient knowledge and data about 
animal populations, offtake rates, incidence of diseases, and domestic consumption, among others. 
Somalia conducted its last national census of livestock in 1975, and subsequent animal population 
and offtake estimates are largely based on limited surveys and extrapolations. Thus, reliable 
information, including a comprehensive animal population survey, is needed. 
There is still no consensus in the scientific literature over whether the Horn of Africa will 
experience more or less rainfall in coming decades. However, all studies and projections  by 
climate scientists agree on a warmer future, with increased variability and frequency of extreme 
rainfall events, with related cycles of intensified floods and drought. The current large livestock 
population estimated at 56 million, compared to historical pre-war levels of less than 40 million, 
moreover, has been putting a major strain on limited natural resources such as rangeland, 
vegetation cover, and water. Moreover, the increasing frequency of severe droughts has meant that 
rangelands have less time to rebound and recover before the next one. Recommended priority 
actions are: 
 
Large scale investments in watershed management and infrastructure that would mitigate the 
impact of extreme cycles of rainfall, floods and drought will be critical for the resilience of 
Somali livelihoods, including those dependent on livestock.
 
Improving the natural rangelands resource base is critical to sustaining the production and 
productivity of livestock, in parallel with improving livestock productivity and value addition.
=== PAGE 12 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xi - 
 
 
 
 
Efforts to improve land tenure management are also required to deal with grazing rights, the 
fast-expanding private enclosures on previously communal rangelands, and commercial crop- 
and grasses-producing areas
 
Ensure secure migration routes to maintain mobility of livestock both within the country and 
cross-border.
 
While local breeds are well adapted to the country’s harsh geographical and arid climate, poor 
breeding practices are a factor contributing to their low live weight and productivity, e.g. in milk 
and meat yields. Experiences from similar countries, such as Ethiopia, however, shows that the 
extensive pastoral production systems in challenging arid and semi-arid lands pose a big challenge 
for the feasibility, adaptability and sustainability of improved exotic breeds and hybrids. Such 
experiences instead clearly demonstrate the benefits of community-based breeding programs,  
which should remain the main core strategy to improve productivity while maintaining adaptive 
traits, though cross-breeding is recommended for cattle. 
Despite widespread livestock ownership, most Somalis have a diet composed more of cereals 
(home grown sorghum and maize and imported rice and wheat) and sugar and oil (also imported). 
This is complemented by consumption of animal products, mostly fresh milk and meat (including 
from poultry), which provide the most important source of protein. Within even pure pastoral 
households highly dependent on markets and basic food imports, Somalia is very vulnerable to 
market disruptions caused by conflict or by severe global market price hikes in commodities. A 
flourishing livestock export sector is thus crucial in financing the substantial annual import bill 
required to ensure food security for many Somalis. Disruptions in exports, therefore, have negative 
effects on pastoral households’ food security as they rely on animal sales to access food markets. 
Somalia’s close proximity to various export markets and favourable consumer preferences in the 
Gulf markets provide Somalia with a competitive advantage that can support growth of exports of 
live animals as well as of meat, hides and skins, and possibly other livestock products. To exploit 
this potential advantage, however, Somalia needs to address successfully the vexing issue of the 
reliability and credibility of its health certification system for exports, and to diversify and reduce 
both its export product concentration and its heavy reliance on a few export markets, which makes 
the live-animal export sector acutely vulnerable to exogenous risks. These markets include Saudi 
Arabia and the U.A.E. for shoats, Yemen and Oman for cattle and shoats, Kenya for cattle, and Egypt 
for camels. 
A deeper understanding of the dynamics and decision-making processes behind the recurring 
import bans is required, as are urgently needed interventions to address export ban and related 
market risks, including: 
 
Reassessing the relationship between public and private veterinary services and quarantine 
stations and the responsibilities of each stakeholder;
 
Using a unified Animal Health Certificate issued only by FGS public veterinarians, as already 
agreed among the FGS and the FMs and with Saudi Arabia, and as mandated by law;
 
Analyzing the cost of regulatory compliance and enforcement (for example the three-week 
quarantine and health certificate emission) for both public and private sector players;
 
Mainstream standard methods and procedures (SMPs) for export quarantines, already 
validated by IGAD member states.
 
Strengthening Animal Health, Welfare and Nutrition 
While the private sector and livestock professional associations (LPAs), accredited by the MoLFR 
and funded by development partners, have largely taken up the provision of critical veterinary
=== PAGE 13 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xii - 
 
 
 
services, such efforts remain insufficient to address the serious risk of animal diseases and 
consequent import bans. Moreover, animal welfare issues arise during loading, transporting and 
unloading of livestock, not so much because of mistreatment by their handlers, but mainly because 
of the inadequacy of available facilities and services, such as sanitation, nutrition, water, and 
veterinary care, both in rural areas and along transit routes. Even though the FGS adopted in 2016 a 
National Veterinary Law, there is need for a more comprehensive legislative and regulatory 
framework and for effective institutional arrangements for its implementation and enforcement. 
To improve animal health as well as to meet international standards and requirements of 
importing countries, this LSDS prioritizes the following interventions: 
 
Apply the OIE-PVS Pathway Tool to assess, monitor and improve veterinary services;
 
Establish a national agency for recording disease information and outbreaks reports and for 
conducting epidemiological studies;
 
Mainstream livestock health data in the OIE/AU-IBAR-managed information systems and within 
the framework of the One Health Framework;
 
Improve the administrative structure of the public veterinary services, and its collaboration 
with LPAs, entrepreneurs and producers’ cooperatives;
 
Make qualified manpower available in the field and as advisors to federal and state ministries;
 
Support the development of community-managed health services;
 
Monitor and promote proper drug quality and use to minimize the risk of antimicrobial 
resistance; and
 
Study animal welfare issues and train stakeholders in relevant international standards.
 
Ensuring year-round affordable good quality feed is important to improve animal quality, to 
mitigate lack of pasture and browse during seasonal or prolonged severe droughts, and to reduce 
the pressure of a large animal population on increasingly scarce natural resources. The feed 
subsector, however, has always been and is to this day characterized by a traditional low-input 
grazing and browsing system, which is subject to seasonal shortages of pasture and very limited 
commercial feed/fodder production. Other major challenges are degradation, deforestation and 
continuous overgrazing driving massive-scale soil erosion, desertification, and reduction in edible 
biomass on the rangelands, due to weaknesses of government agencies tasked to manage the 
country’s natural resources (especially since the demise of the pre-war National Range Agency) and 
the erosion of traditional rangeland management institutions. Other constraints and gaps are the 
uncontrolled expansion of enclosures on previously open rangeland, the low levels of technical 
knowledge on fodder production and storage, poor access to fodder seeds and other production 
inputs, inadequate availability of water, storage, and transport infrastructure, and in southern 
Somalia, unsettled land tenure issues. 
The 2016-2017 major prolonged drought that led to high mortality of livestock across the country, 
however, has stoked interests among investors and traders in producing, commercializing and 
storing improved feed and fodder from grasses, crops residues (mainly sorghum and maize stalks), 
and oil seeds. Development opportunities in this subsector can rely on much increased demand for 
fodder throughout the year by expanding commercial livestock rearing operations, field testing by 
some research institutes (e.g. SATG) of promising better grasses, and existing large-scale commercial 
production of cereals and oil seeds in some regions. Crop production could easily be doubled to 
reach again pre-war levels, if necessary investments in the rehabilitation of irrigation and flood 
control infrastructure will be made and in promoting the widespread adoption of smart agricultural 
techniques in rainfed cultivation. 
To address the major constraints and exploit available opportunities, the subsector would benefit 
from the following priority interventions related to water management and fodder supply:
=== PAGE 14 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xiii - 
 
 
 
 
Rehabilitate the existing water reservoirs and increase the number of water catchment 
facilities, both along the two major rivers and on the rangelands;
 
Conduct a feed and fodder inventory and balance assessment, to assess availability based on 
supply estimates and feed needs based on the estimates from the expected animal population 
survey;
 
Study potential feed production capacity, linkages of feed and fodder value chains with relevant 
subsectors, access to production areas and markets, and transport linkages between them and 
develop a livestock feed and rangeland strategy;
 
Analyze feed and fodder value chain linkages with all relevant subsectors, access to production 
areas and markets and transport linkages between them
 
Consider market support schemes to stimulate and facilitate inclusion of distant communities;
 
Support technical training on feed and fodder production;
 
Support private sector and cooperative investment in feed and fodder production; and
 
Re-establish a rangeland management agency and enforce pre-war and/or more recent policies 
and laws (and traditional systems) to rehabilitate and manage the rangelands.
 
Ensuring Food Safety and Promoting Value Addition in Livestock Products 
The main constraints that have kept milk production and the processed dairy sector from reaching 
its potential are both institutional and structural. However, demand for fresh milk and other 
processed dairy products will continue to increase in all states beyond the natural population 
growth, which alone will provide over a million new potential consumers in the next decade. Major 
growth constraints include most of those affecting negatively the health and welfare of live animals 
as well as the poor food safety practices and poor environment for private sector investment. 
Promoting more varied dairy and by-products of meat production and consumption, including from 
poultry, would have positive nutritional outcomes for the country’s population, besides being an 
additional source of income for Somali pastoralists and agro-pastoralists. 
Dairy-subsector stakeholders see strong prospects for value-addition via processing 
(pasteurization and yogurt and cheese production, among others), if supported by an appropriate 
public policy and robust private investment. With regards to the prospects for dairy processing, two 
recent studies by Oxfam and the FAO-Investment Centre found that most consumers perceive fresh 
milk to be of superior quality and hence prefer that to any other dairy product on the market. Thus, 
they concluded that value addition via processing of milk may not be profitable, as demand for 
pasteurized milk and other dairy products represents a small niche market segment, as most buyers 
are unwilling or unable to pay for processing of any kind. The governments’ and stakeholders’ more 
optimistic view, however, is rooted in the current poor and inefficient state of the subsector, the 
large number of available milk-producing animals, the commercialization trend that has already 
started, and the high, growing and more sophisticated domestic demand linked to rapid 
urbanization, higher incomes, and diaspora returnees. This view is also buttressed by international 
comparisons and by trade statistics, in particular, the large bill in recent years for imported dairy 
products, ranging between US$60 million and $82 million annually, mostly for milk powder and long- 
life packaged milk. 
Despite many challenges, a market-oriented peri–urban camel and cattle production system has 
been emerging around many towns across Somalia, where the sale of raw milk is already a very 
important economic activity. The growth opportunities for milk producers in the peri-urban context 
are based and depend upon mainly the remaining availability of fertile rangelands near many peri- 
urban areas, opportunities to deploy solar and wind power to reduce energy costs, especially for  
cold chains, and improved livestock management and feeding practices that can raise productivity by 
reducing seasonal fluctuations without expanding herd size.
=== PAGE 15 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xiv - 
 
 
 
In addition to improving subsector specific knowledge, there is a need to strengthen the 
subsector’s organisation and management and to draw private investment for its expansion and 
modernization. The immediate priority interventions proposed from the literature and the 
stakeholders are: 
 
Improve veterinary and extension services to control the spread of animal diseases, improve 
the health of the peri-urban herds, and meet hygienic standards for milk handling and 
transport;
 
Strengthen government oversight and auditing of milk production, handling and transport 
facilities, to ensure food safety and that quality standards are applied effectively;
 
Support the establishment of a Dairy Board to represent peri-urban investors and serve as an 
entry point for discussion and private sector participation in policy dialogue;
 
Adopt a two-pronged approach to the sector’s development: promote safe raw fresh milk 
production and marketing for the rural population and processed dairy products, including 
yogurt made from camel milk, for urban and diaspora consumers;
 
Promote and facilitate credit access for, and private sector investment in, dairy processing 
(including cold chain infrastructure, technology, and business management); and
 
Foster widespread adoption, including by facilitating access to credit, of dairy processing and 
preservation techniques—to support expanded supply of products with a long shelf-life.
 
The meat value chain, like that for dairy, is also still almost exclusively focused on supplying the 
local market. Most slaughterhouses and meat markets are largely unhygienic, in various states of 
disrepair, and lack drainage systems. Many small and medium-size towns have no slaughterhouses 
at all. While there are signs of incipient commercialization, current processing is minimal, with only 
three meat processing companies operating in the country. The oldest in Mogadishu (SoMeat) has 
two modern slaughterhouses and exports frozen cattle carcasses to Oman (and in small quantities 
also to Qatar) in refrigerated containers, with plans to invest in the machinery required for 
producing and exporting cuts. The other two operations on the outskirts of Hargeisa (Hilibsan) and 
Galkayo (Beira Food Factory) to process and sell meat in local markets in a hygienic way, have been 
recently established but have plans to expand, including into meat canning. 
Unlike for dairy, however, there are strong opportunities for expanding meat exports to Gulf 
States and in the broader East African region, where demand is projected to increase. In addition, 
there are prospects of selling meat to AMISOM troops in the country. Meat export prospects, 
however, need to be further analyzed vis-à-vis other countries like Pakistan, whose exports have 
already displaced Somali meat in the UAE market. Furthermore, an expansion of frozen  meat 
exports via sea beyond Mogadishu and Berbera, the only two container ports in the country, looks 
unlikely and that of chilled meat beyond major cities with international passenger flights may not be 
cost-effective, due to the high cost of operating pure cargo aircrafts. 
Based on the above issues as outlined, the following priority interventions are thus recommended 
for the meat subsector’s development: 
 
Approve and implement a federal Meat Inspection Act;
 
Support public and private investments and PPP arrangements for the establishment and 
management of modern slaughterhouses and cold chains in all urban centers, after clarifying 
roles and responsibilities of investors and supervising agencies;
 
Improve integration of backyard producers of goat meat with slaughterhouses and cold chains;
 
Study the constraints and opportunities for developing a thriving offal industry; and
 
Offer training on proper food (meat) handling practices for stakeholders across the value chain;
=== PAGE 16 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xv - 
 
 
 
Hides and skins, cured mostly to sun-dried or dry-salted stages with only some processed to wet- 
blue stage, albeit of very poor quality, barely register today in the trade statistics because of the 
extremely low price they fetch. They are nonetheless still exported, suggesting potential 
international demand for a revival of exports from Somalia that would provide significant 
opportunities to livelihoods, especially in rural areas, as was indeed the case before the war. To 
harness this potential, it would be necessary to implement the following priority recommendations: 
 
Undertake studies that look at the economics of production and marketing of hides and skins 
and assessing requirements and preferences in existing and potential export markets;
 
Collect statistical information about the subsector (starting under the planned livestock 
population survey) and about the constraints to attracting foreign investment from the 
importing countries;
 
Formulate an enabling policy and regulatory framework for a future tanning industry;
 
Formulate subsector and product quality standards that are based on international 
requirements;
 
Support and strengthen organizational capacity of hides and skins trading associations;
 
Encourage private investment in technology upgrading of existing and new slaughterhouses 
and tanneries, including via tax incentives and rebates; and
 
Expand access to finance for traders and processors in hides and skins trade.
 
The poultry subsector remains underdeveloped, with poor linkages between producers, market 
and consumers, and minimal external donor support. However, because of an expanded 
commercial production and related consumption, poultry has become increasingly important to 
Somalia’s food security and protein availability. Despite poor knowledge of the sector and myriad 
growth bottlenecks, government and private stakeholders believe that the available high market 
demand underlies a strong potential for expanding and upgrading the poultry subsector. 
The recommended priority interventions for the development of the poultry subsector, so that it 
can contribute to strengthening food security and complement traditional Somali livestock, are 
mainly focused on modernizing and professionalizing the entire value chain, as follows: 
 Create an enabling legal and regulatory environment that encourages private investment;
 Explore PPP arrangements for the development of a local chicken feed mill industry;
 Promote investments in hatcheries, if it is determined that there is sufficient demand;
 Provide extension services on poultry-specific animal health services; and
 Develop appropriate policies, regulations and guidelines for hygiene, production and packaging 
standards.
 
Beekeeping remains a small-scale side business for most beekeepers, who have only a weak 
understanding of modern production, handling and safety techniques, with honey consumption 
mostly limited to local markets. There are, however, several socio-economic benefits  of 
beekeeping, especially for rural women. Unlike other agricultural enterprises, beekeeping is less 
affected by climate-change and honey sales greatly reinforce women’s income sources during 
drought periods when there are few alternatives. Small producers and traders use income from 
honey sales to purchase food and invest in education and other household items, thus diversifying 
their livelihoods options. Furthermore, honey is a popular product, widely believed to possess 
medicinal properties and thus locally in high demand. Prices for raw honey in recent years have been 
very high, thus profitable for beekeepers. Stakeholders’ recommendations for the subsector’s 
development are to: 
 
provide extension services on production and handling practices and on market opportunities;
=== PAGE 17 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xvi - 
 
 
 
 
facilitate their access to modern production and protective equipment;
 
support the establishment by the private sector of honey refineries, to add value; and
 
launch an awareness campaign to educate the public on beekeeping as a supplementary source 
of income and livelihood, and of its benefits in pollinating trees and crops.
 
Financial and Human Resources for the Sector’s Development 
Few financial services, with the exception of money transfers and some microcredit, are available 
to Somali pastoralists, due to their inability to pledge collateral. Livestock insurance is not available 
at all. Furthermore, traditionally women in pastoral and agro-pastoral communities have faced even 
greater barriers than men in accessing finance. There is a need to increase financial access 
throughout livestock value chains, including via enhancing financial literacy, formal financial credit 
services, and innovative digital financial services provision. Moreover, adoption of risk transfer 
instruments like livestock insurance, as for example is already the case in Kenya, would be a useful 
tool for managing drought shocks in pastoral areas. 
Mostly private technical secondary schools and higher education institutions in veterinary sciences 
and animal husbandry lack regulations and standards usually set by governmental authorities 
responsible for education at the local, state or federal level. They also lack the infrastructure, 
financial resources and systemic governmental and institutional checks and balances that ensure 
effective institutional operations. As a result, the quality of teaching and learning is weak and 
inadequate to meet the educational demands of Somalia’s livestock sector. The sector’s educational 
institutions, moreover, have the added disadvantage of not attracting female students. 
Key recommendations for strengthening educational policy, regulations and institutions and the 
capacity of both public and private technical schools and universities to meet the needs of the 
livestock sector include: 
 
Enact national education legislation and regulations to improve the quality of programs offered, 
including those in veterinary science and animal husbandry, by standardizing the academic 
curriculum and establishing higher-education commissions at federal and state levels;
 
Enhance monitoring and evaluation of performance and enforcement of regulations by the 
education and livestock ministries over the sector’s educational institutions;
 
Build capacity of faculty of veterinary and animal husbandry educational institutions to improve 
the quality of teaching and learning.
 
External Development Assistance: Challenges and Lessons Learned 
External assistance during the past decade has been highly concentrated on Somaliland, Puntland, 
and the federal government, with only one recognized project covering southcentral Somalia. 
Most interventions have aimed to strengthen animal health systems, the policy and regulatory 
environment, and private sector development. Activities to accomplish these goals included training 
sessions, workshops, knowledge and technology sharing, bringing together stakeholders, capacity 
building, creation of cooperatives, and trade facilitation. These objectives were and are indeed still 
relevant to the development needs of the country, the livestock sector, and the targeted 
beneficiaries. However, some major binding constraints have remained unaddressed. For instance, 
while many projects focused on animal health or market linkages, they did not address 
environmental issues such as degradation and deterioration of pastures and rangelands, even 
though these issues were widely recognized across projects. Similarly, few (none in southcentral 
Somalia) projects focused on rehabilitating watering facilities or fostering commercial feed supplies 
and markets.
=== PAGE 18 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xvii - 
 
 
 
External development partners have generally expressed a high degree of confidence in the 
sustainability of the outcomes of their interventions. However, the sustainability of project benefits 
remains unclear, given the many daunting implementation issues that they faced, including major 
construction delays (in the case of slaughterhouses and fish processing plants) and outright failures 
(as in the case of cold chains) in ensuring hand-overs and operations of facilities that they built in the 
northern regions, as well as the lack of rigorous impact evaluations and slow progress on domestic 
resource mobilization. Implementation challenges were related to poor education/skills availability; 
weak institutional capacity; narrow project design; limited advance planning for hand-over of project 
assets to local communities or to the private sector and related management issues; poor donor 
coordination, beneficiary involvement and poor sector governance; dilapidated infrastructure; and 
insecurity. 
Key lessons learned from donors and development agencies’ self-assessments and independent 
evaluations are summarized below: 
 
There needs always be a major skills and capacity building component in all projects. While 
long procurement delays and slow delivery should be expected, using local procurement, 
sourcing in-country expertise, and engaging private and community stakeholders and local 
authorities in project design and implementation would enhance capacity building, ownership 
and chances for project success.
 
Because of insecurity in some areas affecting government officials and foreign personnel, 
partnering with private sector and local community organizations is necessary for project 
implementation.
 
A collaborative approach among different institutions that can leverage different expertise and 
strengths and enhance technical coordination is also critical for project success.
 
Value chains often require good understanding of social dynamics, as industry stakeholders 
may value their relationships more than pure economic gains.
 
Selection of project sites must be transparent, evidence-based and technical criteria-driven.
 
Strong awareness is needed by regulators and other value chain actors of SPS and quality 
standards prescribed by the OIE and/or required by importing countries, to avoid trade 
restrictions.
 
A pooled and programmatic approach that layers different activities, has longer timeframes, 
and provides flexibility is essential in fragile and complex environments such as Somalia.
 
Shocks and disasters are highly likely and thus their risk should be addressed at project design 
stage.
 
Linkages with other sectors and ministries should be considered, as multi-sectoral approaches 
to strengthen resilience and disaster management are more effective than just a one-sector 
approach.
 
Suitable technology that is geographically, environmentally, climatically, economically and 
culturally appropriate should be used to ensure resilience, reduce risk and increase 
sustainability.
 
Local animal species, while less productive, are better adapted to survive the conditions of the 
local environment; thus, improving herd genetics with foreign breeds can only be successful if 
required inputs are available and is better undertaken by relying on community-based breed 
improvement programs.
 
Conclusions and Next Steps 
As summarized above and elaborated in the report in further detail, the livestock sector remains 
key to economic growth and poverty reduction. However, the sector’s performance and prospects 
are undermined by considerable structural weaknesses, severe exposure to downside risks, and 
vulnerability to climate and market shocks. The main constraints for the sector’s development are:
=== PAGE 19 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xviii - 
 
 
 
 
Weak institutional and policy framework, including limited and largely informal organization of 
the livestock value chain; lack of coordination and integration among its actors; weak human 
capacity; minimal or zero budgetary resources; lack of enforcement of existing rules and 
regulations; and poor linkages and ineffective negotiations with main trading partners; 
 
Dilapidated water and transport and inefficient energy infrastructure (e.g. insufficient and 
irregular and inadequately harvested rainfall and broken pavement) causing water shortages 
and higher transport and marketing costs than necessary, all of which affect negatively livestock 
health and survival, availability of natural fodder, delivery of quality animals to markets, and 
expansion of dairy production and processing; 
 
Lack of access to good quality pasture, due to land degradation and reduction of productive 
land, which is in turn the result of deforestation, soil erosion, invasion of unpalatable plant 
species, spread of private enclosures, and climate change bringing increasingly frequent 
recurrence of drought and floods; 
 
Poor veterinary health services and transboundary animal disease control, with occasional flare- 
up of epidemics, other livestock diseases, and pests, compounded by limited availability of 
quality veterinary education and technical training facilities in pastoral areas; 
 
Overreliance on only a few export markets and on live animal exports; 
 
Financial constraints – with very low government budgetary allocations to the livestock sector 
and private investment in processing for value addition, partly due to poor access to credit and 
no access to insurance, and lack of incentives and encouragement by governments; and 
 
Persistent insecurity in large parts of southcentral Somalia. 
Despite the daunting challenges of overcoming these constraints, there is in fact strong scope for 
the sector’s recovery and growth in the medium- and long-term, if managed properly, in both live 
animals and livestock products. This LSDS reflects the key sector needs and strategic development 
priorities of the FGS, its FMS, and the sector’s key stakeholders, beyond those related to 
emergencies and short-term recovery from shocks. The key goals this LSDS has endorsed are to: 
 
Strengthen the production and marketing systems for live animals, via improving 
environmental stewardship of the country’s rangelands, including their adaptation to an 
increasingly threatening climate change; health services, including the credibility of health 
certifications; and feed and fodder availability; 
 
Develop the many value addition opportunities available in livestock products, via promoting 
investments in processing and quality improvements, while ensuring food safety. 
 
A comprehensive and ambitious Master Plan for the sector’s development, however, needs to be 
developed in order to operationalize this LSDS’ priority and medium-term recommendations. Also 
needed are quantifiable targets for the achievement of the LSDS objectives, as well as specific 
interventions (policy and institutional changes, technical assistance and investment projects), with 
estimated costs. proposed timeline and clear roles of government and external development 
partners. These interventions should clarify and address the following areas: 
 
Overall sector governance and institutional reform, including (i) assignment of responsibilities 
among all levels of governments; (ii) capacity building; (iii) broad participation, including by 
women, youth and disadvantaged groups, in policy formulation, institutional reforms, project 
design and implementation; and (iv) effective aid coordination and partnership with 
international donors and development agencies; 
 
Animal production and marketing systems, including effective rangeland management, land 
tenure, and climate change adaptation; 
 
Animal health services (and credible health certifications for exports), nutrition, and welfare; 
 
Production and marketing of dairy, meat, hides and skins (for exports), poultry and beekeeping 
products, to promote value added and ensure food security and safety;
=== PAGE 20 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- xix - 
 
 
 
 
Access to modern financial services, including insurance; and 
 
Human resource development, including capacity building for the public and private 
stakeholders and for technical and higher education in veterinary, food sciences and animal 
husbandry sciences. 
 
Under the recently approved “Water for Agro-pastoral Productivity and Resilience (WAPR), or 
Biyoole Project, the World Bank can financial support to the MoLFR’s for the preparation of such a 
Master Plan and related project proposals. However, to attract external funding to carry out an 
ambitious program of interventions, the FGS and FMS also need to clarify effective implementation 
mechanisms and a monitoring and evaluation framework that can assess the relevance of the 
objectives, effectiveness of interventions (outputs and outcomes), the performance of implementing 
agencies, domestic and external. To carry out all necessary next steps, and also to implement much- 
needed animal population survey and feed inventory and balance, the MoLFR is seeking technical 
assistance from its development partners during the second half of 2019 and through 2020. This will 
also ensure adequate input for the most important sector of the Somali economy into the National 
Development Plan II (2020-2022), currently under preparation.

   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 1 
 
 
 
 
 
1.0 
INTRODUCTION 
 
More than two decades after the start of a civil war and the collapse of the central government, 
which destroyed most of the country’s physical and socio-economic infrastructure, Somalia turned 
a page when a peaceful transfer of power from a transitional government in 2012 generated 
domestic political momentum and a new Federal Government of Somalia (FGS) obtained 
widespread international recognition in 2013. Four Federal Member States (FMS) have been 
established since in southern Somalia, Jubaland, Southwest, Galmudug and Hirshabelle. They 
complement two other longer-established states in the northern regions, Puntland and Somaliland. 
To date, businesses operate flexibly across all state borders, but there is a need for further 
improvement in the working relationship and coordination between the FGS and FMS towards 
creating a clearer and more favourable environment for regulatory and economic governance, 
including that related to interstate trade and exports, especially of livestock. Government at all 
levels, moreover, has remained primarily focused on addressing still formidable security and 
humanitarian challenges, to the detriment of providing essential oversight, services and 
infrastructure for inclusive and sustainable economic growth and development. Widespread 
environmental degradation—fuelled by demographic pressures, unsustainable exploitation of 
groundwater, rangelands and forests, and breakdown of customary and deep-rooted clan-based 
land management systems—and global climate change also threaten the viability of traditional 
livelihoods and the country’s food security. Further, continued conflict in the southern and central 
regions of the country remains a threat to Somalia’s overall political stability. 
While Somalia’s livestock sector faced and still faces formidable challenges, it has proved 
remarkably resilient amidst headwinds of social unrest and recurrent droughts, and the sector 
remains key to economic growth and poverty reduction. In contrast to a crop sector whose 
production is today only about half its pre-war peak, the livestock sector experienced surprisingly 
strong growth, including impressive export growth, in recent decades through the mid-2010s, largely 
due to substantial investments in infrastructure and service quality improvements by the Diaspora, 
foreign private companies and donor-funded programs. With almost half of the population still living 
in rural areas and engaged in some way in the livestock sector, the livestock sector remains the 
largest contributor to Somali GDP and the biggest source of foreign exchange after remittances. 
The sector’s performance and prospects, however, are undermined by considerable structural 
deficiencies and by exposure to downside risks. Production constraints related to poor resource 
management, climate change adaptation, animal disease control, poor nutrition, and genetic 
resources are compounded by other structural and institutional weaknesses that impede value 
added diversification and expose the sector to myriad risks. The sector’s vulnerability and fragility is 
evidenced by an estimated $2 billion in damages and losses suffered during the most recent 2016/17 
severe drought period and a drastic drop between 2016 and 2018 of $183 million (from $430 million 
in 2016) in live animal exports after Saudi Arabia and the UAE imposed an import ban on health 
grounds at the end of 2016. 
Despite current challenges, there is in fact strong scope for the sector’s recovery and growth in the 
medium- and long-term, if managed properly. Sizeable areas of the country are suitable for grazing, 
browsing, and fodder production. At an estimated 56 million heads in 2018, the country has one of 
the largest livestock populations in the world (and the largest camel population). Its close proximity
=== PAGE 22 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 2 
 
 
 
to and favourable consumer preferences in Gulf markets provide Somalia with a competitive 
advantage that can support export growth of live animals and animal products. Production of milk 
and meat, moreover, has grown steadily since the late 1980s to satisfy a growing urban demand in 
the country supported by large remittance inflows. Strengthening the sector’s resilience to 
increasingly frequent and severe weather shocks and improving its growth performance will not only 
provide a major contribution to the country’s overall economic development, but will also foster 
peace and security, alleviate poverty and malnutrition, and enhance health outcomes in both rural 
and urban areas. 
Success in tackling the sector’s constraints and harnessing its potential, however, requires a clear 
vision and strategy for the priority policy reforms and interventions needed across federal and 
state levels, with strong, coordinated support by its development partners, to alleviate the binding 
constraints and mobilize and guide public and private investment. In 2004, the Food and 
Agricultural Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the World Bank and the European Union (EU) 
collaborated with senior officials of the then Somalia Transitional National Government (TNG) in the 
development of a background document for a livestock sector strategy. Due to overwhelming 
concerns with political and security challenges in southern Somalia and the lack of external financing 
for development activities, the TNG did not adopt nor implement any development strategy or plan. 
This Livestock Sector Development Strategy (LSDS) provides an updated, coherent, nationwide 
vision and framework for the sector’s medium- and long-term development, reflecting the 
priorities and needs of its key stakeholders beyond those related to the sector’s short-term 
recovery from the recent 2016/2017 drought. It is based on a comprehensive literature review and 
two additional original studies on livestock-related higher education and on the peri-urban dairy 
value chain. Extensive consultations with public and private stakeholders on their vision, challenges, 
and priority recommendations were held via a series of state-level 2-day workshops conducted in 
October-November 2018 throughout the country (see Main Annex 1 and Online Annexes) and a 3- 
day validation workshop in June 2019 in Mogadishu (see Main Annex 2). The LSDS will also provide a 
timely contribution to the just-launched National Development Plan II that will guide in 2020-2024 
public and private investments, as well as external assistance from external donors and development 
agencies. 
This document is organized as follows. It starts by summarizing the country’s geography, its overall 
economic and trade performance, and its environmental and social context in Chapter 2. The 
livestock sector’s historical economic and social performance is discussed in Chapter 3, followed by a 
brief preview of its main development challenges. Chapter 4 presents the overall vision for the 
sector’s long-term development, including the roles of its public and private stakeholders and the 
capacity needs of federal and state livestock ministries. Chapter 5 discusses in more detail 
constraints, prospects, opportunities, and recommended interventions from the literature, from 
governments’ policy and planning documents and from stakeholders for the development of its live 
animals’ production and marketing systems, as well as two critical inputs, namely animal health and 
nutrition (water and feed). Chapter 6 similarly addresses the main product subsectors, that is, dairy, 
meat, hides and skins, poultry, and beekeeping. Chapter 7 discusses and presents challenges and 
recommendations related to cross-cutting sectoral issues, including lessons from external assistance. 
Chapter 8 concludes by discussing the next steps necessary to operationalize this sector’s 
development strategy.
=== PAGE 23 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 3 
 
 
 
2.0 
COUNTRY BACKGROUND 
 
2.1 
Climate and Geography1 
Somalia’s climate profile is generally arid and semi-arid with bi-modal rainfall. Very small, usually 
elevated areas have an annual average rainfall of 500–600 mm, but most of the country has an 
average rainfall that is only 100–200 mm, with rain mostly falling in isolated and heavy storms.  
The wettest regions have typically 40–60 rainy days each year with daily rainfall of the order of 5–15 
mm (Kammer, 1986). Open water evaporation usually far exceeds rainfall and is in the range 1,600– 
2,400 mm per year in the south of the country. The bimodal rainfall pattern has two rainy seasons 
throughout most of the country (less pronounced in the northern mountain region) known locally as 
the ‘Gu’ (April to June) and the ‘Deyr’ (October to November). These result from the north-south 
oscillation of the Inter-Tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ) and its related frontal systems. 
Droughts occur regularly at intervals of 2–3 years in the ‘Deyr’ and 8–10 years in both seasons 
(UNDP, 1987). The temporal patterns of high rainfall variability over Somalia is the main direct cause 
behind such extreme events impacting the country, including floods (UNDP/ICPAC, 2013). The 
occurrences of these extreme events happen when the Indian and Pacific Ocean experiences 
anomalous sea temperatures and circulation anomalies during El Niño/La Niña, together with IOD 
events. These include the recent droughts of 2000, 2004, 2008 and 2010-2011; and floods of 
1997/1998. Furthermore, analysis of global data from 1901-2005 shows temperatures have 
increased by 1.0°C over the course of the century, while inter-annual analyses of more recent 
national data shows that mean air temperatures in Somalia remain high throughout the year, 
ranging from 15–25°C in the northern mountains to 25–35°C in the southern regions. 
As a result, most of the country’s landmass covering an area of 638,000 square kilometers in the 
Horn of Africa is arid or desert, but also with a large semi-arid zone, and in southern Somalia a 
sizable humid zone. Nomadic pastoralism takes place mostly in the arid zone, agro-pastoralism in 
the semi-arid zone, and irrigated crop cultivation in the humid zone (see Figures 2.1 and 2.2), with 53 
percent of Somalia recently classified as rangeland (SWALIM 2014), compared to 45 percent in 1979 
(World Bank 1979). Another 30 percent is covered by sparse vegetation and less than 5 percent is 
suitable for either rainfed and irrigated cultivation (see Table 2.1 and for more details of land use 
systems also Figure SA1.1 and Table SA1.1 in Statistical Annex 1).

By 2015, according to the FAO, what was left was a total forest cover estimated at only about 10 
percent of the country’s land area, compared to 62 percent through 1980. 2 Already by the end of 
the 1980s, virtually all the floodplain forests had been cleared for irrigated agriculture production. 
 
 
2 See SDG Indicator 15.1.1 - Forest area (as a percentage of total land area) at http://www.fao.org/sustainable- 
development-goals/indicators/1511/en/ for 2015 and Openshow 1982 for an historical baseline.
=== PAGE 26 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 6 
 
 
2F 
 
Between 1990 and 2009, Somalia lost an average of 76,750 hectares, or 0.93 percent of its forests a 
year (FAO 2010). All the remaining forests, which include juniper trees in the northern Golis 
mountain range, tropical vegetation along the Juba and Shabelle rivers and on their adjacent 
floodplains and mangroves along its coast, are facing increased pressure of commercial exploitation. 
The related progressive destruction of plant life has already impaired animal habitats and reduced 
forage, affecting Somalia’s wildlife and its greatest food resource, its livestock. 
Somalia's terrain consists mainly of plateaus, plains, and highlands. However, in the far north, the 
Karkaar Mountains lie at varying distances from the coast of the Gulf of Aden. In the north, a 
maritime plain that parallels the Gulf of Aden coast is covered with guban, a semi-arid Scrub well 
suited for grazing livestock in the rainy season. The mountain ranges descend to the south, forming 
an elevated plateau without perennial rivers, which include shallow valleys with mostly dry 
watercourses and is called the Ogo. The Ogo in the central part form the Mudug plain while the 
eastern part gently undulates towards the Indian Ocean. This plateau merges in the Haud to the 
south forming broad undulating plains best for grazing by the nomads, despite having inadequate 
rainfall for a greater part of the year. 
Livelihoods in Somalia are highly dependent on the ecosystem, but climate change, the spread of 
invasive species and past scale of charcoal production for exports has resulted in environmental 
degradation in most parts of the country. The Horn of Africa is one of only two biodiversity hotspots 
that are mostly arid and semi-arid, holding more endemic reptiles than any other region in Africa. 
Unfortunately, this region is also one of the most degraded in the world, with only about 5 percent 
of its original habitat remaining, caused largely by overgrazing and charcoal production with 
inadequate resource management efforts. Invasive species such as Prosopis juliflora are also an 
increasing threat to species extinction and habitat destruction. Many were initially introduced to 
East Africa for the stabilization of dune systems and for providing fuel wood after prolonged 
droughts in the 1970s. In many areas, however, species have hybridized to an extent that current 
varieties have lost most of their valuable woody attributes and have aggressively suppressed native 
shrub and tree vegetation. 
In 2019, SWALIM mapped more than 5,089 water points across the country (see Figure 2.3). From 
previous surveys, it estimated that only 2,200 are functional and perennial under normal non- 
drought conditions. Of these functional sources, only around 500 are improved ones, mainly deep 
boreholes, which in many other African countries commonly provide water for domestic use, 
livestock, and irrigation. In Somalia, however, groundwater sources are technically demanding to 
identify and exploit because aquifers are deep (more than half the boreholes are over 130 m deep, 
with some over 400 m) and water within aquifers is often of low quality (salty or hard) that makes it 
unsuitable as drinking water or for irrigation. 3 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
3 See http://fmt.faoso.net/imms/fmt/maps/website/227
=== PAGE 27 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 7 
 
 
 
  Figure 2.3: Somalia Strategic Water Sources, 2018 
 
 
Source: SWALIM 
 
The vegetation in Somalia is still varied, although much less so than three decades ago, as 
inappropriate land use has led to the original vegetation cover being heavily degraded, especially
=== PAGE 28 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 8 
 
 
3F 
 
in northern Somalia, and in various places it has been entirely destroyed, according to SWALIM 4 
and to Odouri et. al 2007. Current vegetation cover ranges from the forests of the Golis mountains 
in the North to the bush land in the Lag Badana ecosystem of the South. Patches of mangroves are 
found in Zeylac, Berbera and Calula on the northern coastline and in Kismayo on the southern coast. 
Expansive grasslands are found in Puntland and sparsely covered sand dunes cover a band of several 
kilometers along the coast. Somalia’s distribution of vegetation, visually illustrated in Figure SA1.1 
in Statistical Annex 1, is as follows: 
 
 
The coastal plains vegetation consists mainly of herbaceous plants. The vegetation in this region 
extends to the feet and slopes of the Golis Mountains. Close to the mountains, the predominant 
vegetation is sparse bushy Acacia, Balanites aegyptiaca, and Commiphora associations including 
Boswellia species. 
 
In the hinterland plateaus, vegetation is dominated by open shrubs and woody plants of Acacia 
bussei, Acacia etbaica, Boscia spp, Cadaba spp, and Acacia mellifera. Some herbaceous plants 
(mainly Chrysopogon aucheri and Sporobolus spp.) can also be found here. 
 
In the northern Golis mountain range the vegetation consists of evergreen trees of Junperus 
procera and Juniperus excelsa and open shrubs of Buxus hilderbrandtii, Dodonea viscose and 
Terminalia brownii, etc. In the past, the Golis Forest remained largely uninhabited and 
protected while today it is experiencing increased exploitation pressure. There are now even 
human settlements in the forest. 
 
In the central plains, vegetation varies from extensive grassland along the fixed dune areas to 
shrubby bushland with scattered trees in the west toward the Ethiopian border. Plants and 
bushes include Andropoon kelleri, Chrysopogon aucheri, Soporobolus ruspolianus, Indigofera 
ruspolii, Acacia spp., Commiphora spp., Cordeauxia edulis, Delonix elata, Terminalia orbicularis 
and Dobera glabra etc. 
 
In the southern regions (especially in the floodplain), the vegetation type is mainly low 
deciduous bushland of Acacia spp. which extends to the coastal dunes. 
 
Mangrove swamp communities are also situated at the tidal estuaries of the seasonal rivers 
towards the Indian Ocean coast and Gulf of Aden. They areas include Bushbush, Caanoole and 
Lag Badanaa. At certain points along the River Juba there are sections of riparian forest. 
 
2.2 
The Economy5 
The Somalia population can be divided into five diverse livelihood types: pastoralists, agro- 
pastoralists, fishing and coastal communities, urban population, and internally displaced persons 
(IDPs, also mostly located at the outskirts of major urban centres). According to UNFPA estimates 
from its 2014 nationwide population survey (UNFPA 2015), about 49 percent of the population still 
lives in rural areas, with 26 percent (or 3.2 million) of the total population classified as pure 
pastoralists and about 23 percent (or 2.8 million) as agro-pastoralists, depending on settled crop and 
livestock farming. Alternative estimates for direct employment by the ILO for 2014 (ILO 2015) 
suggest that about 46 percent of all employed persons in the country are working in agricultural 
activities, with about 25 percent employed in agro-pastoral activities, 9 percent in nomadic herding, 
4 percent in fishing, 7 percent in other rural activities. The rest or about 54 percent of employed 
people work in urban-based governments and the private sector (the latter includes marketing of 
livestock products), according to the ILO. 
 
 
4 Downloaded as of March 15, 2019 from http://faoswalim.org/land/land-cover for both text and Figures 2.1 and 2.2. See 
also Odouri et. al 2007 for more detailed description of main land cover types and vegetation units and their locations. 
5 This section is largely based on the analysis of economic trends presented in recent World Bank reports, covering the 
broad agricultural sector, including livestock (World Bank 2018) and the whole economy (World Bank 2019b, forthcoming).
=== PAGE 29 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 9 
 
 
 
Almost three decades after the start of the Somali civil war and the eventual collapse of the 
central government in December 1990, the country’s economic growth is still weak and mainly 
consumption-driven, enabled by large remittance and aid inflows. Between 2013 and 2017, real 
GDP has grown an average of 2.5 percent p.a., while population grew by 2.9 percent p.a. The result 
has been an annual contraction in per capita incomes of 0.4 percent during the same period– 
significantly below fragile, sub-Saharan, and low-income country averages–leaving per capita GDP at 
just over $500, making Somalia among the poorest countries in the world. 
In the past two to three decades through the mid-2010s some major structural changes stand out 
in Somalia’s economy and in its agricultural production and trade. Many pre-war sub-sectors such 
as crop production, agro-processing, other manufacturing, utilities and government services shrunk, 
increasingly buffeted by the country’s degraded and fragile natural environment, more extreme and 
frequent cycles of drought and floods related to an intensifying climate change, lack of research and 
extension service, insecurity and the consequent deterioration of flood control, irrigation, water 
facilities, and transport infrastructure in the south-central regions. Just before the most recent 2016-
2017 drought, cereal production was about half of what it was pre-war and banana production an 
even smaller fraction, with sesame the only crop experiencing growth in production and in exports. 
Other sectors, unshackled by the stifling pre-war government monopolies and regulations, 
managed to thrive through the mid-2010s, including livestock, telecommunications, construction 
and retail trade. The livestock sector’s performance however experienced a precipitous drop after 
2016. Its recent contribution to the country’s gross domestic product (GDP) remains speculative, due 
the lack of reliable and consistent estimates across subsectors for production, farmgate prices, and 
input use. The World Bank estimated for this report that its share rose significantly from its 
historical, pre-war level of 43.7 percent in 1980 and 37.2 percent in 1986-88 to 61.5 percent in 2013- 
2016. However, because of the double whammy of the 2016-2017 drought on the animal 
population, health, and productivity, and of the December 2016 livestock import ban by Saudi Arabia 
and the U.A.E., the livestock sector’s GDP share is estimated to have fallen to 43.4 percent in 2017 
(see Table 2.2). 
Construction, telecommunications and money transfer services have been the key growth sectors 
in recent years, but due to a poor enabling environment agro-processing, manufacturing and other 
services have all suffered from weak growth, even though the country has experienced rapid 
urbanization during the past two decades. The growth of agro-processing and manufacturing, in 
particular, is limited by an adverse investment environment – Somalia is ranked at the bottom of the 
WBG’s Doing Business Survey. This is largely because the government, especially in the southcentral 
regions, has not yet been able to establish strong security and public sector management 
frameworks, to ramp up its capacity and muster the needed resources for the provision of essential 
public services, and to attract large scale private investment. 
Somalia’s agro-processing sector, in particular, was negligible before the civil war and remains 
even more limited today. Before the outbreak of the civil war, almost all of the agro-processing 
industries, including for dairy products, meat, and hides and skins, were owned or heavily controlled 
by the government. Few of these were fully developed and well managed. All of them, however, 
were completely destroyed soon after the start of the civil war. Since the 1990s, a few new privately 
owned agro-processing industries have emerged, with high-potential opportunities for future 
expansion, job creation, and contributing to a higher and sustained economic growth, as the 
northern regions re-established security and self-governance institutions in the subsequent years
=== PAGE 30 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 10 
 
 
 
and the regions in the south-central part of the country have become more secure in recent years 
(see World Bank 2018, including the related online Annex listing surveyed agro-processing ventures 
in operations). Nonetheless, in all regions of the country, and despite its enormous potential, agro- 
processing, including livestock processing, was minimal even before the civil war and is even smaller 
today. In addition to the generally poor enabling environment noted above, other factors play a role 
in stifling the growth of agro-processing, including limited access to financing, high energy costs, and 
domestic consumers’ preferences for fresh products (e.g., produce, raw milk) over processed ones.

Export performance through the mid-2010s (before the recent 2016-2017 drought) has been 
impressive, with annual exports climbing every year since the late 2000s to a peak in 2015 of 
US$701 million ($644 million in 2016) and an average annual value in the early 2010s about 
fivefold higher than before the civil war. This was mostly due to strong growth of livestock exports, 
whose peak value of $546 million in 2015 was almost 10 times higher than in the late 1980s. Overall 
crop exports, on the contrary, have been in recent years a fraction of what they were before the civil 
war. As a result of such record performance of livestock exports and the collapse of banana exports,
=== PAGE 31 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 11 
 
 
 
once a close second item by value, the livestock sector accounted in the peak 2015 year for about 78 
percent of total export earnings, a much higher share than the 53 percent registered in the pre-war 
years (livestock exports are discussed further and in more detail in chapter 3).
