Qualitative studies and anecdotal evidence strongly suggest that Somalia has also become a major 
informal supplier to large parts of the Horn of Africa of cattle and charcoal and a re-exporter of 
sugar, pasta, and electronics, among other commodities subject to high import tariffs in 
neighbouring countries. This formal and informal trade expansion was facilitated by the 
combination of the growth of the telecommunication sector and of the remittance companies,  
which supported a deeper domestic, cross-border, and international market integration despite the 
highly insecure conditions in the southcentral regions of Somalia. The country has become a de facto 
duty-free zone and a critical part of a regional trade network spanning from Dubai—a critical supply 
hub for Somali businessmen—to Ethiopia and Kenya. 
There has also been a steady and large increase in food imports, including livestock products, since 
the late 1980s, reaching around US$1 billion this decade from an annual average of only US$72 
million in the late 1980s. The combination of increased domestic food demand, mostly for cereals, 
sugar, and other preparations, and for vegetables, including qat (mild narcotic leaves), driven by 
rapid population growth and urbanization and largely supported by remittances, and of the collapse 
of domestic crop production are two key factors that have led to a massive increase in food imports. 
Imports of live animals and animal products have also increased, to about $76-81 million in 2016- 
2017 from around $7 million before the civil war (see Table 2.4).

Remittances are the major source of external finance for consumption and investment in Somalia. 
Somali migrants and refugees outside Somalia doubled between 1990 and 2017 to total more than 2 
million. During 2015–2017, Somali diaspora sent home about an official US$1.3 billion per year, but 
remittances may be significantly larger when considering unrecorded flows. Remittances represent 
20 percent of GDP, about the same amount as grants to Somalia, and more than three times foreign 
direct investment (FDI). Remittances may be counter cycle as relatives and friends often send more 
during economic downturns, disasters, conflicts, or other negative shocks (World Bank 2019a). 
 
2.3 
Poverty and Vulnerability: High Overall, Including among Pastoralists7 
According to a recent study of poverty and vulnerability, Somalia is one of the poorest countries in 
Sub-Saharan Africa, and in the absence of appropriate poverty-reduction policies its vulnerability 
will impede its economic and social development. Nearly 7 of 10 Somalis live in poverty, the 6th 
highest rate in the region, behind only the Democratic Republic of Congo, Burundi and South Sudan. 
The overall incidence of poverty of 69 percent is 19 percentage points higher than the unweighted 
average of low-income Sub-Saharan African countries of 51 percent in 2017. Moreover, a significant 
group of non-poor are vulnerable to falling into poverty, as about 10 percent of the population is 
within 20 percent in terms of total daily consumption above the poverty line. Poverty incidence is 
highest in IDP settlements and in Mogadishu, but it is also high at 72.2 percent for the rural 
 
 
6 As for total exports, there is a very large and unexplained divergence between import totals based on product-by-product and country- 
by-country statistics captured by COMTRADE (partner countries) and import totals based on aggregate flows captured by the IMF (also 
based on partner country reporting). For instance, total imports, including non-food, nonagricultural imports, from Somalia were US$2,143 
million in 2014, according to the UN COMTRADE database, but US$3,482 million according to the balance-of-payments statistics in the 
latest IMF report (IMF 2016, see Table 4a on page 23). 
7 This section is based on an analysis of poverty and vulnerability in Somalia, based on the second wave of the Somali High 
Frequency Survey, implemented in late 2017/early 18. The survey achieved greater geographical and population coverage, 
including of nomads and of many households in insecure areas, compared to Wave 1 of the Somali High Frequency Survey 
(SHFS) conducted in 2016 covering Puntland, Somaliland and Banadir only and the Somaliland Household Survey (SLHS) 
carried out in 2013. Wave 2 targeted almost 6,400 households distributed among rural and urban areas all regions except 
Middle Juba.

- 13 
 
 
 
 
population (which includes agro-pastoralists, in addition to pure farmers and other rural dwellers) 
and for nomads at 71.6 percent, with both ratios being higher than for other urban areas (see Figure 
2.5). 
Figure 2.5: Poverty is high for all and highest in rural areas and IDP settlements 
 
Source: World Bank 2019a. 
 
Every nomadic household owns at least one goat, sheep, camel, donkey, cattle or chicken, and 
they tend to own more than non-nomadic households (World Bank 2019). Every nomadic 
household owns some type livestock, compared to 53 percent of the rural households, 35 percent of 
IDPs in settlements, 23 percent in other urban areas and 19 percent in Mogadishu. Chickens are the 
only type of livestock owned by a higher share of rural (including agro-pastoral) than of nomadic 
households; 18 vs. 11 percent respectively (Figure 2.6a). Among owners, nomadic households also 
own a larger number of livestock than non-nomadic households; nearly twice for cattle, sheep, goat 
and camels (Figure 2.6b). Wealth in the form of livestock represents an advantage against other 
populations living in IDP settlements, urban and rural areas. Yet, relying primarily on livelihood  
based on livestock makes them more vulnerable to climate-related shocks. 
 
Figure 2.6a: Livestock Ownership 
Figure 2.6b: Number of Livestock Owned 
 
 
 
Source: World Bank 2019a. 
 
The latest IPC tabulation by the FSNAU for the period April-June 2019 shows that 44.2 percent of 
the pastoral and agro-pastoral population is classified as either stressed (IPC-2, 29.1 percent), in 
crisis (IPC-3, 14.2 percent) or in emergency phase (IPC-4, 0.9 percent). Somaliland appears the most 
affected, with IPC-coverage rates ranging between 50-88 percent across its regions for its
=== PAGE 34 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 14 
 
 
7F  
 
pastoralists and agro-pastoralists. In Puntland’s regions, IPC-rates range between 50-70 percent. The 
impact of the current drought is less in all other regions. In Galmudug’s regions, IPC rates range from 
21 percent in the coastal cowpea agro-pastoral belt to 70 percent in the Hawd and Addun pastoral 
areas. IPC coverage rates in Hirshabelle’s regions are also relatively low, in the 10-50 percent range, 
except for a peak of 70 percent in its Hawd pastoral area. The range in South-West’s regions is 
similar, at 10-45 percent, while Jubaland’s regions appear the least affected so far, at 10-20 percent. 
Though information on households’ herd numbers and composition over time remains weak and 
preliminary, recent reports indicate that in many areas pastoralists have become poorer, with 
more households shifting from middle to poor wealth groups and from poor to very poor wealth 
groups (Bishop, et al., 2008; World Bank 2019). Recurrent drought episodes, often combined with 
insecurity, have led to pastoralists losing large numbers of animals and many migrating to (peri) 
urban areas. For pastoralists, loss of animals is closely related to destitution and marginalization 
from community life. 
According to the most recent household survey of poverty and vulnerability in Somalia, access to 
services is limited particularly for rural residents, IDPs in settlements, and nomads (World Bank 
2019). Improved water & sanitation is critical for health, school performance, and productivity, but 
only 5 of 10 households have access to improved sanitation, and 8 of 10 to improved water sources. 
Only 5 of 10 households have electricity. Access to services is somewhat higher in urban areas. Poor 
households are less likely to have access to improved sanitation and electricity. Markets and health 
clinics are far—more than 30 minutes walking distance—for 34 to 40 percent of Somali households 
and for most nomads. Over-age school enrollment is common, with stark geographical and gender 
disparities, and with nomads and girls facing the biggest challenges. Improving service provision– 
especially education–is crucial to improve human capital and reduce inequality that 
disproportionately affects girls/women, IDPs, and rural and nomadic households. 
Somali households are vulnerable to shocks such as natural disasters and epidemics, as well as to 
household-level shocks such as injury, death, or unemployment. Shocks contribute to extreme 
poverty and vulnerability, constraining economic opportunities and livelihoods, damaging assets, 
and limiting access to farms, fishing, and pastoralist routes. The persistent cycle of shocks increases 
Somalis’ vulnerability to future shocks as there is limited public and private insurance. The threat of 
climate change also increases the risk of displacement by reducing the viability of livelihoods that 
support rural (and urban) populations in Somalia. 
Jobs play an important role in fragile environments, but Somalia suffers from acute 
unemployment and underemployment. Unemployment rates are considerably higher among 
women and Somalis under 30 years of age, who represent 70 percent of the population. Jobs not 
only contribute directly to poverty reduction, productivity, and economic growth but also facilitate 
social cohesion and reduce the risk of violence. However, according to the International Labour 
Organization (ILO), only 54 percent of the active population (ages 15 to 64) in Somalia is 
unemployed. The unemployment rate among youth ages 14 to 29 years is 67 percent—among the 
highest rates in the world (UNDP 2015). Women fare worse (74 percent) compared to men (61 
percent) 8. Due to high fertility rates (6.2 births per woman in 2010-2015), the youth population is 
 
 
 
8 Gender inequality is alarmingly high in Somalia at 0.776 out of a value of 1 (complete inequality), with Somalia at the 
fourth lowest position globally on the Gender Inequality Index, if internationally comparable data were available. Women 
suffer severe exclusion and inequality in all dimensions of the index—health, employment, and labor market participation 
(UNDP 2015).
=== PAGE 35 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 15 
 
 
 
arguably more vulnerable than any other to extremism and criminal behavior and the numbers will 
continue to swell. 
Job-creation, especially for youth, is fundamental to sustainable poverty reduction, vulnerability 
mitigation, and conflict avoidance. Somalia has a large youth bulge, so youth must be able to find 
jobs to contribute to economic growth. Policies to encourage business and entrepreneurship to 
create jobs are needed to avoid idle youth from resorting to conflict. Furthermore, enhancing access 
to domestic markets can increase inclusivity, spur economic activity, and accelerate poverty 
reduction. 
Investment in resilience is also needed to prevent livelihood loss for vulnerable rural households, 
especially due to likely future droughts. A consumption shock of the same magnitude as the 
2016/17 drought is estimated to increase rural poverty from 65 to 76 percent. Investing in resilience 
and rural market access would help these households avoid livelihood loss. Measures may include 
agricultural insurance, enabling households to diversify income, and improving access to roads and 
clean water.
=== PAGE 36 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 16 
 
 
 
3.0 LIVESTOCK SECTOR: STRUCTURE AND PERFORMANCE 
 
3.1 The Natural Environment9 
Severe environmental degradation of the country’s fragile natural environment, including of its 
rangelands and forested areas, and intensifying climate change, with more extreme and frequent 
cycles of floods and drought (eight major droughts since the early 1980s, see Fig. 3.1), have 
affected negatively in the last four decades the performance of all agricultural sectors, including 
the livestock sector. In addition to crop failures in agro-pastoral areas, the impact of recurrent 
droughts includes decreased water availability, reduced pasture availability, accelerated land 
degradation, impaired health, resilience and productivity of livestock, lower livestock prices and 
household incomes, and pastoral migration in search of pasture and water, which can increase and 
exacerbate conflicts over scare resources. 
The cumulative impact of various failed rainy seasons since 2016 hit pastoralists, farmers and the 
internally displaced particularly hard and led to a very serious humanitarian crisis whose human, 
social, and economic effects still linger to this day in a few areas of the country. After coming back 
from the brink of famine in mid-2017, food security and nutrition (and livestock welfare and 
numbers) have been on a steady path to recovery. However, they remain fragile, with weak and late 
rains so far through May 2019. Current humanitarian needs in early 2019 are greater than the pre- 
crisis levels of three years ago, with acute malnutrition still persisting in many areas and the real 
prospect of another drought-related crisis through 2019. Humanitarian action has prevented worse 
outcomes, but sustained assistance for emergency nutrition needs and resilience-building efforts 
remain critical in preventing further deterioration (FAO, FSNAU, FEWS-NET 2019). 
In the recent 2016-2017 drought, massive livestock and other damages and losses were 
experienced following three consecutive poor seasons of erratic and below average rainfall 
(Somalia 2018). Insufficient rain and water availability towards the end of 2016 severely reduced 
water and pasture availability for livestock. The drought stretched the government’s capacity to 
handle the devastating effects of the drought on pastoralists. This also increased the risk and 
vulnerability of livestock-dependent households, with the poor losing up to 60 percent of their total 
stock in some of the most severely affected areas. Somalia suffered over US$1.6 billion in losses and 
damages in the livestock sector – around 50 percent of the total drought damage and losses – during 
the drought period. In addition, an estimated USD 400 million revenue loss from live animal exports 
in 2017 from the import ban imposed by Saudi Arabia and the UAE brought the overall damage and 
loss for the sector to over US$2 billion. Following the drought, the sector and the country faced 
numerous challenges including but not limited to: absence of fodder reserves and alternatives, the 
absence of allocated budget by Government to the sector, and weak government institutional 
capacity to cope with such large-scale drought impact. The overall effect of recurring severe 
droughts, moreover, is on limiting regeneration of pasture and water availability for both animals 
and plant growth. Somalia therefore remains one of the most drought vulnerable country in the 
Horn of Africa, even more so because feed and forage production and supply chains are not well- 
developed and pre-war flood-control and irrigation infrastructure are in disrepair. 
 
 
 
 
 
9 This section is largely based on section 4.2 of a recent World Bank-FAO joint report analyzing the impact of climate 
change on the broad agricultural sector, including livestock (World Bank 2018).

3.2 
Livestock Production Systems 
Livestock remains the traditional repository of household wealth in Somalia and is an important 
source of livelihood and resilience for a large part of the country’s population. In fact, self- 
consumption of livestock (meat) and livestock products (milk and ghee) as well as their market sales 
is critical for the food security of pastoral and agro-pastoral households, with market sales important 
also for the rest of the Somali population. While well adapted to the harsh geographical and climatic 
environment, Somali sheep and goats (shoats), camels and cattle are characterized by poor weights 
and milk yields, largely as a result of young animals with larger frames and body weights being 
selected for slaughter or live export rather than breeding. 
The main production systems in Somalia are nomadic pastoralism and agro-pastoralism (settled 
mixed farming), with little or no integration with crops in the first system, which relies heavily on 
seasonal migration in search of pasture (see Figure 3.2). There is also a smaller peri-urban dairy 
farming system, which mainly relies on purchased supplements and grain and crop by–products as 
fodder. Commercial livestock-rearing operations with dairy animals (mostly camels) have been on 
the rise in peri-urban settings, ranging from small to very large (3,500 animals for a camel dairy farm 
in Benadir), supplying nearby urban markets with fresh raw milk. 
 
Pure pastoralists, at 3,186,965 or about 26 percent of the total population in 2014 (UNFPA 
2014), are mostly nomadic, poor, and are found throughout all rural areas of Somalia, but 
predominantly in the arid open rangelands of northern and central Somalia (tending to their 
sheep and goats, cattle, and camels). They also dominate along the Ethiopian and Kenyan 
borders in southern Somalia (tending in addition also to cattle), using little or no external inputs, 
including feed and health services. This group suffers from a poverty incidence estimated for 
2018 at 71.6 percent (World Bank 2019). This predominant production system is characterized 
by low levels of inputs and productivity and relies largely on (i) natural pastures on the country’s 
vast rangelands, which are covered by grass, herbs, shrubs and sparse trees and (ii) palatable
=== PAGE 38 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 18 
 
 
 
fresh crop residues for fodder. This is coupled with high mobility by both pastoralists and agro- 
pastoralists in search of forage.
=== PAGE 39 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 19 
 
 
 
Figure 3.2: Seasonal Migration of Livestock 
 
Source: FSNAU 2019 
 
Agro-pastoralists, at 2,806,787 or about 22.8 percent of the population in 2014 (UNFPA 2014), 
depend on both settled crop production and livestock rearing, and live mostly in the inter- 
riverine regions of Bay, Bakool, western Hiran, and eastern Gedo in south-central Somalia. They 
also predominate in certain areas of southwestern and northwestern regions with underground 
water and high annual precipitation. A small proportion are riverine crop growers, some of
=== PAGE 40 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 20 
 
 
9F  
 
whom also keep animals on their farm, but most of whom keep no livestock. The poverty 
incidence among agro-pastoralist is also believed to be high, around the 72.3 percent level which 
has been estimated by the World Bank (2019) for rural households (to include also pure farmers 
and other rural dwellers). 
 
3.3 
Livestock Population, Distribution and Composition 
Somali livestock are of a broad range of species and types with some breeds that are well adapted 
to a nomadic way of life, limited feed resources and intermittent water supply. Because Somali 
people live in several countries other than geographical Somalia, some of their traditional livestock 
breeds are also found in Djibouti, Ethiopia and Kenya. See Main Annex 1 for more details about 
Somalia’s livestock genetic resources. 
The last livestock census in Somalia was undertaken in 1975. Until the late 1980s, livestock 
population figures showing a total of about 40 million were estimated by government agencies from 
reliable surveys supported by externally funded nationwide projects and can be found in both the 
official national accounts (Somalia 1989) and in FAOSTAT. Since then, however, animal population 
estimates have been largely based on projections and extrapolations by external agencies with very 
limited information and access to many parts of the country. One such point estimate suggested a 
livestock population of 39 million in 1998 (see FAO, World Bank and EU 2004). All sources 
nonetheless concord that there was a sharp decline in the 1990s, largely because of the civil war, 
followed by a solid recovery only interrupted by a sharp decrease in small stock in 2007-2008. 
More recent baseline livestock estimates for 2014 imply a remarkable resilience by the sector until 
the latest severe drought that affected during 2016 and 2017 the entire country, though at 
different times and with different intensities. 10 As illustrated in Figure 3.3, these baseline estimates 
point to a sharply increased animal population in 2014 (baseline) compared to historical time series 
estimates from FAOSTAT as well as to other point estimates from various source: 52.9 million total 
heads in 2014, including 28.7 million goats, 13.6 million sheep, 6.6 million camels, and 3.9 million 
heads of cattle (Somalia 2018; also in World Bank-FAO 2018, section 5.7, pp 56-58). See also Main 
Annex 2 for an overview of available livestock statistics and Table SA2.1, Statistical Annex 2 for a 
regional breakdown of the baseline animal population estimates for 2014. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
10 These estimates were originally proposed by the FSNAU, applying historical household profiles which included livestock holdings ( 
FSNAU-FEWS 2016) in many districts of Somalia to district-disaggregated official human population estimates for 2014. They were 
discussed with and agreed to by federal and state ministry officials and FAO-Somalia and World Bank experts at the DINA workshop held in 
October 2017 in Mogadishu, and even though based on heroic assumptions and a weak methodology, they remain the best available 
baseline estimates until a reliable comprehensive animal population census or survey will be conducted. For goats these estimates were 
much larger and for camels and cattle somewhat smaller than previous estimates by other sources, and their regional distribution was also 
different. The most recent livestock figures estimated independently in 2012 by the Somaliland government and including the northcentral 
regions (whose control is in dispute with Puntland) are roughly consistent with the regional FSNAU-MoLFR estimates shown in Table 
SA2.1, Statistical Annex 2. They show a steady increase in all types of livestock between 1998 and 2009, with almost 90 percent of its 
livestock being shoats (World Bank-FAO 2018).

FSNAU-MoLFR estimates. The animal population, however, is estimated to have recovered to  
almost 56 million heads in 2018, after more normal rainfall patterns resumed during the second half 
of 2017 (see Table 3.1). Statistical Annex Tables SA.2.2 through 2.4 show the animal population 
estimates by the FSNAU-MoLFR for 2015, 2016 and 2017, which factor in higher drought-related 
mortality and lower natality rates compared to normal years (see also Somalia 2018). In addition, 
they also take into account the impact of the import ban imposed by Saudi Arabia and the UAE in 
December 2016, implying a lower offtake for export since then. 11

Even after considering the animal mortality from the recent drought, Somalia remains the country 
with the largest camel population in the world, more than twice the camel population of Kenya 
and Ethiopia combined (Mahmoud 2013 and, for 2015, FAOSTAT). Somalia also ranked 6th in the 
world and 1st in Africa for goat population in 2015, if the 31.8 million estimated by the MoLFR and 
FSNAU (as opposed to the roughly only one third recorded by FAOSTAT) are compared to those by 
FAOSTAT for all other countries. Livestock herd sizes and composition vary across Somali 
production systems and regions, as illustrated in Table 3.1. Camels are found in all agro-climatic 
zones of the country. Cattle are mostly concentrated in the southern regions, particularly in the Juba 
and Shabelle river valleys. Large numbers of sheep are found in the northern and central regions, 
whereas goats are predominantly found in the northern regions, especially in Puntland, and in the 
southern regions. Average herd sizes are large to very large in the nomadic pastoral system and 
medium size in the mainly transhumant agro-pastoral system. In contrast, mixed crop/livestock 
farming households may have only a few animals. Different species are generally held together, to 
make the most efficient use of range vegetation, because camels and goats are primarily browsers 
while cattle and sheep are largely grazers. Combining different types of livestock also provides 
pastoral groups with a wide array of animal products. Herd diversification is an important risk 
management strategy for protecting against diseases, drought, and lack of drinking water or forage 
(Dahl 1981). 
 
3.4 
Health and Disease12 
Animal diseases are regularly reported in the country, with the most significant being of 
transboundary nature. Among the most notable are Peste des Petits Ruminants (PPR),12F13 
Contagious Caprine Pleuropneumonia (CCPP), Contagious Bovine Pleuropneumonia (CBPP) occurring 
mostly in the southern areas, Foot and Mouth Disease (FMD), Sheep and Goat Pox (SGP), Camel pox, 
and Lumpy Skin Disease (LSD). Other commonly found diseases are Anthrax, Blackleg, Brucellosis, 
Hemorrhagic Septicemia, Trypanosomiasis, Salmonellosis, Clostridium, and ecto- and endoparasites 
all of which affect both production and reproduction of animals kept by pastoralists and 
 
 
12 This section is mainly based on FAO et al. 2004 and World Bank-FAO 2018, and updated by FAO-Somalia. 
13 During vaccination campaigns spearheaded by the FAO since 2012, an overall individual animal PPR sero-prevalence of 62 percent has 
been observed, with a low of 39 percent in the northwestern region and a high of 74 percent in southern Somalia, much higher than the 
5.5-37.6 percent range found in 2001 baseline surveys. So far, there have been no studies to determine PPR  mortality and morbidity  
rates. (Njue 2018).
=== PAGE 43 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 23 
 
 
14F 
 
agropastoralists as well as of the wildlife population.14 Fowl pox is also commonly observed disease 
affecting poultry. Due to the aggregation of a large number of livestock in common grazing areas, 
watering points, and markets and along trading routes, and to shared grazing on the rangelands with 
wildlife, the likelihood of disease transmission remains high. Climate variability also has had a 
negative impact on livestock and human health through vector-borne diseases. Higher average 
temperatures have supported the expansion of vector populations into cooler areas at higher 
altitude (Thornton et al. 2007). 
Rift Valley Fever (RVF) remains at the forefront of many of the problems that the livestock export 
trade has suffered after its presence was first suspected in 1998. 15 Outbreaks in East Africa have 
occurred periodically at intervals ranging from 2 to more than 30 years. The Somali areas bordering 
with Kenya and the north eastern region of Kenya went through a massive epizootic of RVF in 
1997/98 facilitated by extensive flooding occurring in that region. RVF is otherwise enzootic 
throughout Sub–Saharan Africa and Madagascar and reached the Arabian Peninsula in 2000. 
Permanent irrigation in the western coastal zones of Yemen and Saudi Arabia will contribute to the 
risk of the disease becoming endemic in these areas (FAO et al. 2004). The FAO, however, believes 
that RVF is currently not endemic in Somalia (unlike northern Kenya) and thus has not conducted 
mass vaccinations campaigns against this disease, leaving quarantine stations to administer vaccines 
against RVF to the animals in their care depending on the requirements of the importing country. 
The presence of transboundary animal diseases (TADs), the absence or weakness in available 
veterinary services, and the constant mobility of animals within the country and across the Horn  
of Africa region pose a serious risk of disease transmission and subsequent trade bans by 
importing countries. Recognizing the regional dimension of addressing the animal health challenge 
in each of IGAD member states, a regional animal health strategy for 2017-2022 was prepared with 
funding by the World Bank (ICPALD 2017). Its strategic pillars are: (i) strengthening national 
veterinary institutions; (ii) strengthening disease surveillance and reporting systems and regional 
sharing of data; (iii) identify priority disease control measures; (iv) enhancing regional and national 
emergency preparedness; and (v) instituting sustainable mechanisms for regular and timely 
coordination between member states. 
In May 2019, furthermore, Somalia and Ethiopia agreed on a Memorandum of Understanding 
(MOU), facilitated by IGAD/ICPALD for cross-border cooperation and coordination on animal 
health and sanitary measures. The areas of cooperation, to be backed by commitment of financial, 
staffing and physical resources, that are identified in the MOU include: (i) control of trans-boundary 
animal diseases; (ii) mapping of livestock routes, markets and natural resources (water and pasture), 
including control of invasive weeds; (iii) enforcement of regulations on veterinary drugs and vaccines 
use; (iv) facilitation of livestock trade through quality control and certification; (v) access to livestock- 
related infrastructure including diagnostic laboratories, cold chains, holding grounds and 
quarantines; (vi) joint promotion of livestock identification and traceability; (vii) joint capacity 
building of stakeholders; (viii) promotion of cross-border networking among stakeholders. 
 
 
 
 
14 For an historical perspective and more details about the major diseases affecting Somali livestock, see pp. 13-14 of the 2004 “Towards a 
Livestock Sector Strategy - final report” (FAO et al. 2004). 
15 RVF is a viral disease that is maintained and transmitted by mosquitoes whose eggs are resistant to desiccation. The disease persists at 
low levels and can remain undetected until favorable climatic conditions, including higher rainfall with flooding, create a breeding 
explosion in its vector, which is the Aedes (Neomelaniconium) group of mosquitoes, followed by amplification of the virus via other species 
of mosquito.

15F 
 
In the absence of a strong public sector to take the lead in protecting animal health, many critical 
services are provided by the private sector, often but not always with funding from development 
partners. These services include mass vaccination, vector control and supportive treatment 
campaigns, training, and institutional capacity development. A central role in delivering animal 
health services is played by three private, accredited livestock professional associations (LPAs) in 
south central Somalia and two in Puntland and Somaliland. The LPAs are regularly mobilized to 
deliver targeted services such as vaccinations, treatments, and disease investigation and disease 
detection, generally with external funding (see Table 3.2). They also manage the veterinarian drug 
stores in the regions and, with support of the FAO, train community-based animal health workers 
(CAHWs) in their remote local communities on reporting and treating basic livestock diseases, 
veterinary drug administration, basic surgical procedures and reporting to regional veterinary offices 
on suspected TAD cases.

Substantial investments have been made in recent years by international development agencies 
such as the FAO to improve animal health and welfare and also some by foreign private investors 
in quarantine stations to meet the requirements of the importing countries. However, no 
comprehensive evaluation or monitoring of the Performance of Veterinary Services (PVS), according 
to the OIE-PVS Pathway tool, has been done. The FAO, through the accredited LPAs and under the 
supervision, coordination and monitoring of MoLFR, has carried out mass vaccination, treatment and 
vector control campaigns covering in the seven years to end-2018 more than 133 million shoats (see 
Table 3.2) and about 4.3 million households, at a total cost of about US$37 million. 16 Other 
development partners have also conducted other vaccination campaigns, albeit on a smaller scale, 
and various treatment programs. 
Seven quarantine stations for the export of live animals have also been built, each with their own 
isolation areas for sick animals: three in Berbera, Somaliland, with a combined maximum capacity 
 
 
16 From year to year, different animals are vaccinated, including young animals (usually less than three months of age) that were not 
vaccinated during the previous round.
=== PAGE 45 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 25 
 
 
16F 
 
to hold up to 230,400 animals at any one time (211,000 for shoats and 19.400 for camels), and two 
others in Bosaso, Puntland, that can hold more than 367,000 heads of livestock. One of the 
quarantine stations in Berbera and one in Bosaso are owned by a Saudi company (SEIVQMC), while 
the others are owned by Somali business people. A new, locally owned private quarantine facility 
was established in Jazeera, near Mogadishu, in 2011, with a holding capacity of 250,000 animals; and 
another was opened by a private company in Kismayo in 2017, whose capacity is 13,500 animals, 
and handling so far mostly cattle. All these facilities are equipped with laboratories to perform 
various tests before health certification, including for PPR and brucellosis (also known as undulant 
fever, a highly contagious disease), according to the specific requirements of the importing 
countries. 
Through the mid-2010s, such investment in a system of vaccinations campaigns, health pre- 
inspections, quarantine stations, and livestock health certifications at the tail end of the market 
chain had dramatically enhanced the confidence of importers in the safety and quality of livestock 
from Somalia and had led to the lifting of the livestock import ban by the Saudi government and to 
reductions in livestock handling time and overall marketing costs (Godiah et al. 2015; Khadijah and 
Kabue 2012). The northern quarantine stations have on site federally-accredited but state 
government-employed and privately paid veterinarians, who issue health certificates of compliance 
with importing country requirements. Animals are also clinically inspected in some  regional  
markets, such as in Galkayo, Puntland, and Belet Weyn and Jowhar, Hirshabelle, before healthy 
animals are trucked to the quarantine stations near the export ports. 17 
This system, however, was unable to prevent the shipment to Saudi Arabia from Mogadishu Al- 
Jazeera quarantine of animals that tested positive in December 2016 to RVF. The December 2016 
RVF incident led Saudi Arabia and the UAE to re-impose an import ban on live animals from all 
Somali ports. Based on available evidence, the FAO and the MoLFR believe the animals had been 
shipped off to sea from the Jazeera quarantine station (near Mogadishu) by unscrupulous traders 
with the complicity of inexperienced government and private veterinarians. The animals were not 
held under quarantine neither to the end of the two-week incubation period nor to the end of the 
required holding period following the administration of vaccines against RVF, resulting in a false- 
positive result at the port of entry. Another shipment of 27,000 shoats from Bosaso under the 
sacrificial animal exemption was rejected by Saudi Arabia in the months preceding the 2018 Haj, also 
following positive tests to RVF antibodies at destination. This second incident derailed until recently 
the provisional agreements on lifting the import ban with the Ministry of Water, Environment and 
Agriculture of the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia (KSA), which included a request by MoLFR to the World 
Organization for Animal Health (OIE) to evaluate the standards and practices of all quarantine 
stations in Somalia, including their laboratories. As part of renewed negotiations, it was agreed in 
February 2019 that MoLFR shall produce a federally-mandated uniform health certificate form that 
will accompany all animals exported from all quarantine stations, including those in Berbera, 
Somaliland. 
At stake are three critical issues, related to weak capacity, conflicts of interest, and ineffective 
controls that results in poor credibility among key importing countries of the system at Somali 
ports for testing for RVF and other TADs and for preventing tainted shipments. Weak capacity is 
 
 
17 Before September 2009, exports from Somaliland to Saudi Arabia transited through the livestock quarantine facilities at the port of 
Djibouti. This not only increased the marketing costs for Somaliland traders, but also constrained export volumes to the handling capacity 
of the facilities at the port of Djibouti. While the findings by Khadijah and Kabue (2012) cover the case of Somaliland, the same applies to 
exports from Puntland.
=== PAGE 46 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 26 
 
 
 
exemplified by the poor conditions of the public laboratories and the lack of accreditation and 
supervision of the private laboratories at the quarantine stations. Another major issue affecting 
credibility is that health certificates are issued by privately paid veterinary officers, a practice that 
gives rise to conflicts of interest. Although government-accredited and formally government- 
employed, these veterinarians are not independent from the private companies owning and 
managing the quarantine stations and paying their salaries (in addition to government-mandated 
fees). The third issue relates to the general institutional weaknesses affecting export controls by the 
authorities, coupled with the impatience among Somali exporters regarding the long waiting period 
of three weeks in the quarantine stations and reluctance to shoulder the associated livestock 
maintenance costs. All these factors have led and can still lead to rushed shipments too soon after 
vaccinations against RVF and FMD, in contravention of importing country requirements. 
Other weaknesses of the current animal health system are that all vaccination campaigns are 
donor driven and paid; meaning vaccinations only take place when external funds are available. 
Also, existing vaccination campaigns only target TADs. For other important diseases, such as  
anthrax, haemorrhagic septicaemia, blackleg, etc, vaccines are not widely available in the country 
and are imported directly, with no assurance of their quality and certifications, by the private sector 
for use mainly at the quarantine stations. There have also been challenges related to the timing of 
the administration of vaccines, specifically for live vaccines, such as for RVF, and for which it is 
difficult to distinguish between a recently vaccinated animal and an infected animal. The 
unregulated nature of the animal health care system in Somalia makes it difficult to regulate 
vaccinations, for example by ensuring that animals are not vaccinated too close to the point of 
export, and to inform receiving countries of the vaccination campaigns and notify them that tests 
might show false positive results. 
Widespread availability of low-quality veterinary drugs is another challenge. The bulk of veterinary 
drug trade is undertaken by the private sector outside the purview of the Department of Veterinary 
Services in the MoLFR. Despite the recent adoption of the Somali Veterinary Code, there still is no 
veterinary drug quality assurance program and, in any case, enforcement of licensing procedures 
and monitoring of veterinary drug use would be difficult due to current capacity constraints. Most 
livestock owners often purchase veterinary drugs solely according to price, rather than from 
veterinarians, CAHWs, or agro-vet stores. They also lack awareness on their proper use, leading to 
under-dosage, over-dosage and residues in animal products meat and milk. This uncontrolled use 
poses a high risk of bringing about also in Somalia antimicrobial resistance (AMR), which has become 
a global concern around the world, to current antibiotic, antifungal, antiprotozoal, and antiviral 
drugs. With a largely unregulated market, the use of expired, substandard, and fake drugs cannot be 
ruled out.
=== PAGE 47 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 27 
 
 
 
Decades of donor-funded animal health projects/interventions, including distribution in the 
northern regions of free veterinary drugs, has also eroded livestock owners’ willingness to pay for 
good-quality drugs, making the development of a private sector-driven drug supply system very 
challenging. Moreover, many veterinary workers and professionals across Somalia are aging, with 
few youthful ones having been trained in the last decades. As discussed in Chapter 4, IGAD Sheikh 
Technical Veterinary School in Sheikh, Somaliland and a few private universities in Somalia offer 
training programs in animal health and veterinary science, but their output quality and graduation 
numbers still fall far short of the needs of the country’s largest economic and export sector. Due to 
this lack of domestic capacity, many of the livestock exporting companies use veterinarians from 
outside Somalia, who are not registered and accredited by MoLFR and whose qualifications and 
experience on animal inspection and disease investigations are unknown to either federal or state 
authorities. 
 
3.5 
Feed Resources and Animal Nutrition 
There is a high and growing demand for quality fodder along trading routes, in market centers,  
and at terminal ports, even though the predominant pastoral production system still relies on 
natural pastures on the country’s vast rangelands. However, little or no commercial fodder is 
currently available (see Box 3.1), and storage practices of hay production from native grasses is so 
poor that its nutritive value drops rapidly equivalent to that of cereal straws soon after grain harvest. 
In the agro-pastoral and settled mixed farming systems there is medium to high integration with 
crops and even very high integration in the flood plain areas where fodder can be grown. In addition, 
fodder is produced seasonally in some of the rain fed areas with some of the rangeland areas in 
northern Somalia being increasingly enclosed illegally, against the traditional practices, in order to 
grow fodder. Fodder is also grown under irrigation along the riverine areas using mechanical pumps 
and within flooded basins in the plains. There is a proliferation of small-scale irrigation in peri–urban, 
coastal areas and dry river beds areas based on groundwater extraction from boreholes as well as in 
dry river beds using mechanical and solar-powered pumps, where cut-and-carry livestock feeding is 
being practiced. A study undertaken in the border area of Ethiopia, Kenya and Somalia (Nyangaga,  
et al., 2009) found that agro‐pastoralists use part of their crops, mostly residues, to feed their 
livestock. They then sell the rest to peri‐urban livestock keepers in and around towns as well as 
pastoral communities living further away from the rivers when they move closer to their farms 
during the dry season. 
 
Because of the wide catchment area, most animals destined for export are trucked large distances 
from the main livestock markets to the Somali export ports, with rest stops along major trekking 
routes, generating a high and growing market demand for fodder at such rest stops, in market 
centers and at terminal ports. To meet the substantial water and fodder requirements for this trade 
and also to overcome natural feed shortages in seasonal droughts, the biomass currently used as 
fodder are mostly crop residues such as stalks that is mainly made up of maize and sorghum straws, 
that are generally of low nutritional value. Others less commonly marketed fodder sources because 
of their higher prices are green maize stovers, grass hay, pulse and cereal milling by-products 
(brans), and oilseed cakes, except during severe droughts when the very survival of the animals is at 
stake. Fish waste is another valuable by-product but still under-exploited as a source of feed, 
particularly suited to the poultry sector. Successful pilots have also been undertaken, for example by 
FAO, to produce feed from the pods of the invasive Prosopis Juliflora (mesquite or, in Somali, 
garaanwa).
=== PAGE 48 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 28 
 
 
CSET is a private seed company established in October 2018 in Mogadishu, focusing on the production, 
processing and sale of various kinds of quality seeds for animal fodder and human consumption, and aiming to 
become a leading company in the agro-processing subsector. Such a business venture is a promising sign that 
the private sector is willing to step in to meet the growing demand for commercial quality feed and fodder, 
spurred by the recurring severe droughts experienced during the most recent decade. 
The company offers five different varieties of sorghum seeds specifically intended for providing two harvests 
(50-60 days each) per growing season and maximizing the yield from the green stalks, rather than maximizing 
the yield of the sorghum head for human consumption that requires a full season to maturity (110-120 days). 
For instance, the height of their Deeqsom (IRAT204) variety is 1.6 meters, with a protein content of 24 percent, 
a potential yield of 1.25 kg/ha and is medium-resistant to diseases and insects, including the Fall Army Worm. 
Its first harvest, fully sold to livestock owners, was in June 2019 from their demonstration farm under irrigation 
in the Lower Shabelle region and the company is planning another demonstration farm for the next season 
under rainfed conditions in the Bay region. 
Source: interview with CSNET management in June 2019 and company marketing brochure. 
 
 
 
Box 3.1: Promising Commercial Venture in Seed Production for Animal Fodder 
 
The demand for fodder and consequently also its prices peak when livestock exports soar before 
and during the main Islamic celebrations and during the two regular dry seasons that Somalia 
experiences every year. Demand is also high at all times near towns where the number of urban 
consumers of milk and meat has been increasing, generating greater demand for fodder from dairy 
and other animal farms in peri-urban areas. Nomadic pastoralists have also increasingly demanded 
fodder as the rangeland pasture is declining, more so at times of prolonged severe droughts. Prices 
of fodder are highest during the dry season when pasture is scarce and fodder production is 
generally low. A 10-ton truckload of dry grasses (hay) harvested from areas around Burao in 
Somaliland sells for as low as US$50 in the wet season but can go up to US$350 in the dry season. 
Around Dollow in the Gedo Region, when large populations of pastoralists start migrating with their 
animals to the riverine areas due to availability of crop residues produced under irrigation, fodder 
prices increase from less than US$0.20 around May/June per bunch (equivalent to a single handful 
or three stalks of fresh Sudan grass or maize) to US$0.50 two to three months later, when drought 
peaks (FAO, 2017). 
Despite the higher cost which allow many poor pastoralists to purchase only the small amounts 
necessary to ensure the survival of their animals until the next rains, fodder availability offers a 
good coping strategy during dry season and even prolonged droughts. The increased demand for 
and availability of quality fodder in the riverine areas around Dollow, in fact, provides much needed 
support for breeding and lactating animals, for increased milk yields and for consumption by the 
local communities, as well as an opportunity for women to engage in the marketing of fodder as an 
alternative source of income. 
 
3.6 
Marketing of Live Animals 
Similar to livestock production, Somalia’s domestic livestock marketing and export trade is still 
mostly guided by traditional institutions and practices, including kinship and ethnic groupings, 
identity, and religious practices, which serve as alternatives to formal contracting. Livestock 
marketing supplies animals for slaughter, primarily for domestic meat consumption (exports of
=== PAGE 49 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 29 
 
 
17F
18F 
 
frozen beef carcasses remain negligible), and for live animal exports, mostly to Gulf  countries. In 
fact, the Somali livestock sector sets itself apart by the degree to which its production systems are 
geared to supply a thriving export market. Livestock is exported year-round, but with a considerable 
peak in exports during the seasonal Haj, with Saudi Arabia being the main importer of live animals, 
responsible for almost 65 percent of all livestock exports from Somalia in 2015, followed by other 
Gulf countries. 
 
The accurateness and reliability of available offtake estimates are questionable but available 
figures appear to be in line with those for pastoral and agro-pastoral production systems 
elsewhere in sub-Saharan Africa. 18 Estimates quoted in previous sector strategy and needs 
assessment documents for Somalia show annual offtake percentages of 27.3% for sheep, 23.3% for 
goats, 11.3% for cattle, and 1.6% for camels (in FAO et al. 2004, also in World Bank 2007). 19 A more 
recent study using 2013 data obtained from various federal and state livestock and planning 
ministries presented the following offtake rates, as shown in Table 3.3 (ICPALD, 2015), which shows 
a large variation for cattle and camels between the northern and southern regions of the country.

In the absence of government programs and institutions that protect animal health, many critical 
services (e.g., extension, mass vaccinations, quarantine stations, and health certifications) have 
been provided by the private sector and by local veterinary associations funded  from 
development partners. These interventions and services supported a strong export performance for 
live animals through 2016, but they remain insufficient to address the serious risk of various animal 
diseases in the country. Such efforts led to large reductions in handling time and marketing costs, 
and through 2016 dramatically enhanced the confidence of importers in the safety of Somali 
livestock through 2016. In fact, the substantial public and private investment in vaccination 
campaigns, quality improvements, and in quarantine stations have supported an impressive growth 
in live animal exports through the mid-2010s, with export volumes and values in 2015-2016 
considerably higher than before the

civil war and providing about three-quarters of total exports value, as shown in Table 2.3 (World

Saudi Arabia and the UAE in December 2016, with the only exception of 1.2 million sacrificial shoats

2.3 and 3.3 and Figure 3.3. Furthermore, for a visual representation of the main features of the 
sector’s export performance see Figure 3.2. 
On the Arabian Peninsula, there is a strong preference for Somali livestock and meat, both 
because of similar and familiar halal handling requirements and because of similar genetic 
characteristics between Somali and Gulf region’s shoats. This latter factor ensures similar body 
shapes, and taste and cooking properties of the meat, unlike with animals from Australia, New 
Zealand, Europe, and the Americas (FAO 2013). While sales of Somali cattle dominate many of the 
larger and smaller markets in the Horn of Africa, particularly the southward routes and markets on 
the border of Kenya and Ethiopia, its small stock, including a large share originating from Ethiopia, 
dominates northward trading routes to the Gulf States, with a noteworthy peak during the Haji 
season. 
 
  Figure 3.4 -  Live Animal Exports and Meat Value Chain in Somalia 
 
 
 
 
Recorded exports in 2017 and 2018 are dramatically lower than at their peak in the mid-2010s, as 
shown in Figure 3.5 and Table 3.4, and concentrated on a few destination markets. They are 
currently limited mainly to Oman throughout the year, with 1.2 million sacrificial animals shipped to 
Saudi Arabia from the northern ports during the Haji period, and to small occasional shipments of 
camels to Egypt. This is due both to the lingering impact of the latest severe drought in 2016-2017 
and the import ban on the ground of suspected RVF imposed by Saudi Arabia and UAE in December 
2016. Efforts to lift this ban have proven ineffective so far, only partly because of another shipment 
to Saudi Arabia during 2018 was found to include animals affected by positive RVF antibodies, but 
mainly because of a chronic failure to meet international standards and requirements of importing
=== PAGE 51 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 31 
 
 
 
countries and related lack of credibility of the official veterinary certifications issued by the 
quarantine stations in the northern ports. 
Most of the livestock is exported through the northern ports of Berbera and Bosaso transiting 
through Beletweyn, Galkayo, and Burao. These towns are important collection markets for most of 
the pastoral livelihood zones of Somaliland and Puntland, as well as from the southern and central 
rangelands and from across the border in Ethiopia’s and Kenya’s Somali regions. From Burao the 
highway leads straight to Berbera, Somaliland’s main port, from where animals are shipped to Saudi 
Arabia and Yemen, as Berbera is closest to the Yemeni ports across the Gulf of Aden from where 
many animals also transit into Saudi Arabia. Bosaso is the second most important port for exporting 
animals, mainly to Oman and the UAE. Exports of cattle and camels, the latter dominated by exports 
to Egypt, also take place from the northern ports, though numbers are significantly lower than those 
of shoats. Beletweyn is the main connection hub between the southern regions and the main 
northern export markets. For both Somaliland and Puntland several studies are available that 
describe the live animal export value chain. Livestock exports from southern ports came to a 
complete stop following the civil war and exports of shoats resumed from Mogadishu only in 2013 
and of cattle from Kismayo only in 2017. Camel exports from Mogadishu to Egypt have also recently 
started. 
 
  Figure 3.5: Livestock Exports, total number of heads, 1994–2018 
 
 
Source: FSNAU (IDS online database). 
 
At the peak in 2015, a record of 5.3 million live animals were exported from Somalia, composed of 
4.9 million shoats, 295,000 cattle, and 72,000 camels, 6.3 percent above the previous year’s level. 
The value of exports of live animals for food reported by Somalia’s trading partners to the U.N. 
COMTRADE database rose in parallel from around US$56 million annually (on average) in the late 
1980s to an estimated US$377 million annually during 2011–2014, and a peak of US$494 million in 
2015. The additional value of live animals exported for breeding purposes also jumped from virtually 
nil before the civil war to a peak of US$81 million in 2014, though it fell back to $30 million in 2016. 
Eexports of camels and cattle have also seen a spectacular increase, though with wide annual 
variations, since the 1990s, with their number of heads increasing from a range of 16,000-65,000
=== PAGE 52 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 32 
 
 
 
to around 140,000 at their peak in 2011 (according to FSNAU), mainly to Egypt. This may have been 
due to political tensions in Sudan, the traditional camel supplier for Egypt, and between Sudan and 
Egypt. The unfortunate plight of Sudan and the new demand from Egypt has opened an upcoming 
market opportunity for Somalia. Berbera being the larger and more modern port, its export figures 
for both camels and cattle are higher than for Bosaso. However, since 2015 there has been a marked 
increase in cattle exported from Bosaso and a decrease from Berbera. 
The most recent drought spell, which started in 2015 in the northern regions and extended to the 
south central regions in the second half of 2016, already started reducing during 2016 both the 
animal population and, due to the very poor health of surviving animals, also livestock exports, 
which dropped from 5.3 million in 2015 by almost 13 percent to 4.7 million heads in 2016. The 
most recent import ban imposed by Saudi Arabia and the UAE in December 2016 has taken a 
further massive toll, at least in the short term, with the total number of animals exported from all 
four major Somali ports collapsing to 2.7 million during 2017 and 2.8 million during 2018  
(according to FSNAU-IDS database). Of these about 1.2 million or about 40 percent were sacrificial 
shoats absorbed by Saudi Arabia just before and during the Haj and shipped from the northern ports 
of Berbera and Bosaso. The rest were destined mostly to Oman and Yemen.

Compared to the more formal northern trading routes, less information is available on the 
southern cross-border trading routes for livestock, which are largely unrecorded, including cattle 
exports from Jubaland into Kenya and Ethiopia and shoats from Kenya’s and Ethiopia’s border 
areas to be re-exported through Somalia’s northern ports. Though numbers on formal and informal 
livestock  cross-border  trade  are  unknown,  they  are  believed  to  be  substantial,  as  for  Somali 
pastoralists clan territories transgress the Somali border for almost its entire length, from northeast
=== PAGE 53 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy

Djibouti to northeast Kenya (Bishop & Catley, 2008). Before the collapse of the Somali state there 
was a common cross-border route with livestock moving from Ethiopia and northern Kenya into 
Somalia for exporting through the port of Mogadishu. Collapse of the state caused a reversal in the 
direction of livestock trade, leading to an outflow of livestock, particularly camels and cattle from 
Somalia to Kenyan and Ethiopian markets (Mahmoud, 2013). However, in recent years shoats from 
northern Kenya have been and are still been transported all the way to and exported from the 
northern ports.

Livestock sector exports, furthermore, are characterized by highly concentrated product and

(virtually all to Oman) and hides and skins. Moreover, following the lifting in 2009 of the prior import 
ban by Saudi Arabia imposed in 2001 and the opening of modern quarantine stations in Somaliland 
and Puntland managed by a Saudi company, over 98 percent of live animals exports (by value) went 
before the most recent drought and import bans to only four countries in the Arabian Peninsula (see
