Regarding livestock products and key inputs to the livestock sector, the large bill starting in the late 2000s 
and ranging since 2011 from $60 million to $82 million for dairy imports, of which 90 percent is for 
“preserved or concentrated” milk, that is, long-life processed milk and milk powder, stands out as the most 
obvious missed import substitution opportunity. Since 2015, there are also sizeable meat imports around 
US$7-8 million, less than half of which is explained by poultry meat imports (mostly frozen). Imports of animal 
feed are almost nil, which is further evidence of the minimal level of modern, commercial operations of the 
Somali livestock production and trading systems, as well as of the feed subsector. On the contrary, imports of 
“medicaments, including for veterinary use” have been growing and have hovered during 2015-2017 around 
$49-63 million annually. Import of vitamins, antibiotics, and other supplements (e.g. minerals) and 
pharmaceutical products for either people or animals barely register in the international trade statistics. 
 
3.7 
Production and Consumption Trends of Livestock Products 
As shown in Table 3.6, the production (and domestic consumption) of the main livestock products, 
milk and meat experienced substantial growth through the mid-2010s (World Bank/FAO 2018),  
but suffered severely from the impact of the recent drought spells during 2016-2017. The 
aggregate drought-related loss just from lower milk production and reduced body weight for 
exported or slaughtered animals has been estimated at a staggering $1.2 billion (Somalia 2018). The 
production of other items like hides and skins, which used to be important for export and for
=== PAGE 57 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 37 
 
 
21 F 
 
domestic tanning before the civil war, poultry, which has grown in the post-war period from almost 
nil before, and honey are still small today, though providing alternative and/or complementary 
sources of livelihood to some (smaller) population groups throughout the country. 
 
3.7.1 The Dairy Subsector 
Pastoral dairy production is one of the most valuable goods for both rural and urban populations, 
used for both household consumption and commercialization. It is one of the key sources of 
income and employment generation for women and youth. It is also vital for child nutrition. The 
dairy value chain includes individual producers selling directly at nearby markets (villages/towns), as 
well as specialist milk traders (mainly women) who receive or collect milk in bulk from wide 
catchments of producing areas and then market them in the towns (see Figure 3.7). Further to this, 
in recent years, commercially oriented herds of dairy camels and cows are reared quasi-permanently 
in confined areas within a short range to urban centers and marketed to hotels and restaurants in 
the cities. Solely on the basis of the projected growth of 2.9 percent for the human population, there 
will be more than half a million new potential consumers in 2030 compared to 2019, but demand is 
believed to grow further due to income gains and urbanization (Oxfam 2012 & 2018; FAO 2019; 
Hussein 2019). 
Camel milk, which is not typically processed beyond cooling and packaging in plastic pouches for 
street-side sale, is the most popular and fetches the highest price in the market, as the demand is 
hardly met (demand for camel milk can surge by up to 22% during fasting months like Ramadan). 
Camel milk is composed of lactose, fat, and protein in roughly the same proportion as bovine milk. 
However, their relative composition, distribution, and the molecular structures of the milk 
components are different and as a result camel milk is difficult to process into products and is thus 
mainly suitable for drinking as fresh or sour milk. 20 If refrigerated the shelf life of camel milk can be 
as long as 7 days after which the milk become sour and still consumed as such. Quality concerns such 
as bacteria levels in primary processed items like sweets and curd were often not attributed high 
priority, and it was suggested that consumers have low or no awareness of food safety issues 
(Oxfam, 2018). Notwithstanding the popularity of camel milk, cow and goat milk are also in demand 
and often home-processed into ghee for self-consumption and local sale. 
Agro-pastoral and pastoral fresh milk production still accounts for most of the milk sold in city 
markets and will continue to do so in the foreseeable future. Over-dependence on degraded 
pastures leaves the agro-pastoral and pastoral milk producers struggling to maintain current low 
milk production levels. Feed supplementation of lactating animals is widely practiced, but is 
constrained by lack of skills and high costs and limited availability of quality animal feeds (USAID, 
2017). According to stakeholders, lack of finance, knowledge and trained technicians is the main 
impediment for the expansion of storage and cold chain facilities and processing of milk, enabling 
the large-scale importation of long-life milk powder and packaged milk and small imports of yogurt 
and other milk products. 
Although there is a draft National Dairy Act, lacking to date are also updated regulations (or 
enforcement of those existing before the civil war) for milk hygiene and fresh milk adulteration 
practices and quality along the chain. Consumption of powder milk is also promoted vigorously by 
 
 
20 Tesfemariam Berhe, Eyassu Seifu, Richard Ipsen, Mohamed Y. Kurtu, and Egon Bech Hansen, “Processing Challenges and 
Opportunities of Camel Dairy Products,” International Journal of Food Science, vol. 2017, Article ID 9061757, 8 pages, 2017
=== PAGE 58 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 38 
 
 
19 F 
20F 
 
media outlets. As a result, mothers purchase imported powder milk products to feed their babies 
while adults are the main consumers of fresh milk (SATG and UNDP Somalia, 2016). Without any 
prescribed norms that stipulate testing the quality and safety of milk, no one knows the composition 
of, or the presence of veterinary drug residues in, fresh raw milk marketed, with the exception of 
some yogurt processing companies and possibly some embassies and large foreign businesses (such 
as private security companies) in Mogadishu that test in-house the raw milk they receive from their 
suppliers. 
 
  Figure 3.7 -  Milk Value Chain in Somalia 
 
 
 
 
The value of milk production, mostly from camels, had risen steadily since the late 1980s to reach 
US$2.8 billion in 2014 (baseline before the drought), before dropping to $2.4 billion in 2017 
(because of the drought impact, see Somalia 2018). Even before the drought, productivity per 
animal was relatively low, with average yield for cows is 4.1L/day in the wet season and as little as 
2.4L/day in the dry season (a 40% seasonal drop) and camel yield averaging 3L/day in the wet season 
and 2.1L/day in the dry season (with a 30% seasonal drop). 21 These figures are low when compared 
with the average yield of camels in neighbouring Ethiopia that produce 8.6 litters of milk/day in the 
wet season and 5.8L/day in the dry season. 22 
By and large the dairy trade is informal and, according to Oxfam 2018 and FAO 2019, consumers 
prefer to buy unpackaged fresh milk and are unwilling or unable to pay the extra costs of formal 
processing and packaging, making their purchase decisions largely on price. They conclude that 
value addition for milk may not generate returns as the market for processed milk products is a 
small niche segment, and most buyers are unwilling or again unable to pay for processing of any 
kind. Among their findings are that the key determinants of buying behaviour are first and foremost 
price, then home delivery and regular supply of quality (unspoilt) fresh milk. 
 
 
21 USAID. (2017). Somalia Growth, Enterprise, Employment & Livelihoods (GEEL): Dairy Baseline Study. 
22 Galma Wako. 2015. Economic value of camel milk in pastoralist communities in Ethiopia: Findings from Yabello district, 
Borana zone. IIED Country Report. IIED, London.
=== PAGE 59 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 39 
 
 
 
Nonetheless, in Mogadishu there are three commercial dairy factories that have grown rapidly 
with the support of USAID-Geel (USAID 2019), operate profitably and are on a rapid expansion 
path, indicating growing demand for hygienic milk and dairy products. Irmaan Dairy and SoMilk 
Dairy mainly focus on producing, testing, processing and packaging hygienic, including pasturized, 
camel and cow milk that is sold not only to hotels, restaurants, embassies and wholesale milk 
traders. SoMilk also produces and markets its own line of yogurt. Another dairy company, Oog Dairy 
established in 2016, has in fact been sourcing fresh milk from both SoMilk and its own dairy farm for 
making, packaging and selling to wholesale buyers mainly yogurt, but also some cheese and ghee (16 
tons in 2018; see also Box 6.1 for more details on its success and business expansion plans). In 
Garowe, moreover, there is a micro-enterprise whose pasteurized camel milk and limited home- 
made yogurts sell out every day. And in Hargeisa, where all fresh camel milk is still sold only in clear 
plastic pouches by street-side sellers (as in the rest of the country, save for the exceptions 
mentioned above), there is a small company making yogurt out of imported milk powder. 
 
3.7.2 The Meat Subsector 
While production of fresh red meat was on the rise through the mid-2010s, almost exclusively to 
meet rising domestic demand, overall output remained well below potential both before and after 
the severe 2016/2017 drought, largely due to inadequate slaughtering and marketing facilities. 
Pastoralists sell their animals at the nearest primary town markets to either local butchers or 
wholesale traders, who bring them to larger urban markets for resale, including to butchers 
operating in the local slaughterhouses/slabs (see Figure 3.3 for a map of the meat value chain). 
Informal slaughtering of shoats by individuals in their backyards and larger animals by private 
butchers in poorly designed, open, unhygienic slaughter facilities remains the norm. These facilities, 
moreover, are not subject to any enforcement of sanitary regulations and/or meat inspection 
services, since a draft Meat Inspection Act is not yet endorsed. Electricity and cold chains are not 
readily available, partly due to their very high investment and operating costs and prohibitive energy 
costs (among the highest in Africa), resulting in high risk of disruption and monetary losses in the 
value chain. 
Somalia’s most important source of red meat is camels, supplying 52 percent of actual meat 
consumption. According to a study commissioned by IGAD’s Centre for Pastoral Areas and Livestock 
Development (ICPALD 2015), furthermore, domestic consumption in Somalia averaged 4.0kg of beef, 
4.3kg of small ruminant meat and 21.2 kg of camel meat per person in 2013 (ICPALD, 2015). In 
contrast, another study on competitiveness of the meat value chain in Somaliland (Ombui, Mogoa, & 
Matete, 2014) notes that the main sources of red meat for the Somaliland consumer are cattle, 
sheep, goats and camels, in order of importance, and that sheep and goats constitute 93 percent by 
number of the total animals slaughtered in all the six towns surveyed. The contradiction between 
these two studies is likely explained by variations in production systems (and herd composition) in 
the different areas that were surveyed. Analysis of meat trade in Somaliland indicated that trading in 
meat is relatively profitable in all urban markets covered by the study. Better margins (53-63%) were 
obtained when trading in camel meat and beef (51-53%), while lower margins (1.4-27.2%) were 
obtained when trading in sheep and goat meat (Ombui, Mogoa, & Matete, 2014). 
Under the EU-funded ESOLT project (completed in 2017), the FAO conducted various feasibility 
studies about competitively identifying and entering into agreements with private companies to 
manage through public private partnership (PPP) arrangements the day-to-day operations of the 
municipal slaughterhouses that the project was rehabilitating. In the case of the Bosaso facility, an
=== PAGE 60 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 40 
 
 
22F 
 
investment appraisal undertaken in 2017 concluded that the business was indeed commercially 
viable, but that it needed to increase the fees charged and volumes handled and significantly cut 
down operation and administrative expenses to improve its rates of return, payback period and net 
present value. A limitation of this appraisal study, however, was that it did not include an 
assessment of the supply of animals. 
 
Box 3.1: Limitations and Failures in Meat Processing Before the Civil War 
 
 
Other companies, with relatively modern slaughterhouses in Burao, Galkayo and Beletweyn, 
which had ventured in the past two decades into the chilled goat meat export business by air, 
went bankrupt and have ceased operations. There are multiple reasons for the failure of all chilled 
meat export ventures, according to Negassa, et al. (2012), the World Bank-FAO study (2018), and 
more recent feedback from stakeholders (see Annex 1): a ban by the UAE on old Russian aircrafts 
used by Somali exporters causing a major spike in air cargo costs from inland towns; management 
failures; uneven seasonal supply of slaughter animals with carcass weight required by importing 
markets (7-10 Kg); and high prices for livestock in Somali markets, due to both strong export demand 
for live animals exports (before the most recent import ban in December 2016) and for domestic 
meat consumption. Meat exporters from poor countries such as Somalia face stiff competition on 
weight, sanitary standards, and price from exporters in other developed or emerging economies, 
such as Australia, New Zealand, India and Ethiopia for fresh, chilled and frozen sheep and goats, and 
Brazil, Pakistan, India and Australia for fresh, chilled and frozen bovine meat. 
One of the most comprehensive studies by ILRI and Terra Nuova in 2012 described the Somali 
chilled export meat value chain in terms of actors, institutions and practices, and profitability 
(Negassa, et al., 2012). The study highlighted evidence of economies of scale, and of financial 
advantage in species specialization (specifically, goats) by traders, and recognized a consistently 
applied and widely understood grading system in the country for slaughter stock. Quality 
requirements in importing countries were also well known throughout the chain, in that actors’ 
rankings of attributes were consistent within and between actor stages. 23 Inconsistencies 
throughout the chain, however, included the nature and strength of trading relationships: long-lived 
 
 
23 FAO’s SOMEAT Project (as well as its UNDP sister project) provided extensive training in meat safety requirements of 
exporting countries (Good Hygienic Practice and Hazard Analysis Critical Control, HACCP) to key players in the meat value 
chain (ministry staff, livestock traders, brokers, transporters, butchers and export slaughterhouse staff). 
Before the civil war, the Kismayo Meat Factory (KMF) was the only meat factory in Somalia. The plant was 
funded by the Soviet Union and constructed between 1964 and 1968, to produce about 9 tons a day of stewed 
canned meat and meat in its own juice. In 1974–75, a canned corned beef line was added and the canned 
meat line expanded. Daily production capacity after 1975 was 13 tons of stewed canned meat or beef in own 
juice (40,000 cans), 6.8 tons of corned beef (20,000 cans), and 2 tons of sausage. During 1969–76, the factory 
operated at close to capacity, slaughtering 200 head of cattle a day. In 1973, KMF purchased 1,291 tons of 
cattle for sale as frozen sides. During 1980–84, it produced only 1–700 tons of fresh and frozen cooked meat. 
Canned output already began to fall in 1977. Since then, KMF operated only sporadically, working less than 50 
days a year during 1983–85. Because of high cattle prices in the free market and KMF products’ sale prices, 
which were controlled by the government, the company never covered its costs of production in any year 
since its establishment. Other factors hampering its operation included depletion of physical capital and the 
lack of spare parts from the Soviet Union. Like all other state-owned factories, its operations collapsed and its 
physical structures were looted right after the start of the civil war. 
Source : International Science and Technology Institute (ISTI), 1986 (USAID-funded)
=== PAGE 61 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 41 
 
 
 
at exporter and agent level, but short-term and cash-based at producer level. Although exporters 
made payments mostly on the basis of carcass weight, agents of exporters paid based on per head of 
live animals. 
Commercial meat processing is currently mostly limited to small quantities of whole beef chilled 
carcasses exported by air in cool crates and frozen carcasses sea in refrigerated containers from 
Mogadishu to Oman by the Somali Meat Company (SoMeat), which started operations in 
Mogadishu in 1996, plus some small quantities by two other recently established companies in 
Hargeisa and Galkayo targeting the local markets. 
 
The Somali Meat Company has a modern slaughterhouse, with machinery sourced from, 
installed and maintained by Italy, that can slaughter 100–120 animals per hour, and has 
recently built a second one on the outskirts of Mogadishu. It employs in total 300 workers, 260 
of them seasonal, and exports 98 percent of the meat it processes, with 712.1 tons of chilled 
meat by air (in cool crates shipped in the cargo hold of passenger aircrafts) and 1,588.3 tons of 
deep-frozen meat exported to Oman in last two years (2017-2018) in refrigerated containers by 
sea. In regard to waste management, SoMeat at present burns most the stomach content of 
the animals for manure, which it sells directly to farmers who collect it from the slaughterhouse 
for use as fertilizer to their crops. By-products are also sold to small (mostly female) traders 
who in turn resell them in the city street markets. Slaughterhouse effluent is brought to a 
largepurpose-built reservoir far from the slaughterhouse. As of June 2019, SoMeat was still 
awaiting the UAE to inspect its facilities, so as to grant the company permission to export and 
seeking ISO 2200 international certification. It is also currently exploring exporting 
opportunities to Bahrain. 
 
Hilibsan Meat Company, established in the first half of 2019 to supply hygienc meat cuts to 
Hargeisa consumers owns land for a livestock holding ground (where animals are fed with grass 
and water and pre-slaughter health examinations are carried out) and uses its own refrigerated 
trucks for transportation of carcasses of animals slaughtered by its butchers at the Maandeeq 
abattoir. In its modern butcher shop with various cutting machines, large refrigerators for 
storage and refrigerated glass displays, it conducts modern meat cuts that are different from 
traditional cuts of butchers in open air markets, sells to retail customers and delivers to 15 
hotels. In addition to fresh meat priced by weight, it also packages and vacuum seals meat for 
long-life. It currently employs 14 staff, including a veterinarian who performs pre- and post- 
mortem examination of animals and an Ugandan meat expert who trains the rest of the staff. 
Confident in the growing demand for safe and hygienic meat, the company also plans to open 
more hygienic butcher shops in different quarters of Hargeisa and expand its delivery business 
to restaurants and private residences. 
 
Beira Food Processing Plc, established in March 2019 near Galkayo, currently focuses on 
processing meat, beans and vegetables sourced locally or from neighboring regions to supply 
the local market with vacuum-packed, packaged beef stew, pasta sauce and beans and 
vegetable sauce, and also has plans for canning meat and exporting chilled meat. Currently, it 
employs 50 persons, and owns modern power, cooling, processing and packaging machinery 
imported from China and a food laboratory to handle necessary safety and quality controls. 
 
3.7.3 The Hides and Skins Subsector 
While hides and skins exports were before the war the third largest item in value, after livestock 
and bananas, recent production of hides and skins, a by-product of animals slaughtered for 
consumption, is comparable in to levels of the late 1980s, at about 7 million pieces a year. 
However, export earnings have dropped from a peak of $8.7 million (annual average) in 2006-2014
=== PAGE 62 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 42 
 
 
23F 
 
(and $5.2 million during 1986-1990) to US$2.3 million in 2016 and 2017. Before the civil war various 
tanneries used to process the hides and skins through the wet blue stage, before selling them to 
local artisans or exporting them. The hides and skins trade and processing, however, collapsed in the 
1990s. Currently there are a few traders who pick up the skins from villages and hides from 
slaughterhouses. Processing, however, has been minimal and limited today to sun-drying and dry- 
salting since the outbreak of the civil war and the total looting of pre-war tanneries, which used to 
produce wet blue leather. Domestic (rough) leather handicrafts (such as shoes and bags) by artisans 
is at presently negligible, largely confined to sandals and stools. 
Hides and skins are a byproduct of animals slaughtered for consumption and the quality of hides 
and skins is directly affected by the health of animals and of their grazing environment, as well as 
by the flaying skills of the butchers and the storage practices of the traders (Figure 3.8 provides a 
representation of the hides and skins value chain, highlighting the different stages of product 
development, sourcing and marketing channels). Hides and skins are produced at the household 
level for small stock, and at a larger scale at slaughter facilities for camel and cattle hides. Small 
traders collect them from villages and slaughterhouses, where they are sun-dried, before being 
resold to wholesale traders, who cure them further, usually resulting in dry salted hides and skins 
before exporting them or selling them to the few artisans and tanning companies that have been 
operating in Somalia in the past two decades to cater solely to the domestic market for (rough) 
leather products. Market destinations for Somali hides and skins include Dubai, Pakistan, UAE,  
China, and Italy. 
Hides and skins are today of poor quality, and improper processing, preservation and storage 
often leaves them damaged by vermin. Both the quality and supply of hides and skins is directly 
affected by the health of animals, the nature of the grazing environment (e.g. thorny bushes can 
damage the skin causing scars on it thereby lessening the quality of the skin), and by production 
practices. Seasonal or prolonged droughts negatively impact the industry as production of healthy 
and quality animals is reduced, thus yielding mostly hides and skins of inferior quality. This is 
underlined by the high rates of rejections by importers, because of poor quality of and damage to 
the hides and skins (Ombui, Mogoa, & Matete, 2014) and by the very low prices currently being 
fetched by producers, as low as US$0.30 per hide or skin. 24 
Box 3.2 - Chinese factory accused of poisoning Somaliland River water closed down. 
 
 
 
 
 
24 These high rates of rejection and low prices still characterize the subsector to this day, despite that in 2017 FAO’s ESOLT Project 
provided training on modern methods of hides and skin handling and processing to meat value chain actors and produced a training 
manual on Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) for improving the quality of hides, skins and leather products (both in English and Somali). 
A tanning factory, Jeronimo Group of Industries and Trading PLC, set up in the Spring of 2009 by a subsidiary of 
Chinese glove-making firm Phiss in the village of Da’ar Buluq, 60km outside the Somaliland capital Hargeisa, 
was the first foreign-owned tannery in the country post-war history. According to the company website, the 
factory processed 4,000 goat-and sheepskins from raw to wet blue and employed 60 local workers and 6 
Chinese staff. It was shut down by the Somaliland government in 2014, following years of accusations by local 
people of the dumping dangerous chemicals and related health complaints. 
A 2013 article in the Guardian newspaper reported that people living near the factory, had made numerous 
complaints about respiratory problems. A former worker at Jeronimo named Ibrahim said that one day, while 
mixing chromium compounds without a mask, he was overcome by the smell and fell down, hitting his head. 
"The company did not take me to the hospital," he says. "To this day I still have breathing problems." Other 
locals confirmed many health complaints had been made.
=== PAGE 63 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 43 
 
 
 
When the Guardian investigated the Jeronimo compound it found an unbearable smell, and workers with no 
face masks or proper shoes and sacks of corrosive material spilling onto the factory floor. Industrial waste 
[was being] dumped in local waterways, the company admitted, but it was adamant it had adhered to local 
and international rules governing the tanning industry. Livestock [in the area], which comprises up to 80% of 
local trade, had disappeared as animals refused to drink the water and their herders moved elsewhere, said 
local elders and government officials: "The water here was free; God-given. Now people have to buy it from 
travelling sellers. A 20-litre jerry costs US$0.80, too much." 
Foreign investments like Jeronimo [were and still] are seen as vital in proving Somaliland's worth as an 
independent nation, a point not lost on Da’ar Buluq's residents. "We've talked to ministers, deputy ministers. 
Each time our arguments are passed on to someone else," one village elder says. "The government considers 
that it is fighting a broader war internationally to attract foreign investors. So if this one is clamped down on it 
will have a negative impact on that." Somaliland's Chamber of Commerce secretary general, Ibrahim Ismail 
Elmi, said that while a seven-minister delegation was sent to inspect Jeronimo in 2008 [after its opening], the 
situation had been "under review" ever since. But another senior official, who asked to remain anonymous, 
added: "We suspect them [Jeronimo]. They use poisons and chromes, [which] are getting into the [dry] river 
[nearby]." Jeronimo had a $6m agreement with the Somaliland government, the official claimed, that had to 
be paid in full should the firm be shut down. "We don't have the capacity to refund them, so we just give 
advice." 
"If the government was worried about these health issues, they should have checked before we came," says Li 
Fai La, the factory manager. Rather than remove or recycle effluent, he said the company dumped industrial 
waste "in water 3km away from the factory." Li said that he had considered moving Jeronimo to neighboring 
Ethiopia. "You can go to the factory now," he says. "Yes, the smell is bad but trees are growing and there are 
fish in the water nearby.” Although he did not specify what percentage of waste is dumped, the Guardian 
understood the factory had no waste management system in place. 
 
Source: Sean Williams, in the “Guardian”, published on Tue 2 Jul 2013 
 
There are currently no modern facilities and tanneries in the country processing the hides and 
skins collected from formal slaughter facilities in towns and informal slaughters in villages to wet 
blue stage or leather, only traders curing them to dry salted stage and very poor-quality wet blue 
stage. The only post-war tannery, located between Hargeisa and Berbera and owned by Chinese 
investors, sustained operations for only a few years in the early part of this decade, as it was forced 
to cease operations by the local population enraged at the chemical pollution to soil and water 
sources nearby that it created (See Box 3.2).

3.7.4 The Poultry and Beekeeping Subsectors 
There are three distinct systems in Somalia for poultry production: (i) commercial intensive, which 
is rare and largely limited to the main urban centers of Mogadishu and Hargeisa; (ii) semi-intensive 
or backyard, which is practiced in most regional and small towns; and (iii) free range poultry 
production, which is common at the village level. No domestic production and consumption 
estimates exist, but poultry keeping is widespread among poor households, for both self- 
consumption and resale. Traditionally Somalis have raised village poultry for family subsistence and 
some poultry under the semi-intensive or backyard production system for local sales and have not 
bred chickens for meat or eggs on a commercially viable scale. 
Commercial poultry farming, however, has been on the rise, particularly in urban areas, though 
almost exclusively to supply eggs to nearby towns. There are today 38 medium- and large-scale 
commercial poultry farms in the country, with 23 of those in the Banadir Region and surrounding 
areas. Intensive poultry production is highly capital intensive because it relies on (growing) imports 
of day-old chicks, drugs, vaccines and equipment. As a result of strong local demand by a growing 
diaspora community in larger towns, large remittance inflows since the civil war, and the rising price 
of goat and camel meat, chicken meat has become more widely available in urban markets and more 
affordable relative to other meats. Almost all chicken sold in Somalia on a commercial scale, 
however, is imported via Dubai, much of it frozen from Brazil, and most eggs are imported from 
Yemen, though volumes have been declining due to security challenges in the north of the country. 
All such imports take place without any quality control and health certification. 
There is only anecdotal evidence about beekeeping, which remains a small-scale side business for 
most beekeepers. Until recently there were only some small beekeeping operations in rural areas 
throughout the country, and especially concentrated in the beekeeping belt of Bay and Bokol regions 
of South West state, which employ traditional hives and honey harvesting techniques. Recently, 
however, Hormuud (the largest telecom company in Somalia) established commercial beekeeping 
operations in the Tiyeglo area of Bokol region and several other areas of South West state as well as 
in Dhusamareb in Galmudug state. Consumption is still limited to households and local markets.
=== PAGE 65 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 45 
 
 
 
3.8 
Sector Policy Framework 
Somalia’s paramount development framework is the National Development Plan (NDP) 2017- 
2019, the first development plan crafted by a Somali government since 1986. The NDP stipulates 
the country‘s short and medium term strategic direction, development priorities and proposed 
implementation mechanisms, including the use of development aid. Its main theme is to accelerate 
the country’s socio-economic transformation in order to achieve the stated objectives for poverty 
alleviation, economic revival and societal transformation in a socially just and gender equitable 
manner. The NDP includes a specific chapter on livestock, setting out a vision and related 
interventions to support the delivery of essential services necessary to achieving a vibrant and 
commercially competitive sector, sustainable livelihoods, and inclusive economic growth. This 
chapter set out four main operational goals: (i) strengthening institutional capacity and public 
infrastructure relevant to the livestock sector; (ii) increasing animal health services and the 
productivity of small and large livestock sector producers; (iii) enhancing trade in livestock and 
livestock products; and (iv) improving national food security, nutrition, and food safety within the 
livestock sector. 
 
Box 3.3 - Status of the 2016 Veterinary Law Code 
 
 
Furthermore, the FGS and FMSs have endorsed a number of policies to guide the development of 
the livestock sector. Among these, the most important nationwide policy document is the Livestock 
Policy 2015-2020 of the MoLFR (Somalia 2015). This document presented the FGS’ vision for the 
sector, spelled out the public and private sector core functions and addressed issues and needs 
critical for the sector’s development related to livestock health, trade, land degradation and range 
management, institutional framework and coordination, and infrastructure. An updated draft Somali 
Livestock Development Policy was also prepared in February 2017 (by Dr. Abdirahman Nur Qeiliey), 
but was not formally adopted by the ministry (Somalia 2017). Among other recommendations to 
support production levels, it reaffirmed the longstanding prohibition under the Veterinary Law Code 
on export of female animals. In addition, the Ministry of Finance has been implementing a policy of 
Before 2016, the legal framework for registration and licensing of veterinary services providers were based on 
the National Veterinary Code Law No. 20 of the 27th June 1967 and subsequent Ministerial decrees of 12th 
November 1969, 11th November 1968 and 15th September 1985. To foster compliance with modern food 
safety standards and requirements by the World Animal Health Organization (OIE) in its Animal Health 
Terrestrials Code, Chapter 5, as well as by the WTO and foreign governments and private sectors concerning 
import/export procedures, the FGS adopted the new Veterinary Law Code No: 21 of 27 June 2016. 
Since then, the MoLFR has been evaluating for renewal all licenses previously issued to livestock service 
providers, traders and processors. Covered providers include those engaged in disease notifications and 
investigations; dairy processing plants and markets; slaughterhouses and meat markets; live animals, meat and 
hides and skins export trade and certifications; quarantine management; livestock-related agribusinesses 
(dairy and poultry farms), and private veterinarians. for delivering good services which comply with the new 
standards and measures required by the OIE, WTO. SPS and the requirements of the importing countries. 
The new veterinary law code is already under enforcement and MoLFR’s and states’ inspection officers are 
instructed to apply strictly the articles and provisions of the new veterinary law code before issuing any health 
or inspection certificates for livestock and livestock products intended for local consumptions or export. Weak 
capacity, low budget allocations, and in the southern regions of the country also the current patchy security 
situation, however, pose the main challenges for the enforcement of the Veterinary Law Code.

3.9 
Overview of Main Development Challenges for the Livestock Sector 
Despite the recent performance through the mid-2010s in livestock export volumes and earnings, 
Somalia continues to face several major constraints and challenges to the development of its 
livestock subsector. This section summarizes the main constraints to the development of the sector, 
as identified by the federal government in its most recent policy, strategy and planning documents 
(Somalia 2015; 2017; and 2016) and by public and private stakeholders during the recent 
consultations over the sector’s strategy. These challenges (as well as opportunities and 
recommended interventions) are discussed in more detail for each value chain/subsector in chapter 
5 and 6 and for cross-cutting issues in chapter 7 of the LSDS. Many traditional structural constraints 
associated with livestock production have intensified in Somalia recent decades, including: 
 
water shortages, due to insufficient and irregular and inadequately harvested rainfall, affecting 
livestock health and survival, and availability of fodder; 
 
lack of access to good quality pasture, due to land degradation and reduction of productive 
land, which is in turn the result of deforestation, soil erosion, invasion of unpalatable plant 
species, spread of private enclosures, and increasingly frequent recurrence of natural shocks 
such as drought and floods; 
 
lack, or poor quality, of fodder at export ports, along transit routes to them, and around urban 
centers; 
 
seasonal fluctuations in market prices for livestock and livestock products, due to the factors 
listed above and to the lack of storage facilities; 
 
transboundary animal diseases with occasional flare-up of epidemics, other livestock diseases, 
and pests, due to poor and inadequate animal health services and laboratory facilities; 
 
overreliance on only a few export markets and on live animal exports; 
 
persistent transport and market infrastructural and logistical weaknesses, which undermine 
delivery of high-quality animals to domestic and foreign markets and expansion of dairy 
production and processing; 
 
persistent insecurity in large parts of southcentral Somalia; 
 
weak institutional framework, including limited and largely informal organization of the 
livestock value chain, lack of coordination and integration among its actors, and poor linkages 
and ineffective negotiations and agreements with its main trading partners that also sharply 
limit the country’s potential in the livestock commodities export trade; 
 
absence of necessary legislation and lack of enforcement of existing rules and regulations; 
 
limited availability of quality veterinary education and technical training facilities in pastoral 
areas; and 
 
financial constraints – with very low government budgetary allocations to livestock and lack of 
private investment in processing of livestock products (dairy, meat and hides and skins mainly) 
for value addition, partly due to poor access to credit and lack of incentives and encouragement 
by governments.
=== PAGE 68 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 48 
 
 
25F 
 
4.0 
SECTOR DEVELOPMENT STRATEGY: VISION, PRIORITIES, STAKEHOLDERS 
 
4.1 
Overall Vision for Development of Livestock Sector 
The vision is for Somali livestock sector by 2030 to make a major contribution to: i) better nutrition 
and enhanced food security of its entire population via an expanded supply of high-quality and 
hygienic animal proteins; ii) to improved livelihoods of the majority of the rural population via 
higher incomes from livestock products; and iii) to the country’s economic growth, via more 
efficient, inclusive and environmentally sustainable production, improvements product quality, 
and expanded value-adding processing. This general vision for the sector, as well its development 
potential, objectives, principles, and priorities were already outlined in the Livestock Policy 2015- 
2020, were embedded in the NDP 2017-2019, and were reconfirmed by public and private 
stakeholders during the October-November 2018 consultations. It is thus a shared vision by the 
federal government, the federal member states, and the sector’s other public and private 
stakeholders. 
Despite myriad challenges, the livestock sector has the potential to become in the medium- and 
long-term resilient to climate shocks, to ensure the safety and quality of all its products, to reach 
self-sufficiency in dairy and poultry, and to export a larger surplus of higher value-added meat 
(frozen, and possibly also chilled, meat carcasses and cuts) and hides and skins. Underwriting this 
big potential across all sub-sectors are a growing local population and returning diaspora, increasing 
global demand for animal source foods, very low current production and productivity levels, and 
minimal processing. 
Commercialization and climate shocks are key drivers of wealth differentiation in pastoral areas 
and thus require a pro-poor approach to the sector’s development. Selected studies of some 
pastoral areas in the Horn of Africa have found that wealthier pastoral household are not only 
increasingly owning larger numbers of animal to the detriment of poorer households, they are also 
more actively engaged in their commercialization, and therefore benefitting more from export trade. 
Not only would a policy of promoting commercialization likely have the biggest positive impact on 
the already more commercial, and therefore generally better-off, households, but it can actually be  
a factor leading to further gradual distribution of livestock from the poorer to the wealthier 
households. Such a shift leads to various changes in terms of resource access and social behaviour 
that over time make it more difficult for poor pastoralists to build herds (Catley, 2017). Climate 
shocks also facilitate a transfer and consolidation of animal stocks from poorer households to 
wealthier households. Therefore, an important implication of this research focused on the 
pastoralist production systems is that a pro-poor approach to pastoral development should go 
beyond promoting only livestock commercialization and should focus also on preventing avoidable 
losses and better drought management, enabling poor herders to re-build and expand their herds 
and their incomes, and ensuring access to markets and rangelands (Catley, 2017). 26 
 
4.2 
Operational Objectives and Priorities 
Toward achieving such a vision and realizing the sector’s potential, this LSDS sets out a number of 
operational principles and objectives to address the main constraints that prevent its optimal 
 
 
26 In agro-pastoral and mixed farming production systems livestock also plays an important role in household income and resilience. When 
considering livestock development from a poverty reduction perspective, the role of animals in such systems should be better understood 
and documented.
=== PAGE 69 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 49 
 
 
 
performance (the constraints will be discussed in more detail in Chapter 5, 6 and 7 under each sub- 
sector or cross-cutting issue below). The principles to apply to institutional strengthening, capacity 
building, policy- and rule-making, strategic planning (as for this LSDS), sector management and 
project implementation by federal, state and local governments, as well as to the support of external 
development partners, are: consultative, participatory, and transparent processes and mechanisms; 
interactive, inclusive and evidence-based decision making; and consensus building with community 
and private sector ownership and buy-in. The LSDS operational goals are: 
1. Preserving Animal Resources – Safeguarding the diversity and optimizing the use of existing 
animal resources is critical for both resilience and growth. Key priorities identified in recent 
government documents and during the October-November 2018 stakeholders consultations 
include: 
 rehabilitating the deteriorated environment and especially the rangelands for grazing; 
 reducing the prevalence of transboundary and infectious diseases, with improved 
management and control of animal diseases and pests in line with relevant international 
standards; 
 increasing the sector’s resilience to climate change, by easing the chronic shortage of water 
and developing a commercial feed and fodder subsector; and 
 conserving and improving the available genetic resources available for the livestock sector, 
both plant and animal, including introduction of exotic stock where appropriate and 
promoting technically and financially recent private ventures on artificial insemination. 
2. Improving the management of the livestock industry - For growth of the sector, it is also critical 
to ensure optimal productivity, including for the rapidly growing dairy and poultry farming 
subsectors, and more efficient, less costly, and expanded domestic and export trade; 
3. Ensuring quality and hygienic livestock products in domestic and international markets – Food 
safety is needed to protect the health of domestic consumers as well as to regain lost export 
markets and expand into new ones; 
4. Increasing the processing of livestock products – The sector’s growth is dependent on higher 
value addition, as the current pattern of supplying domestic and export markets with live 
animals and minimally processed products has reached its limit; 
5. Supporting home-grown research institutions - Investment in analyzing and offering solutions to 
current and emerging problems is necessary for both resilience and sustainable growth of the 
sector; and 
6. Ensuring security throughout the country, but especially in affected areas in southern Somalia - 
Without enhanced security in the many areas which governments and external partners cannot 
yet access, any public and private operation for rehabilitation and development projects and for 
expanded and new business ventures would remain challenging and costly, thus attracting 
limited donor funding and private investment. 
 
The livestock sector’s overall priority development needs, further elaborated in the next sections of 
this chapter as well as in the following chapters, are: 
 
Significant capacity building of public sector institutions, including on policy formulation and 
review, planning, coordination and implementation of emergency and development programs 
and projects, drafting and enforcement of laws and regulations, ensuring quality of veterinary 
and animal husbandry education, and data collection and analysis (via both an animal population 
and production survey on the stock/output side and a feed assessment on the input side); and
=== PAGE 70 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 50 
 
 
 
 
Large public investments for expansion of public services (with emphasis on strengthening 
veterinary services, including disease control, meeting international sanitary and phytosanitary 
standards and ensuring veracity and credibility of health certifications), and for the rehabilitation 
and expansion of critical infrastructures such as for water catchment and road transport 
(Malabo Declaration); and
 
Adequate security and access to finance for the private sector to expand its currently minimal 
processing of livestock products.
Other medium-term needs for the sector’s development are: 
 
addressing the many and vast knowledge gaps; 
 
adopting a modern commercialization and market orientation mindset, including for 
livestock owners; 
 
requiring external assistance to move towards a resilience (plan and prepare for shocks) and 
development (inclusive and sustainable growth) focus, while also ensuring emergency 
support in crises; 
 
fostering and adopting appropriate and innovative technology; 
 
clarifying the respective roles and responsibilities for the public and private sectors in policy 
formulation, planning, and implementation of projects, and between and among the federal 
and state livestock, forestry and rangeland ministries and agencies; 
 
ensuring improved coordination and collaboration on all development activities; and 
 
devoting adequate resources to the sector. 
 
4.3 
Stakeholders 
This LSDS covers all public and private sector participants in the livestock economy, from policy 
and rule makers to enforcers and from producers to consumers. However, it pays particular 
attention to the inclusive nature of the strategy and its impact on marginalised groups. Public 
sector bodies that are key stakeholders in carrying out this sector strategy include federal and state 
livestock ministries and affiliated institutes and research bodies. Private sector stakeholders that are 
critical in-service provision and investment decisions include all livestock producers, input and 
service providers, processors, exporters and importers of live animals and animal products, and 
consumers that participate along the various livestock value chains. Some of these, such as private 
veterinarians and large livestock traders, are also actively engaged as members of professional 
associations that deal with livestock animal health services or with domestic marketing and exports. 
Other stakeholders include external development partners bringing lessons from international 
experiences, sharing best practices, and providing needed financing for public investment, and to 
private investment. 
Previous livestock sector studies and policy and planning documents, however, have almost 
exclusively 
focused 
on 
the 
technical 
challenges 
to 
production, 
value-addition 
and 
commercialization for both public and private stakeholders within the sector. A good 
understanding of the social, economic, cultural and traditional characteristics of the communities 
that are the intended or unintended targets and beneficiaries of sectors activities, public 
interventions and private investments is an important pre-condition for the relevance, effectiveness 
and positive long-term impact of this sector development strategy. 
There is a large gap in current knowledge regarding the socio-economic and cultural characteristics 
of the livestock sector’s current stakeholders, potential participants, their dynamic and complex 
communities, as detailed socio-economic data and analyses on these households and their
=== PAGE 71 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 51 
 
 
26F 
 
livelihood strategies are very limited. There is an even deeper gap regarding intermediate and final 
consumers of livestock products. This knowledge gap poses a big challenge to policy and decision- 
makers in assessing the implications of their recommendations and plans of action to deliver on key 
development priorities including poverty reduction, food security, nutrition, resilience, gender and 
socio-economic distribution. 27 
 
4.4 
Overall Sector Governance 
The livestock sector falls under the responsibility of the Federal Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and 
Range (MoLFR), with the engagement of the federal members state level livestock ministries. 
These key government institutions have linkages with various public and private research, training, 
monitoring and implementation bodies, professional associations, and civil society organizations that 
provide key services, such as education, training, monitoring of weather, water, soil, animal 
population, production and trade, animal health services, technical advice, and project 
implementation. 
This LSDS supports the creation of a sector co-management framework and the collaboration of 
public and private institutions to deliver animal health and veterinary services, disease and pest 
management and control, natural resource management, market and trade infrastructure 
investments as well as promoting commercial investment in processing and value-addition. 
However, the current ad-hoc and unclear division of institutional responsibilities between the  
federal and state levels impedes needed coordination among key public stakeholders and needs 
resolution as a matter of priority. Furthermore, lack of clarity and of operational and supervisory 
arrangements for public-private partnerships (PPPs) impedes needed coordination among key 
stakeholders, resulting in low investment and poor results. For example, the export of live animals is 
currently hindered by the existing quarantine arrangements, which are based on privately owned 
and managed quarantine/holding facilities. These facilities pay veterinarians who are accredited by 
the FGS but formally under the control of the Somaliland and Puntland state governments to issue 
animal health certificates. Such arrangements provide for insufficient accountability, transparency, 
and credibility of the process. 
In order to make these and other PPP arrangements—which include municipal-owned but 
privately-managed slaughterhouses–effective, efficient and accountable, the roles and 
responsibilities of both public and private agents must be clearly defined and agreed, to enable 
each partner to perform his/her respective duties. Currently, this is not often the case. In addition, 
the rehabilitation and improved management of holding grounds and other infrastructure have to  
be clearly delegated to either public or private stakeholders, providing clarity on investment and 
maintenance responsibilities. Comprehensive analyses of the cost of compliance and its implications 
for the various stakeholders involved are also needed as a basis for improvements on current 
arrangements. 
Consultative and participatory processes and mechanisms require that livestock producers and 
other stakeholders have a voice through mechanisms such as representative producer/pastoralist 
associations/ 
cooperatives, civil society organizations or other welfare groups. Currently the stakeholders 
 
 
27 The FSNAU over the past two decades and the World Bank in the last year has gathered some data and has also 
conducted some general analyses related to these dimensions, but further data collection and analyses specifically related 
to the livestock sector by the government in close collaboration with these institutions are needed.

   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy

27F 
 
formally organised are the traders/exporters association and the livestock professional association, 
as mentioned at end of chapter 3. It is important to understand and discuss further their respective 
roles and to assess to what extent they already contribute or participate in decision-making and 
implementation. Nonetheless, in order to ensure participatory and inclusive development of the 
sector, awareness, capacity, power and resources of these organizations need to be strengthened 
with external support, so that they can participate in policy dialogues, influence policy decision- 
making processes and help the public sector deliver key services. 
 
4.4.1 
Capacity Needs: Federal Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and Range 
The federal Ministry of Livestock, Forest and Range (MoLFR), one of the oldest and strongest 
governmental institutions in Somalia, has major gaps and capacity needs in order to meet its 
mandate. MoLFR’s mandate is “to create policies, regulations and frameworks as well as provide 
oversight of livestock development, conservation of forests and range management to promote 
sustainable economic development and food security in the country”. 28 Approved by the cabinet in 
April 2016, the Ministry’s work plan (2016-2019) in the National Development Plan (NDP) has the 
following agenda, to: 
 
Strengthen institutional capacity and infrastructure of the ministry and the livestock sector; 
 
Improve livestock health services and increase productivity of small and large producers; 
 
Enhance livestock and livestock products trade (in local, regional and international markets); 
 
Improve national food security, nutrition and food safety systems in the livestock sector; 
 
Establish a system for range, forest and wildlife management; 
 
Foster South-South knowledge transfer with Somali institutions in aspects such as natural 
resource management and climate-smart technologies; 
 
Restructure and reform the current organization of the ministry, and the way it functions, in 
terms of both hierarchy and internal procedures (a task for the MoLFR’s Director General). 
 
The ministry comprises of six departments: Forestry and Range, Animal Health and Public 
Veterinary Services, Animal Production and R&D, Administration and Finance, Quarantine, 
Planning and Training. Capacity and level of operation vary across departments, but all are 
negatively impacted by insufficient policies in the livestock sector, weak internal systems, and 
inadequate infrastructure and resources, including staffing, technical expertise and knowledge, 
materials, logistical tools, supplies, etc. 
There is also weak information management system and archives, nor a research unit, which limits 
innovative proposals for livestock development. Furthermore, other resource constraints include 
basic office needs; for example, all heads of departments use their private laptops and phones. Due 
to limited available budgetary and external funds, there is also an absence of external advisors or 
experts. Insecurity in various parts of the country hinders transportation of staff for monitoring, 
coordination with the states, and service delivery. 
 
 
 
 
28 This section summarizes the findings and recommendations of two studies that have assessed the organizational and 
capacity needs of the ministry. One of these studies was commissioned by the MoLFR to undertake a comprehensive 
capacity assessment on the functions of departments within the ministry, supported by AU-IBAR. The other study was 
conducted under the Capacity Injection Mechanism (CIM), an initiative of the World Bank to enhance efficiency of public 
service delivery in the country.
=== PAGE 73 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 53 
 
 
 
The MoLFR currently lacks the resources and human capacity, including highly qualified and 
experienced technical advisors and consultants, to establish and support badly needed research 
institutions that can provide livestock producers with modern breeding products to strengthen the 
animal population’s resilience to fast changing environmental conditions. The same applies to 
agencies that would provide animal health and disease control services, disease monitoring 
mechanisms, and adequate data for planning and investment. For instance, neither the public nor 
the private livestock laboratories in the country have the modern equipment necessary to conduct 
credible analysis of various specimen for timely information to combat diseases and for assuring 
importing countries of the health of livestock shipments from Somalia. 
Capacity needs to be built by increasing budgetary resources, improving knowledge and skills 
(including IT skills) of individual staff members via training, and simultaneously enhancing 
organization capacity. The ministry needs to formalize and establish mechanisms for internal 
communication, as well as basic systems and processes like effective information management, 
office conduct, management, monitoring, and accountability. For example, there is need to sustain 
and extend the collection of the production and price data that was undertaken under the ESOLT 
Project (May 2015-March 2018), which established a Livestock Market Information Management 
System in many areas of the country, including of data on disease monitoring. Finally, the ministry 
reorganization requires clarification of the roles of heads of departments and of the Director 
General, and availability of external advisors providing technical assistance. 
There is also an urgent need for the federal ministry to enhance its role in regulating and 
managing the rangelands and other forestry resources, including the strengthening of mechanisms 
for dealing with land rights and disputes and enhancing the availability of water and nutrition 
sources for livestock. Developing federal authority over such areas will be a critical factor to ensure 
rehabilitation of the natural resource base for the nomadic pastoralism that Somalia’s livestock 
sector depends on and for minimizing communal and inter-regional and inter-state conflicts. 
 
4.4.2 
Capacity Needs: FMS Ministries of Livestock 
Institutional capacity needs and challenges across Somalia’s federal member states vary greatly, 
and especially between the long-established northern states and the more recently established 
states in the southern part of the country, where budgets, physical facilities and staffing of their 
ministries of livestock (in most cases also responsible for rangelands and forestry) are still 
minimal. This section summarizes the key capacity challenges and priority areas highlighted in the 
capacity self-assessments of the five state ministries (Galmudug, Hirshabelle, Jubaland, Puntland, 
and Southwest) that responded to a semi-structured survey conducted as part of the October- 
November 2018 stakeholders’ consultations on the LSDS (more details are in the annexes that 
summarize the findings from the consultations with each state’s stakeholders). It also includes 
information about Somaliland gathered during the state-level consultation in October 2017. 
Among the states surveyed, Somaliland’s and Puntland’s livestock ministries are the only ones 
reporting having some vehicles and own office facilities, and receiving both a budgetary allocation, 
albeit small, and some external financial assistance for both recurrent expenses and projects, and 
thus being able to maintain a fully paid ministry staff. While no budget allocation estimates were 
made available by the Somaliland ministry, of its 433 total staff, 76 or 18 percent are female, and 
239 or 55 percent (including 52 female staff) are based in Hargeisa, with the rest in regional offices. 
The 2018 budget for the Puntland ministry was $206,148, of which $95,280 (about 46%) paid out to 
staff. The ministry received US$268,080 in external assistance. All 107 staff members are paid,
=== PAGE 74 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 54 
 
 
 
including the Minister, Minister of State, Vice Minister, Director General, four department directors, 
and 84 professional technical staff (11 of whom are being paid from a World Bank-funded project). 
While Puntland is offering some basic services to livestock producers and traders, however, it offers 
none to other livestock products’ stakeholders (such as dairy farmers or butchers). 
The other four states all lack physical infrastructure and receive minimal or even no budgetary 
resources and external project funding to support functioning livestock ministries. The Jubaland 
ministry, at the better end of this group, has no vehicles nor supplies and is currently housed in 
shared, cramped building, but will soon move into a new, modern building. It also received no 
external assistance in 2018, but had a budget allocation of US$80,288, of which US$34,680 (about 40 
percent) is paid out to the Minister, Minister of State, Vice-Minister, DG, and a veterinarian. Among 
the three states in the earliest institutional building phase, the Galmudug ministry with 70 staff also 
received no external assistance and a total budget of US$67,430, from which only 12 staff (mostly 
veterinarians or veterinary technicians) are paid. Southwest listed 47 staff and no budget for the  
past three years, with only a staff member, the Director General, being paid from external sources.  
In Hirshabelle, the ministry has no infrastructure at all and received no budget or external assistance, 
with all its 25 staff working as unpaid volunteers. 
Furthermore, all these states’ ministries cite lack of information, knowledge and skills as major 
constraints to their ability to deliver services. Thus, their institutional capacity needs extend widely 
from dedicated office space to vehicles, from budgetary and external resources for paying staff 
salaries to covering the cost of necessary supplies (including fuel) deliver basic services and 
undertake simple development projects, and from access to information and knowledge to 
professional training. 
Lack of monitoring, critical facilities, and service provision related to animal health and food safety 
standards is another major challenge for most states, as they affect negatively the marketability 
(especially for export) of the animals raised or transiting through their states, the health of 
consumers, and the development of higher-valued processing. Hirshabelle, for example, has no 
centers to monitor and no clinics or emergency teams to control outbreaks of diseases, let alone 
deliver any other animal health care services. Other states struggle with compliance with 
international regulations related to livestock trade, including limited or no availability of animal 
health services and both institutional and technical limitations in health inspection and certification 
processes the major export ports. 
To meet the states’ goals of effective disease surveillance and adequate health and food safety, all 
states need strengthening of their public veterinary services, including via training of Community 
Animal Health Workers (CAHWS), replacing aging veterinarians with suitably educated young 
ones, and manning health emergency response teams. In terms of facilities to support the 
implementation of both preventative and control animal health measures, states need functioning 
and adequately equipped laboratories, stocking and delivery of vaccines to control transboundary 
diseases, rural clinics, vehicles, and other physical facilities and supplies for monitoring and 
extension services. They also listed technical and financial needs for external support to establish 
forest and range reservation lands and sustainable range land management, including the 
development and adoption of a uniform policy and legal framework to control the migration of 
livestock and the expansion of private enclosures on communal pastoral lands (emphasized by 
Galmudug and Jubaland).
=== PAGE 75 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 55 
 
 
 
There is also a general inadequacy across all states of wholesale and retail hygienic and modern 
market facilities for live animals and main livestock products such as milk and meat, with 
Hirshabelle for instance citing no market facilities at all in the entire state. Thus, capacity needs 
cited across states to support domestic trade include training of stakeholders along and 
infrastructures and equipment for the main value chains (milk, meat, hides and skins, and poultry), 
more inspectors for enhancing livestock transport, and the improvement or new development of 
wholesale (such as milk collections centers) and retail market facilities under public-private 
partnerships. Puntland emphasized also the need for legislation and regulations, their enforcement, 
and building of cooperatives. Galmudug highlighted issues with extensive, recurrent droughts and 
listed the need for projects such as drilling water wells, development of water catchments, and even 
the development of wind and solar renewable energy.

4.4.3 
Private sector: expected responsibilities 
Private sector individuals and entities are actively engaged at all levels of the livestock value chain. 
These include production, processing, domestic and international trade, veterinary services, 
provision of inputs such as veterinary drugs and animal feed, and financing. The relatively stable 
situation in Somaliland and Puntland and the return of relative peace and stability in Mogadishu,
=== PAGE 76 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 56 
 
 
 
Kismayo and surrounding milk catchment areas is attracting new investment from local and diaspora 
investors. Somaliland has recently developed an investment strategy for its private sector, while 
Puntland is in the process of upgrading its Development and Investment Authority and establishing a 
one-stop-approach for private investors. In Mogadishu, the Ministry of Planning, Investment and 
Economic Development is also in the early stages of establishing its one-stop-shop. Various  
challenge and capital venture funds have also been/are supporting Somali business start-ups and 
expansion, including commercial dairy farms and some processing business ventures. However, 
issues such as high risks related to political instability and in some areas insecurity, high cost of 
electricity, poor access to commercial financing, and the lack of well-developed, all-year-round 
supply systems make the economic risk of such investments considerably high (SATG and UNDP 
Somalia, 2016). 
The roles and functions of the private sector, including in PPP opportunities, are envisaged to 
remain as listed in the 2015 Livestock Policy, including in, but not limited to, the activities listed in 
Table 4.1. In delivering these functions, the private sector will be expected to operate within the 
regulatory environment provided by government. In many areas, the public and private sectors need 
to work closely and share important information with each other, in order for government to deliver 
effectively the required services and for the private sector to receive the support it needs, such as in 
animal health, food safety and related to bilateral commercial agreements with foreign governments 
or private importers. 
4.4.4 
Gender, youth and disadvantaged groups 
Particular attention in the design and implementation of this sector strategy will be given to 
women and youth, whose engagement in livestock trading and marketing has been traditionally at 
the local and subsistence levels and whose equitable benefits and participation should be ensured. 
Women and youth bear substantial responsibilities in livestock production, though Somali society 
maintains clearly delineated gender roles and responsibilities. Men and boys are responsible for 
most activities related to camels and cattle, including buying, owning, grazing, milking, slaughtering, 
and selling them. Women and girls are responsible for most of these same activities related to small 
stocks (goats, sheep and household poultry) as well as for the sale and processing of most livestock 
products. They control at least 80 percent of milk production and virtually all of the milk wholesale 
collection and retail trade. Men and women are jointly responsible for sheep and goats trade and 
export, with men typically handling the major livestock trade for export. 
Gender roles, however, have been shifting as households are confronted by crises and shocks, as 
men and women adopt different coping strategies to increase household resilience. Women’s 
engagement in a range of income generating activities, including livestock trade, value addition and 
product business opportunities, has increased. Though the diversification of livelihoods may 
contribute to household resilience and women economic empowerment, trade-offs occur as women 
are facing increasing workloads and responsibilities. This impacts women’s availability for 
reproductive and caring tasks and may negatively impact children’s nutritional status and well-being, 
as they may be left in the care of others for substantial periods. 
Limiting factors for inclusive and gender-sensitive sector development include inadequate 
awareness among politicians, officials and local elders on the need for gender mainstreaming, lack 
of gender-disaggregated information, and limited technical knowledge and skills and access to 
technical higher education by women. To enhance the income-generation activities of women and 
youth, awareness raising should be complemented by promotion of appropriate technologies and by
=== PAGE 77 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 57 
 
 
28F
 
their participation and empowerment in decision-making, as well as by enhancing their access to 
resources, including finance and practical information. 
Youth participation in the sector is a necessary condition for long-term development and stability, 
starting with education 29 and the improvement of technical skills and knowledge needed to 
upgrade and promote sector growth. The live animal export value chains provide the bulk of the 
employment opportunities, including small-scale traders, brokers, large scale traders, as well as 
trekkers, feedlot operators, lorry drivers, loaders and others providing ancillary services. Large 
additional employment opportunities, however, lie in the value addition and processing of livestock 
products (outputs and inputs). The expansion of dairy, meat, and fodder production offer  
substantial income generating opportunities for women, as they are already involved in many tasks 
at different levels of the value chain (from production to sales). Besides the number of jobs, the 
quality of the employment (decent work) and the per capita earnings from these jobs should be 
considered. For many of those who have moved to (peri) urban areas and found new and different 
primary livelihoods, additional support is required to integrate some livestock keeping activities in 
their livelihood portfolio. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
29 A section covering the status of and recommendations for livestock-related education and human resources is in Chapter 7.
