5.0 PRODUCTION AND MARKETING: CONSTRAINTS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
 
Several livestock subsectors are found in Somalia, varying in their relative economic importance 
among regions, as determined by factors such as the natural resource endowment, the availability 
of water, pasture and labor, and the sizes and types of livestock raised. The main value chains 
relate to ruminants (i.e., camels, cattle, sheep and goats) and to two critical inputs for their 
production and marketing (i.e., animal health services and feed/fodder). Their main products (milk, 
meat, and hides and skins) and two other emerging value chains/subsectors (poultry and 
beekeeping) are covered in the next chapter. 
 
5.1 Strengthening animal production systems 
Livestock production in Somalia is constrained by several factors linked to the nature of traditional 
production systems. These constraints include: (i) shortages of water, due to insufficient rainfall 
harvesting facilities; (ii) inadequate availability of natural feed and commercial fodder (to be 
expanded in a later section); and (iii) high incidence of diseases. The latter is mostly due to 
insufficient veterinary services and oversight, also to be expanded below. Moreover, resilience of 
animals to climate shocks remains low, partly because of the (iv) degradation and deforestation of 
the rangelands, and partly because (v) the livestock population increased greatly through the mid- 
2010s until the most recent severe drought, putting additional stress on the rangelands. Other 
systemic physical constraints to live animal trade are: (vi) inadequate and, in many cases, 
dilapidated, transport infrastructure which are increasing both cost and time for moving livestock 
from production areas to domestic urban markets or transhipment ports; and (vii) lack of adequate 
resting areas (including pre-quarantine holding grounds) with water, pasture and health/vaccination 
facilities for the large number of livestock in transit between the major livestock collection centres 
and the export ports. Another set of institutional constraints and gaps is related to (vii) the lack of 
and/or inconsistent implementation of regulations and policies, and (viii) insufficient knowledge and 
data about animal population, offtake rates, incidence of diseases, and domestic consumption, 
among others. 
Somalia conducted its last national census of livestock in 1975, and subsequent animal population 
and offtake estimates were based on limited surveys and extrapolations. Thus, new reliable 
information on the livestock sector is badly needed. Box 5.1 describes current plans for a 
comprehensive aerial and ground animal population survey to establish a reliable new baseline and 
the foundation for a credible livestock statistical system, to guide the Federal Government and 
Federal Member States and their development partners in planning and implementing effective 
livestock interventions. 
While local breeds are well adapted to the country’s harsh geographical and arid climate, poor 
breeding practices are a factor contributing to their low live weight and productivity, i.e. milk and 
meat yields. The traditional community-based breeding system, in which the best animals were 
exchanged for breeding purposes, has been negatively affected by the move toward more 
commercial production systems, in which animals with larger frames, and therefore heavier body 
weights, are sold for slaughter or live export, because they attract better prices. Apart from the very 
limited introduction of improved exotic dairy cows in some peri-urban areas, there have been no 
major interventions in Somalia on breed improvement.
=== PAGE 79 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 59 
 
 
 
Experiences from similar countries such as Ethiopia, however, shows that the extensive pastoral 
production systems in challenging arid and semi-arid lands pose a big challenge for the feasibility 
and adaptability of improved exotic breeds and hybrids. In fact, centralized genetic improvement 
schemes, through importation of improved breeds in the form of live animals, semen, or embryos 
and crossbreeding have not succeeded much and, even if successful in the near term, sustainability 
remained a big challenge. Experiences in neighbouring countries clearly demonstrate the benefits of 
such community-based breeding programs. In Ethiopia the community-based breeding program 
(CBBP) for small ruminants has been piloted by the International Centre for Agricultural Research in 
Dry Areas (ICARDA) with notable achievements so far, to include: (i) higher animals’ productivity, i.e 
more births, better growth and reduced mortality, ii) increased income (20 percent on average) 
among participating households, implying major livelihood improvements, and (iii) increased meat 
and milk production and consumption in participating communities, implying improved nutrition 
(ESGPIP 2008; Haile et al. 2019). As a result, the Government of Ethiopia included CBBP as one of its 
key livestock breed improvement strategies in its national Livestock Master Plan, which builds on 
existing traditional practices for the selection of best performing local breeds and broad community 
participation through the entire stages of selection, production, promotion and marketing. 
Therefore, the traditional, community-based breeding system should remain the main core 
strategy in Somalia to improve productivity while maintaining adaptive traits, versus a centralized 
and exotic breeds-based breeding program. Cross-breeding with foreign semen for cattle, however, 
is recommended to improve milk and meat yields, and there is already a recently-established private 
company (Somali Bovine Genetics or SBG) offering such services. An animal genetic  resource 
strategy is currently under preparation by the MoLFR, with plans for all livestock stakeholders to 
contribute to and validate it. 
Despite the many daunting constraints listed above, strong opportunities exist for reviving and 
expanding the live animal trade from its peak years of 2015-2016. There is strong potential for 
improving the quality and resilience of Somali livestock, and its offtake, but without targeting or 
fostering increases in the animal population, because of the limited carrying capacity of the 
degraded rangelands and the broader environmental challenges discussed elsewhere. Moreover, 
there is an existing and growing demand for freshly slaughtered meat products from grass-fed 
livestock in both local and international markets. Somalia's strategic location and proximity to the 
Gulf countries also can be better leveraged to supply much of the large demand in such markets now 
satisfied by imports from elsewhere for live animals (and their products). 
Livestock producers with large number of animals (mainly goat and sheep) should raise their 
offtake to between 10-20 % of their stock, and either save the income generated into a reserve 
fund for emergencies or utilize it to fund their children’s education, etc. As of now, those 
pastoralists may run the risk of losing most (if not all) of their livestock stocks when a severe drought 
strikes and they will become destitute because they don’t have not established a safety net to fall 
back. There will be also the added advantage of reducing competition to limited pasture and 
environmental degradation. Almost all Somalis who attended the consultations workshops in 
October-November 2018 [and in April 2019] were in favour of this innovative approach and 
recommend that awareness of pastoralist be raised through media so that the pastoralist start 
changing their old habit of rearing livestock.
=== PAGE 80 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 60 
 
 
29 F 
 
Box 5.1 – Proposed Aerial and Ground Livestock Population Survey 
 
The aerial component of the 1975 census comprised the use of rear seat observers counting animals from 
flying sample transects, but technology has changed significantly since then. The FAO, with initial input from 
the World Bank, worked on the terms of reference of an international consultant, who developed the 
methodology to conduct a livestock population survey for Somalia. The proposed design, to cost in the $1.2 
million-$2million range, conforms to current FAOs guidelines and best practices, while considering also the 
lessons learned from the 2008/09 Trial Survey, which was conducted in Jowhar (only partially because of 
insecurity), Borama and Oodweyn districts, and which showed wide variation in accuracy. Its methodology, 
coverage, and timing (likely in 2020 to allow sufficient time for mobilizing the necessary funding from donors) 
is currently under consideration by the MoLFR. 
Among the key features of the new proposed methodology are that: 
 
An SRF with transect spacing 20 km and sample size of 5 percent be conducted over the whole of Somalia 
using the new ‘oblique-camera-count’ (SRF/OCC) method;
 
flights be conducted at 1,200-3,000 meters above-ground-level for security reasons and requiring two 
months of flying;
 
interpretation of the imagery will require a further four-five months;
 
livestock estimates be determined using the standard Jolly II analysis for SRF data and plotted onto a 20 
km national grid;
 
the aerial survey be accompanied by a ground component, with a simplified questionnaire using a 
stratified sampling approach to also collect information on natality, mortality, offtake and other 
production-related aspects; and
 
a calibration testing flying at these altitudes be carried out in trials in three districts in three different 
regions in Somalia.
Source: FAO-Somalia 
 
 
In ensuring water security for people and livestock, boreholes can play an important role, but they 
are also associated with environmental degradation. The yield of groundwater-fed boreholes is less 
vulnerable to short-term fluctuation in rainfall than other sources such as berkads, open dams, 
shallow hand-dug wells, and springs. This makes boreholes an important source of water in times of 
severe drought especially for humanitarian response and particularly in non-riverine regions of 
Somalia. However, heightened pressures on pasture around these boreholes during drought events 
can cause long-term damage to the surrounding rangeland, creating so-called ‘sacrifice zones’. Deep 
boreholes are also not a cost-effective solution for watering livestock and for increasing crop 
productivity. The costs for drilling and equipping them are high, ranging between US$0.5 million and 
US$1 million due to a combination of physical, market, and security conditions, 30 and furthermore 
their operation and maintenance costs are much higher than from shallow water sources and 
challenges related to water quality (for example, high salinity). 
Based on recent project experience, ample low-cost opportunities exist to enhance rural 
communities’ access to water across Somalia’s dry lands by deploying small-scale water harvesting 
and storage technologies and without exacerbating conflict. Wadis in northern Somalia, and in 
 
 
30 UNICEF Somalia, personal communication.
=== PAGE 81 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 61 
 
 
30 F 
 
areas other than the Juba and Shabelle valleys, are ephemeral with water flowing for very short 
periods during the seasonal rains. Following seasonal rains, water infiltrates into shallow aquifers 
that last for only a few months of the year. Around these shallow aquifers, there is a small but 
growing horticultural production base selling vegetables to urban areas and improving rural 
incomes. 
Pilot projects in Puntland and Somaliland, including the World Bank-financed Water for 
Agropastoral Livelihoods Pilot Project (WALP, P152024), have demonstrated that water harvesting 
and storage in these dry lands can be increased through investment in small dams such as sand 
dams, subsurface dams, and infiltration galleries. 31 Sand dams, in particular, are experiencing a 
renovated interest because of their relative simplicity and their potential in enhancing the resilience 
of marginal dry-land environments. Studying the impacts of sand dams in Kenya on vegetation 
biomass, Ryan and Elsner (2016) concluded that sand dams hold strong scope to improve the 
adaptive capacity of dry lands by helping sustain vegetation biomass during drought periods. 
Increased water availability supported by improved vegetation biomass and soil management means 
effective support for agricultural activities and food production, including for livestock watering and 
nutrition, in the arid and semi-arid zones of the country. 
While animal health-, nutrition-, and export-specific recommendations are further dealt in the two 
following sections, priority interventions necessary (but not sufficient) to address the constraints 
mentioned above and to realize the main goal(s) of increasing the subsector’s productivity (and 
exports) are to: 
1. Improve communications within the sector regarding sector policies, regulations and domestic 
and export requirements and ensure processes are in place and resources are available to assist 
stakeholders in complying with these requirements; 
2. Strengthen public extension services on health and animal husbandry to address rural needs 
(social, economic and environmental) for diversification, resilience, sustainability; 
3. Incentivize the private sector to rehabilitate pre-war holding/resting grounds in strategic 
locations along both domestic and export trade routes, where veterinary services, fodder and 
water can be provided; 
4. Rehabilitate and expand pre-war critical public livestock watering-related infrastructure (mainly 
sand dams, open dams, and reservoirs or berkads for rainfall harvesting, but also shallow hand- 
dug wells and springs) and livestock transport-related rural and main roads, the former to be 
managed by communities and the private sector; 
5. Gather a critical set of nationwide data and information, including via a comprehensive, reliable 
census/survey of animal population and production, and feed and fodder inventory and 
balance. 
 
In the medium-term, other recommended interventions include: 
 
Supporting private sector investment in modern and resilient production systems that reduce 
current vulnerabilities and offer better opportunities in international livestock markets;
 
Developing a strategy to strengthen availability of natural feed on rangelands and to expand 
greatly availability of quality commercial fodder;
 
Improving local breeds’ production and productivity via scientific and community-based 
innovative programs such as: participatory genotype characterization through phenotypic 
features, training of communities on simple breeding concepts and improved husbandry

5.2 
Environmental Degradation and Natural Resource Management 
The current large livestock population compared to historical pre-war levels has been putting a 
major strain on increasingly limited natural resources such as rangeland and water, particularly in 
the northern, central and southern rangelands. Its impact also needs to be assessed in light of 
competing resource uses, for example, agriculture production and water for human consumption 
and other sources of degradation such as charcoal production, wood collection and increasing 
frequency and severity of droughts. 
Trade bans may have some negative environmental effects, by keeping the livestock that would 
have been taken off the rangelands on increasingly stressed land, and by vulnerable livestock 
keepers resorting to other activities to meet their needs, including charcoal burning for illegal 
exports. 32 Not only are producers forced to retain animals, but the drop in their value may lead to 
producers having to resort to destructive coping measures in order to maintain an income and 
livelihood. 
The potential negative environmental impacts, particularly in peri-urban zones, of intensive zero- 
grazing or semi-intensified milk production systems should be better understood and strategies 
should be developed to mitigate these impacts. According to a recent study (USAID, 2017), for 
instance, a high concentration of dairy animals (about 12,000 to 15,000) is kept on what have 
effectively become permanent pastures, causing gradual environmental degradation in the area. 
Furthermore, if the use of chemicals by tanning factories remains unregulated, processing of hides 
and skins to higher stages such as wet blue may also cause adverse environmental impacts, which 
should be well understood and mitigated. 
Improving the natural rangelands resource base is critical to sustaining the production and 
productivity of livestock, in parallel with improving livestock productivity and value addition. 
There is a pressing need to clarify land tenure arrangements; reach consensus over policies and their 
implementation with local communities; improve communities’ capacity to manage their natural 
resources in a sustainable manner; and, specifically for the livestock sector, support rangeland 
rehabilitation with technical assistance and public investments for intensified soil and water 
conservation, reforestation and afforestation, and the reseeding of pastures. Improved community- 
level water management is also key in improving resilience against droughts, through the protection 
 
 
32 However, export volumes trends show that declines in export numbers following export bans are temporary (as alternative trade 
outlets are found), so negative impacts would be experienced mostly in the short-term.
=== PAGE 83 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 63 
 
 
 
of livelihoods and assets (UNICEF, Safe the Children, 2017). Somaliland and Puntland are engaged in 
a number of proactive initiatives, often with support from development partners, to address 
widespread communal rangelands degradation, including investments at grassroots levels in 
establishing and upgrading community-managed rangelands reserves. The impact and sustainability 
of these initiatives should be assessed and strategies for upscaling of successful practices should be 
developed. 
Many stakeholders recommend re-establishing the National Rangeland Agency. However, 
management of rangelands through effective community and producers’ associations may well be a 
more effective alternative, especially considering that the government has no effective oversight in 
all the grazing hinterlands. Participatory land use planning and enforcement, including communal 
control over the expansion of enclosures, are also priorities that need to be agreed and enacted 
upon. 
 
5.3 Climate Change Vulnerability, Adaptation and Mitigation 
There is still no consensus in the scientific literature over whether the Horn of Africa will 
experience more or less rainfall, but all studies and projections by climate scientists agree on a 
warmer future, with increased variability and frequency of extreme rainfall events, with related 
cycles of intensified floods and droughts. Among 167 countries ranked for overall vulnerability to 
climate change, the Center for Global Development places Somalia at the top of the list, at number 5 
for  most  vulnerable  to  extreme  weather  risks  in  2015  (Wheeler  2011). 32FTherefore,  regardless  of 
whether climate change will result in absolute wetting or drying, large scale investment in watershed 
management and infrastructures that would mitigate the impact of extreme cycles of rainfall, floods 
and drought will be critical for the resilience of Somali livelihoods. A recent analysis of prehistorical 
trends suggests that climate conditions in the region will become even drier, more erratic, and more 
extreme than in recent decades and thus less favourable to crops, livestock, fisheries, and forestry- 
based livelihood systems, but most global and regional climate models predict higher rainfall rather 
than drier conditions, although the absolute value of projected changes vary greatly across models. 
Climate change, moreover, is likely to affect different states differently, may also change the 
rainfall intensity of different seasons, and even if wetting would provide benefits to the 
agricultural sectors, temperature increases may well offset any such benefits. In the Lower Jubba, a 
recent study projected a decreasing trend in rainfall through 2030, followed by an increase in rainfall 
in the 2050 and 2070 scenarios (Ogallo et al. 2018).33 F           Another recent study pointed out that projected 
changes in precipitation are most uncertain across the Greater Horn of Africa due to local factors, 
such as complex terrain, large inland water bodies, land heterogeneity, and their interactions with 
large-scale climate forcing mechanisms consequently result in diverse spatial rainfall patterns across 
the region. Still, this second study also points to a wetting signal over Somalia under 1.5°C -2°C 
global warming scenarios (Osima et al. 2018). A third study found that most models agree on the 
projected increase in rainfall during October-to-December over Somalia (Endris  et al. 2018). 
FOther 
studies, which project a scattered pattern of intensified rainfall over East Africa in terms of locations, 
frequency, duration, and intensity, also point to this change occurring in Somalia during its short 
Deyr rainy season (Han et. al. 2019). 
The consequences of such climatic changes on Somalia’s already fragile ecosystems and for Somali 
livelihoods that depend on them are dire. Higher air temperatures will increase evapotranspiration 
and more variable and extreme rainfall on already barren soils will reduce spatial infiltration and
=== PAGE 84 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 64 
 
 
 
facilitate more runoff and erosion, resulting in less groundwater recharge and surface water for 
plant growth. Other likely impacts include reduction of vegetation for grazing and more variable 
water availability, with grave impacts on livestock herding and related livelihoods. Rising sea 
temperatures and acidification will also blunt fish stocks and change their distribution, potentially 
undermining a valuable resource such as fish waste for animal feed. 
Climate change is coming on top of the threats from severe deforestation, other environmental 
degradation of the rangelands from poorly regulated human activity including overgrazing and 
enclosures, and erratic and diminishing water flows in its major rivers due to much expanded use 
of water in their upstream basins in the Ethiopian highlands. The combination of these factors 
poses a serious threat to the long-term viability of agriculture in Somalia, including its livestock 
sector. They also pose another huge and unprecedented challenge to the country’s weak 
institutions, limited public capacity, and private investors. In the absence of substantial progress in 
adaptation to make agricultural systems, including livestock ones, more climate resilient, there is 
little scope for economic recovery. In such a scenario, the United Kingdom’s Met Office Hadley 
Centre for Climate Science and Services predicts that Somalia’s vulnerability to food insecurity will 
grow by 27 percent and 38 percent by 2050 and 2080, respectively. 
In the short-term, Somalia can strengthen the predictive capability for drought and cyclone of the 
early warning system provided by SWALIM and FSNAU and ensure preparedness for timely and 
targeted emergency responses to shocks. In the medium-term, widespread water management 
planning and investment in rainwater and water runoff catchment infrastructure are essential, at 
individual household level as well as with large-scale pans, reservoirs and dams without losing too 
much to evaporation. Traditionally, catchments for water storage have been dug; however, with 
expected dramatic climatic shifts, widespread management is needed to take advantage of either 
additional or reduced water supply. Otherwise, soil erosion and gully formation caused by flooding 
and erratic rainfall, which is already taking a toll on Somali agriculture, including the livestock sector, 
will continue to increase. Beyond planning, expanded management requires strong institutions, 
infrastructure development, market access, and skills and technology development. 
While many traditional practices are already in place to cope with climate variability, they need to 
be strengthened to cope with increasing risks due to climate change. These include moving 
livestock and families, keeping diverse animal species, creating reciprocal arrangements with other 
pastoralist groups for access to pasture and water, developing water-conservation techniques, 
observing and disseminating early-warning signs of impending drought, and practicing alternative 
livelihoods. As the country’s institutions become stronger, it is also important to integrate traditional 
practices with government policy. Beyond resilience, there is also a need to strengthen the  
country’s livestock emergency response capabilities, for which the Livestock Emergency Guidelines 
and Standards (LEGS) offers guidance through a best practice handbook and an evaluation tool. 
Somalia’s Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) to the UNFCCC outlines intended project 
areas necessary to adapt to climate change, with some measures also for mitigation. The intended 
project areas are: 
 
Sustainable land management, to build resilient rural livelihoods and enable national food 
security; 
 
Integrated water resources management, to ensure access for vulnerable communities and 
sectors; 
 
Reducing risks among vulnerable populations from natural disasters;
=== PAGE 85 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 65 
 
 
 
 
UN Joint Programme on Sustainable Charcoal Production and Alternative Livelihoods 
(PROSCAL), and promoting charcoal production from Prosopis and replacing related livelihoods 
with crop production; 
 
Rehabilitation of Fanoole Hydro-Electric Dam and associated irrigation infrastructure; 
 
Domestication of indigenous and introduction of new economically important plant species; 
 
Up scaling the Use of Solar Energy; and 
 
Marine and Coastal Environmental Governance and Management of Somalia 
When actively promoting livestock development, including promoting a commercial feed value 
chain, opportunities should be considered for reducing GHG emissions, especially methane gas 
(CH4), although only about 62.92 MT CO2 or less than .12 percent of total global emissions in 2015 
originated from Somalia, 95 percent of which came from agriculture (including livestock), forestry 
and other land use (AFOLU). Somalia submitted its first National Communication to the UNFCCC at 
the start of 2019. While Somalia is not obliged under the Climate Change Convention to reduce its 
GHG emissions, the country has opportunities to ensure sustainable development and limited 
emissions compared to a business as usual scenario. 
Most of the emissions in Somalia in agriculture are coming from livestock in the form of enteric 
fermentation and animal wastes and from the fodder it consumes. Small ruminants’ production for 
meat represents between 7 and 10 percent of the sector emissions globally and emissions from the 
production, processing and transport of feed actually account for about 45 percent of sector 
emissions. Mitigation will require a holistic approach by integrating animal manure waste 
management systems and harnessing biogas capture--which can be used for lighting and cooking in 
households. Mitigation options such as agroforestry and grassland and pasture management can 
increase soil carbon sequestration and offset emissions from livestock. Furthermore, use of solar and 
wind water pumps for irrigation and watering of livestock can replace use of diesel and petrol 
generations, and the use of solar energy can improve on farm productivity. 
Strengthening feed and fodder systems could mitigate the risks related to dwindling natural 
resources and reduce livestock losses during future drought episodes. Emissions can be reduced by 
adopting climate-smart fodder production systems that ensures feed nutritional value is improved 
by integrating leguminous forages and more efficient use of crop residues through processing. The 
recommendation of promoting commercial fodder production (offered in Chapter 5) and other more 
radical ones, including to move towards zero grazing, with their associated impact on the 
environment and contribution to greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, must be balanced by the fact  
that mobile livestock management is still regarded by experts as one of the best forms of land-use in 
arid sub-Saharan countries such as Somalia. This is despite that nomadic pastoralism and its 
associated low-input low-output production system are becoming increasingly difficult in a country 
with a growing population, rapid urbanization, weak governance and in a warming world. Thus, the 
impact on GHG of commercial feed production compared to potential benefits in other 
environmental aspects needs to be researched using local emission data and calculations. Caution 
should be exercised, moreover, when proposing the development of feed from fish products, to 
ensure this does not originate from illegal and unsustainable fishing practices. Rehabilitating 
Somalia’s forests and effective rangeland management can also be a crucial GHG emission mitigation 
option as a carbon sink as well as blunting the impact of extreme weather events.
=== PAGE 86 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 66 
 
 
 
5.4 Land Tenure 
Land tenure security is a fundamental prerequisite to long-term sustainable development, as 
private investments in infrastructure and the development of rangelands, food and fodder 
production, enclosures, etc. are all conditional on the security of land ownership and user rights. 
Current unclear tenure rights foster overgrazing, lack of action to combat soil erosion and gully 
formation, and the expansion of private enclosures on communal rangelands. Women, furthermore, 
are particularly affected by insecurity of tenure, reflecting complex dynamics extending from the 
interaction of multiple legal systems and Somali socio-cultural norms. The Provisional Constitution, 
however, guarantees the rights of every citizen to own land provided that it is managed in an 
equitable, efficient, productive and sustainable manner and the FGS is in the process of drafting a 
new land policy that guarantees access to all its citizens, including women and minorities. 
Under Somali pastoral traditional norms, local communities collectively use and share rangelands 
and forests. However, grazing disputes leading to fighting between neighbouring pastoral clans have 
become more common since the collapse of the pre–civil war government in all parts of Somalia, 
including at the borders with Ethiopia and Kenya (see Box 5.2). In general, clashes involving rival clan 
militias that started from communal disputes remain the single most common form of armed 
conflicts in Somalia, comprising about 35-40 percent of total security occurrence per month. These 
conflicts are often resolved by local clan elders and religious leaders, however, with local district or 
regional authorities (including neighbouring authorities in Ethiopia and Kenya) intervening only in 
the case of major interclan fighting (World Bank and FAO, 2018). 
Box 5.2. Addressing Conflict Risks and the Needs of Nomadic Pastoralists 
 
In Somalia, as elsewhere in the Horn of Africa, localized conflicts between farmers and herders, and between 
different pastoralist groups, frequently revolve around issues of contested land use, grazing rights, and 
insecure access to water and pasture. Scarce and under increasing pressure, these resources must be shared 
with rural farming communities and the needs of growing urban centres. 
Expansion in recent decades of private enclosures on traditionally open communal rangelands, especially along 
livestock migration routes, increasingly jeopardizes the mobility of pastoralist communities, thereby 
weakening their capacity to cope with adverse climate conditions. Existing tensions and conflict risks are 
amplified during extended dry periods—the frequency and intensity of which have increased in recent 
decades—when pastoralist livelihoods become particularly precarious. In the absence of provisions to assist 
pastoralists to safeguard their capital stock during emergencies and otherwise recover from the shock, 
resulting economic insecurity and deprivation increases the risk of wider violence and social breakdown. 
Moreover, political and socioeconomic marginalization of pastoralist communities and policies that either 
neglects or undermines traditional governance and arbitration mechanisms have, over time, weakened 
resource management and conflict resolution capacities among rural communities. 
The design of future interventions to support delivery of services to rural communities in Somalia’s 
marginalized dry-land areas should emphasize a learn-by-doing approach to generate new understanding 
about (a) the current dynamics of nomadic pastoralist systems in Somalia; (b) the specific needs of pastoral 
communities, and their relationships with other livelihood groups including landed farming and agro- 
pastoralists communities; (c) reforms of regulations and land tenure rights relating to access to pasture land 
and water for communities, especially nomadic pastoralists; and (d) mechanisms needed to ensure emergency 
access for pastoralists to water and pasture and fodder during droughts. 
These lessons should inform downstream interventions designed to mitigate drivers of vulnerability and 
conflict, build trust within communities, strengthen resilience, and enhance participation of pastoralists in 
consultative and decision-making processes that would support customary approaches and traditional 
governance systems for managing competition over natural resources and conflict resolution. 
Source: World Bank, Somalia - Water for Agro-pastoral Productivity and Resilience Project (P167826), under preparation.
=== PAGE 87 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 67 
 
 
37F 
 
In Al-Shabaab-controlled areas in the southern regions, their harsh rule has many detrimental 
effects on rural, including pastoral, communities, threatening the lives of summarily judged 
sinners, confiscating assets, performing abductions, forcing marriages and instilling a culture of 
fear and mistrust in the people, according to the findings of a study commissioned by the United 
Nations in Somalia (UNSOM XXXX). Al-Shabab’s rule has also negatively affected the overall 
economy, trade, security, logistics and religious life, as well as community participation in peace 
initiatives. As government becomes stronger and cleaner, more transparent and equitable, effective 
community responses to the current security challenges can also be expected. 
Efforts to improve land tenure management should therefore aim to: 
1. Strengthen the Water and Land Management Section of the Forestry and Range Department in 
MoLFR; 
2. Create formalized land commission units consisting of elders and religious leaders from various 
communities, already engaged in land conflict resolutions, of civil society engaged in conflict 
resolution, and of relevant government officials. 
3. Reach consensus over policies and their implementation with local communities, tailoring these 
to the specific regions, being conscious of the level of insecurity and government control; 
4. Promote equitable access in practice to land rights, as guaranteed in existing laws, the 
Provisional Constitution and the expected land policy; 
5. Ensure secure pastoral migration routes, including along the riverine areas and cross-border, to 
enhance livestock mobility, which is key to its resilience even during the dry season and at very 
harsh times. 
6. Support participatory land use planning; 
7. Institute and support a process of land tenure formalization, particularly in relatively stable 
areas, by issuing titles for some cultivated farmland (and demarcating urban property 
boundaries). 
 
5.5 
Recovering and fostering live animals’ exports 
Somalia’s close proximity to various export markets and favourable consumer preferences in the 
Gulf markets provide Somalia with a competitive advantage that can support growth of exports of 
live animals, but also chilled meat and other livestock products. To exploit this potential advantage, 
however, Somalia needs to address successfully the vexing issue of the credibility of its health 
certifications for exports, and to diversify and reduce both its product concentration and heavy 
reliance on a few export markets, which makes the live-animal export sector very vulnerable to 
events in Saudi Arabia for shoats, Yemen and Oman for cattle and shoats, Kenya for cattle, and Egypt 
for camels (Godiah et al. 2015). Moreover, camel and especially cattle overland exports  to Kenya  
are expected to continue, as Kenya is challenged to meet its domestic beef meat demand and 
informal exports of animals from Somalia, as well as from Tanzania and Ethiopia, help Kenya meet 
this demand (Aklilu, Little, Mahmoud, & McPeak, 2013). 
A deeper understanding of the dynamics and decision-making processes behind the recurring 
import bans by Saudi Arabia and the UAE, however, is required. These include who  takes  
decisions, how they are communicated and enforced, how they impact on the price of various 
livestock types, how much of the market share is lost and to whom after taking account of the 
redirection of exports through third countries. 33 
 
 
33 Such studies would benefit from support and expertise of the World Organization for Animal Health (OIE), which has an 
international mandate as a regulatory body for animal health and disease declaration.
=== PAGE 88 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 68 
 
 
 
The ban on livestock imports has highlighted various issues that must be addressed, namely: 
1. Reassessing the relationship between public and private veterinary services at quarantine 
stations and the roles and responsibilities of each stakeholder, in regard to (i) laboratory testing 
and reporting and health certifications at export points, (ii) mechanisms for government 
oversight and enforcement of relevant regulatory measures, and (iii) current lack of 
government accreditation of foreign veterinarians who deliver services in the quarantine 
stations; 
2. Using a unified Animal Health Certificate issued only by FGS public veterinarians, as agreed 
among the FGS and the FMs, as mandated by the Veterinary Law Code, and also as recently 
demanded by Saudi Arabia, which was the main destination market for Somali livestock until its 
December 2016 import ban; 
3. Analyzing the cost of regulatory compliance and enforcement (for example the three-week 
quarantine and health certificate emission) for both public and private sector players and how 
that cost can be distributed along the value chain with minimal upstream impact towards 
producers; 
4. Mainstream standard methods and procedures (SMPs) for export quarantines, including for 
pre- and post-phases, that IGAD and AU-IBAR developed and validated with the participation of 
the IGAD member states. 
 
Other recommendations for the medium- and long-terms are: 
 
Understanding the targeted export markets for livestock (and meat) and their characteristics, 
preferences, and perceptions around the quality of Somalia’s exports, their competitiveness 
and opportunity for volumes or value optimization, as well as the cost/benefit of entry into new 
markets, so as formulate evidence-based effective marketing strategies; 
 
Analyzing the various existing regional trade channels, including cross-border trade; 
 
Analyzing the equity implications of promoting expansion of the live animals’ trade; 
 
Elaborating a specific livestock export strategy focused on competitive advantage and best 
ways to optimize strategic proximity to Gulf States’ and other international markets, especially 
in Southeast and East Asia, including via the establishment of FMD-free zones as suggested in 
the OIE Animal Health Terrestrial Code, Section 5 covering trade measures and import/export 
procedures; 
 
Understanding the differences and potential synergies between the commercial and sacrificial 
live animal export value chains and how to optimize benefits for both. 
 
Adopting regulations that can ensure traceability of live animals (and other products) exported. 
 
5.6 
Improving and expanding animal health services 
While the private sector and LPAs accredited by the Ministry of Livestock Forestry and Range of 
the Federal Government of Somalia and funded by development partners have provided most 
critical veterinary services, such efforts remain insufficient to address the serious risk of animal 
diseases and consequent import bans. Animal diseases, especially those transboundary in nature, 
are common in Somalia and in neighbouring countries, and are one of the major concerns for the 
production and marketing of live animals. 
The 2016 National Veterinary Law no. 21, which updated the preceding Law no. 20 of 1967 
provides sector stakeholders with the policy and legal environment to ensure the effective 
prevention and control of animal diseases, including, and especially for, those which are of

transboundary nature (see Box 3.3 in Chapter 3). 34 In addition to animal health strategies adopted

diagnostic services, field clinics, vaccination, emergency investigation, and treatment teams at

government surveillance and oversight on animal health and certification processes; 
2. Apply the OIE-PVS Pathway Tool to assess and monitor the performance of veterinary services, 
in all four components: (i) human, physical and financial resources to attract resources and 
retain professionals with technical and leadership skills; (ii) technical authority and capability to 
address current and new issues including prevention and control of biological disasters based 
on scientific principles; (iii) sustained interaction with interested parties in order to stay on 
course and carry out relevant joint programmes and services; and (iv) ability to access markets 
through compliance with existing standards and the implementation of new disciplines such as 
the harmonisation of standards, equivalence and zoning. 35 
3. Establishment at the national level an Epidemiological Data Management Unit (EDMU) for 
surveillance and recording disease information and outbreaks reports (with district focal points 
to be established for reporting disease outbreaks and incidents to EDMU) and for conducting 
epidemiological studies. 
4. Gather and mainstream data on livestock health, antimicrobial drug use, and prevalence of 
antimicrobial resistance in animals and animal products in the OIE/AU-IBAR managed 
information systems and within the framework of the AU-IBAR sponsored One Health 
Framework;

The OIE Global Animal Welfare Strategy was developed from lessons learned from actions taken at national 
and regional level and aims to be a source of ongoing guidance for the OIE’s activities in this area. Adopted in 
2017 by all Member Countries, it was developed with the objective of achieving: “A world where the welfare of 
animals is respected, promoted and advanced, in ways that complement the pursuit of animal health, human 
well-being, socio-economic development and environmental sustainability”. 
According to the OIE Terrestrial Code, animal welfare means ‘the physical and mental state of an animal in 
relation to the conditions in which it lives and dies.” The guiding principles which inform the OIE’s work on the 
welfare of terrestrial animals include the ‘Five Freedoms’, developed in 1965 to capture global societal 
expectations for the conditions animals should experience when under human control. 
Of these five widely recognised freedoms, no. 1, 3, and 4 are those most relevant to the Somali context. 
1. 
freedom from hunger, malnutrition and thirst; 
2. 
freedom from fear and distress; 
3. 
freedom from heat stress or physical discomfort; 
4. 
freedom from pain, injury and disease; and 
5. 
freedom to express normal patterns of behaviour. 
Source: World Organization for Animal Health (OIE). 
 
5. Implement the cooperation elements of the IGAD Animal Health Strategy, including sharing of 
data and coordinating disease prevention, emergency preparedness, and control with 
neighbouring countries. 
 
For the medium- term, the LSDS offers also the following recommendations: 
 
Improve the administrative structure of the Public Veterinary Health Services, and its 
collaboration with private veterinary associations (PVAs), entrepreneurs and producer 
cooperatives;
 
Make qualified manpower operational again in the field and as advisors to the federal and state 
ministries, by recalling, reorganizing, retraining and adequately funding former veterinary 
professionals and livestock experts and technicians;
 
Monitor, prevent and control TADs not only among livestock but also among the wildlife 
population
 
Monitor, control, and educate traders, CAHWs and livestock professional and owners on quality 
and proper use of veterinary drugs to ensure effective treatment and minimize the risk of AMR;
 
Support the development of community-owned and managed health services.
 
5.7 
Animal Welfare 
Animal welfare concerns the humane care and treatment of animals that are raised, transported 
and sold commercially. It is both important per se according to Somali cultural and Islamic beliefs, 
for enhancing animal health and productivity, and as a means to enhance the value of marketed 
animals and their products. Most of animal welfare issues in Somalia occur during loading, 
transporting and unloading of livestock, not because of mistreatment by their handlers but because 
of the inadequate facilities and services that are available, such as sanitation, nutrition, water, and 
veterinary care, in rural areas and along transit routes. Even at the major export ports there are few 
loading and unloading ramps. There are also no standards for maximum transit time during 
transportation. The country also lacks adequate facilities for animals to rest and recuperate, before 
export or domestic marketing in large urban centers. It is therefore essential that strategic resting 
facilities be established along the major trading routes. 
Box 5.3 – Five Freedoms of Animal Welfare
=== PAGE 91 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 71 
 
 
 
Animal welfare issues have not been well studied across Somalia, as it has not been a priority 
largely due to lack of exposure to global animal welfare norms and debate, except in Somaliland, 
where most available documentation on animal welfare comes from. Somaliland’s law dedicated  
to animal welfare, which is very comprehensive and surpasses OIE standards, needs dissemination 
and implementation. There are also some civil society organizations, e.g. the veterinary faculty at 
Golis University and the Somaliland Veterinary Association, amongst others, that deal with animal 
welfare and whose objectives and activities include promotion of community awareness and 
education on animal welfare, advocacy for animal welfare legislation, and mobile veterinary clinical 
intervention. However, no one in the state ministry or among livestock stakeholders has been 
trained on international standards such as the OIE Improved Animal Welfare Program. 
Even though livestock transport trucks are modified specifically for this purpose, a welfare 
assessment at a key market in Somaliland rated it poorly. Inadequate water and feed for animals 
during most of the year was recognised as a weakness, and drought a major threat. Loading facilities 
at the port of Berbera have been upgraded, but handling of animals leaves a lot of room for 
improvement. The overall conclusion from IGAD is that there is an interest to improve animal 
welfare in Somaliland, and where there is a will there is a way. Other parts of Somalia face much 
worse conditions and still needs to start by establishing the will and then by searching for the way to 
ensure better animal welfare. 
 
5.8 Improving and expanding animal nutrition (water, feed and fodder) 
Ensuring year-round affordable good quality feed by enhancing fodder production and feed 
systems development is important to improve animal quality, to mitigate lack of pasture and 
browse at times of seasonal or prolonged severe droughts, and to reducing the pressure of a large 
animal population on increasingly scarce natural resources. The feed subsector, however, has 
always been and is to this day characterized by a widely practiced traditional low-input grazing and 
browsing system throughout Somalia, subject to seasonal shortages of pasture, and very limited 
commercial feed/fodder production. In recent decades, the underdevelopment of this subsector has 
been compounded after the outbreak of the civil war by the breakdown of government institutions 
(such as the defunct pre-war National Range Agency) and traditional management institutions, as 
well as by degradation, deforestation and continuous overgrazing resulting in major soil erosion, 
desertification, and reduction in edible biomass. Other constraints and gaps are insecurity and 
difficult access to some parts of the southcentral rangelands, the uncontrolled expansion of 
enclosures on previously open rangeland, low levels of technical knowledge on fodder production 
and storage, poor access to fodder seeds and other production inputs, absence or inadequate water, 
storage, and transport infrastructure, and unsettled land tenure issues in southern Somalia. 
The 2016-2017 major prolonged droughts that led to high mortality of livestock across the country 
has stoked interests of investors and traders in producing, commercializing and storing improved 
feed and fodder, from grasses, crops residues, and also from cereal grains and oil seeds. The 
nascent commercial forage production and marketing systems, however, are for the moment very 
limited and inadequate. It is also not very well studied, as no state-level quantitative information 
exists on the amount of commercial fodder grown, on the availability and uses of various inputs, and 
on the number and type of actors involved. 
Development opportunities in this subsector can rely on much increased demand for fodder by 
expanding commercial livestock rearing operations (throughout the year), field testing by some 
research institutes (e.g. SATG) of promising better grasses, and existing large-scale commercial
=== PAGE 92 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 72 
 
 
 
crop production in some states of cereals and oil seeds. Furthermore, they can leverage the 
availability of suitable land and water in some states (e.g. South West and Jubaland, but also in 
Somaliland and Puntland where seasonal rivers flow in abundance but drain into the sea without 
their waters being utilized and of some water catchment facilities on the rangelands that only need 
rehabilitation. 
Pastoral destitution is principally driven by feed and water scarcity, as the natural resource base in 
the rangelands is shrinking fast. To address this, and also to enable realistic improvements in 
livestock production, feed resources ought to be considered in the broader development perspective 
and not just as an emergency response to drought, as is the case now. Institutionalization of a feed 
security system is therefore requisite such that the country is aware of its needs, resource 
availability, gaps, and how the gap can be filled within the country, the region or beyond. A feed 
strategy can only be developed following an assessment of the national feed inventory, which 
includes measuring actual and potential feed sources, and of the national feed balance, which 
compares feed sources to feed needs, as illustrated in Box 5.2.36 
 
Box 5.2: National Feed Inventory and Balance

In view of these constraints and opportunities, the subsector would benefit from the following 
priority interventions related to water and fodder, as suggested in recent research (UNICEF, Safe 
the Children, 2017) and in stakeholders’ consultations (see Main Annexes 1-2 and Online Annexes): 
1. Rehabilitate the existing water reservoirs and increase the number of water catchment facilities 
on the rangelands (see Box 5.X); 
2. Conduct a feed and fodder analysis that looks at availability (inventory) and current and 
prospective feed needs (based on reliable estimates expected from the proposed livestock 
population survey); 
3. Study potential production capacity and develop a livestock feed and rangeland strategy; 
4. Analyze feed and fodder value chain linkages with all relevant subsectors, access to production 
areas and markets and transport linkages between them, and consider market support schemes 
to stimulate and facilitate the inclusion of more distant communities in the fodder value chain; 
5. Support technical training on feed and fodder production; 
6. Support private sector and cooperative investment in feed and fodder production; 
7. Re-establish a rangeland management agency and enforce pre-war and/or more recent policies 
and laws (and traditional systems) to rehabilitate and manage the rangelands; 
 
In the medium-term, implementation of the following recommendations would further benefit 
the development of this subsector: 
 
Support integrated production systems, leveraging the complex interlinkages and synergies 
between crop agriculture and (peri-urban) livestock rearing systems; 
 
Develop feed production contract farming, to supply feed to the quarantine and animal holding 
centers and to ensure availability for pastoral communities of fodder during dry 
seasons/drought periods; 
 
Promote drought- resistant fodder varieties that can withstand harsh weather conditions. 
 
Develop a land use strategy, with strong participation and involvement of local communities 
and authorities, in order to minimize conflicts over land tenure and user rights; 
 
Align and harmonize policies and institutional arrangements among various line ministries, as 
animal feed production is at the nexus of crop agriculture, livestock, and environment; 
 
Re-establish a forestry and range training institute, with branches in all states.
