6.0 VALUE ADDITION: CONSTRAINTS AND RECOMMENDATIONS 
 
6.1 Livestock-related Nutrition and Food Security 
Despite widespread livestock ownership, the great majority of Somalis, whether rural or urban, 
have a diet composed more of cereals, sugar and oil complemented by consumption of animal 
products, which provide the most important source of protein. In pastoral zones, households are 
highly dependent on markets, where they purchase cereals (sorghum, rice, and wheat flour), oil and 
sugar – the basics of the pastoral diets. Somalia usually needs to import more than half of its basic 
food, especially rice, wheat flour and sugar from the world market and sorghum and maize from 
Kenya and Ethiopia (though the import requirement changes from year to year, see FSNAU, 2016). 
Being highly dependent on basic food imports, Somalia is very vulnerable to market disruption 
caused by conflict, and also to severe global price hikes in commodities coming in from the world 
market. A flourishing livestock export sector is crucial in financing the substantial annual food 
imports required to ensure food security for many Somalis. Disruptions in exports, most recently as a 
result of lingering effects of the drought and the livestock import ban imposed by Saudi Arabia, 
therefore have negative effects on pastoral households’ food security as they rely on animal sales to 
purchase their food. Poorer households are believed to be particularly affected, especially during 
drought episodes, as they are often less able to continue their commercial activities thereby 
reducing their income. 
Although many pastoral households are highly dependent on markets for their food stuffs, a large 
share of dairy products (fresh milk and ghee) are also for self-consumption. Camel milk and to a 
lesser extent goat milk complement their diet, especially during the wet seasons when milk 
availability peaks. Poor households have less access to milk than middle income and better-off 
households, as their herd sizes are smaller and include fewer lactating camels. Some do, however, 
receive gifts of milk from wealthier neighbors. 
According to FSNAU data, consumption of own meat or milk constitutes on average 10% of the 
diets of poor pastoral households, compared to an average of around 20% for wealthy pastoral 
households. Promoting more varied dairy and by-products of meat production and consumption, 
including from poultry, would have positive nutritional outcomes for the country’s population, 
besides being an additional source of income for producers. Comparing pastoral households in 
Somaliland and Puntland, the former appears to consume more of their own meat and milk, with 
own livestock product consumption of poor households making up around 10% of their diet, against 
around 5% for Puntland’s poor pastoralists. Wealthy households in Somaliland also consume more  
of their own livestock products as compared to Puntland. In the Juba pastoral and Southern-inland 
pastoral livelihood systems, consumption of own meat and milk in family diets is significantly higher: 
up to almost 30% for poor households and just under 40% for wealthy households. 
Furthermore, a recent econometric study of food demand showed that, while the basic staples of 
cereals and oils are income inelastic, the more expensive food categories such as meat/fish and 
dairy are highly income elastic (1.43 and 1.45, respectively; see Hussein et. al 2019). Thus, the 
demand for meat and dairy products is likely to increase well above the expected rise in average per- 
capita income, by 43-45 percent more in fact. Furthermore, increased processing, storage and 
consumption of readily available by-products of meat production, such as offal, would provide 
additional cheap sources of protein for domestic consumers.
=== PAGE 95 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 75 
 
 
40 F 
 
6.2 The Dairy Subsector 
The main constraints that have kept the dairy sector from reaching its potential are both 
institutional and structural, and include most of those affecting the health and welfare of live 
animals. Under the first category, the dairy subsector suffers specifically from lack of regulations  
and from poor or no enforcement of low and outdated pre-war hygiene standards. Under the  
second category, the list of major constraints specific to the dairy subsector include lack of processes 
to preserve milk quality, inefficient marketing channels fraught with excessive intermediaries, the 
poor state of transport infrastructure, rapid spoilage from high temperatures and humidity, lack of 
sufficient quality commercial fodder and the absence of economies of scale. Other constraints 
reported in the literature (World Bank and FAO, 2018; Younan, Gure, & Schulze, 2013) and by 
stakeholders are the low and highly seasonal productivity, due to lack on modern management skills 
and knowledge on animal nutrition and feed supplementation. Poor hygiene and a complete 
absence of cooling system in the camel milk value chain also cause rapid souring of the milk, leading 
to losses in market value of 20-40 percent (Younan, Gure, & Schulze, 2013). 37 Stakeholders also 
highlighted limited disease control and surveillance, low levels of technical knowledge on 
commercial milk production, high transport costs from remote production areas, and, as noted 
above, lack of access to finance. 
In all states, according to studies (e.g. Oxfam 2018, USAID 2017) and to stakeholders, demand for 
fresh milk and other processed dairy products will continue to grow beyond the natural 
population growth, which alone will provide over a million new potential consumers in the next 
decade. According to the most recent Oxfam 2018 and FAO 2019 studies, moreover, most 
consumers prefer fresh raw milk to packaged milk and most buyers are unwilling or again unable to 
pay for processing of any kind, thus processing may not be profitable. The FAO-Investment Centre 
recent study on peri-urban dairy value chains, funded by the World Bank and based on case studies 
conducted by Somali researchers in Hargeisa and Burao in Somaliland, Bosaso and Galkayo in 
Puntland, Mogadishu in Banadir and Kismayo in Jubaland focused on the fresh milk subsector and its 
recommendations for its development have been integrated in the broader list below. 
Dairy-subsector stakeholders, however, see much stronger prospects than the Oxfam and the FAO-
Investment Centre studies for value-addition via processing of milk supported by an appropriate 
public policy and robust private investment. Their view expressed during the October- November 
state-level consultation workshops and during the June 2019 validation workshop in Mogadishu was 
rooted in the current poor and inefficient state of the subsector, the large number  of available milk-
producing animals, the commercialization trend that has already started, and the high, growing and 
more sophisticated domestic demand from increased and still increasing urbanization, higher 
incomes, and diaspora returnees. This view is also buttressed by the success and current expansion 
plans by the three dairy processors in Mogadishu that have been mentioned at the end of Section 
3.6 in chapter 3 and highlighted in Box 6.1, as well as by international comparisons and trade 
statistics, in particular the large bill in recent years for imported dairy products, ranging between 
US$60 million and $82 million, mostly for milk powder and long-life packaged milk (see Fig. 3.6 and 
the last paragraph of section 3.6 in chapter 3 in this report). The recent study by Hussein (2019) that 
found a high-income elasticity of dairy (1.45) also provides confidence in a steady expansion of dairy 
demand, including of quality, safe processed products. 
 
 
37 Shelf life of refrigerated camel milk is about 7 days, after which the milk become sour, though still consumed 
as such.
=== PAGE 96 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 76 
 
 
 
 
Box 6.1 – OOG Dairy’s Processing Success and Expansion Plans 
 
The OOG Dairy company, which produces mostly yogurt, started in July 2016 among family members with $5,000 as seed 
money, with the business later scaled up gradually to a cumulative investment of about $160,000 (including other non- 
family shareholders) as of November 2018. 
OOG Dairy has been buying raw cow milk from the Somali Milk Co. (SoMilk), but only produced by cows not treated with 
antibiotics (as that would not allow yogurt-making), which is a common practice on dairy farms near the Shabelle river 
because tse-tse is endemic in the area. The company also sources cow milk from its own dairy farm, where it built a shed 
housing 10 local-breed cows on 4 ha of land at the outskirts of Mogadishu. The reason for the choice of local breed cows 
(9 of which produce 12 liters/day, with the remaining one 9 liters/day) instead of importing higher yielding Holstein cows 
was better adaptability to local hot/humid climate, resistance to diseases, and minimal treatment needs with antibiotics 
and drugs. Since families buy cow milk and its products mainly for children, minimizing pharmaceutical treatment is a plus 
for marketing. 
The company does its own testing for antibiotics in the milk, as well as for density, fat content, and adulteration (added 
water). It then pasteurizes the milk (at 95 C) which is processed for yogurt through its plant, including heat exchanger, 
fermentation tank, and recently acquired chilling equipment comprised of freezers, chillers, homogenizers and incubators. 
The latter was acquired thanks to a grant from USAID/GEEL (before, it used a cooling tank). 
Current capacity is 800 liters per day of drinkable yogurt in bottles, 200 l/d for classic yogurt in cups, 100 l/d for fermented 
milk (lala/laban in Somali) and 120 kg/d for ice cream. In 2018 it produced 16 tons of yogurt (mostly), including very small 
quantities of fermented milk and ice cream. In June 2019 it also started producing yogurt from camel milk, for which 
marketing is about to start. The company offers two types of yogurt in various flavors (natural, vanilla, mango and 
strawberry), a classic variety in modern plastic cups and the drinkable variety in sturdy plastic bottles of varying sizes, the 
latter having become by mid-2019 its best-selling item. It does not sell directly to the public, but to a distributor that 
places its 25-yogurt packs in supermarket, hotels, small shops, and preschools in and around Mogadishu (as far as Afgoi). 
The company has two small delivery trucks, and its distributor also has its own delivery truck, all with OOG Dairy logos on 
them. 
Revenues exceed variable costs, but no provision for depreciation of assets has been yet made (or accounted for), so 
whether the business is profitable or not is uncertain. Its biggest cost is raw cow milk (around $1 per liter, representing 
40% of total variable cost), followed by electricity ($0.37/Kwh, representing 25%). Laborers and drivers are paid 
$150/month, the finance clerk and the production manager $350/month and the CEO $450/month. Licensing fees are $500 
annually with MLFR and the company also pays $200 annually as a local tax to sell their products in Afgoi. The USAID grant 
allowed both improved quality and the addition of 15 new jobs, for a total by mid-2019 of 31 direct-hire staff and 200 
indirect beneficiaries. 
Since the company believes it has sufficient spare capacity to meet any further growth of demand for yogurts, its next 
expansion plans are focused on adding in 2020 to its current small processing footprint an automated line for the 
production of 1,500 liters/hour extended shelf life (ESL) fresh milk. Such an expansion is expected to require the hiring of 
69 additional workers on a full-time basis. Proper training has been and will continue to be provided to workers who 
operate the facility, to ensure plant and food safety, improve product quality, reduce raw materials’ losses, and increase 
utilization of by-products, and thereby increase the customer base and profitability. 
Among its strengths, Oog Dairy lists the college-level technical and business education of its founders and current top 
managers, the active involvement in its business by its main shareholders, its brand recognition in the local market, and its 
vertical integration with a cow dairy farm. It believes its success has crucially depended on its ability to ensure a regular 
supply of milk and other essential inputs, on its thorough quality checks at different stage of the production process, on 
available cold chain for the distribution of its products, and on its aggressive marketing efforts. Among its opportunities are 
strong, unmet and growing demand for quality milk and for dairy products and availability of suitable land for the planned 
factory expansion, including a milk collection facility. Constraints to further expansion of capacity are limited access to 
capital and availability of trained electricians and mechanics to operate its plant. The major risks it sees are the general 
insecurity in and around Mogadishu, which can easily disrupt the timely distribution of its products, the competition from 
imported dairy products, and shortages of milk supplies. 
Source: Interviews with Oog Dairy’s CEO and Production Manager in November 2018 and June 2019. 
 
A number of knowledge gaps have been identified in the existing body of literature on Somalia’s 
dairy value chain that need to be urgently addressed in order to enable evidence-based decision- 
making regarding the best way forward. This subsector’s development planning thus needs to start 
by improving the understanding of the following knowledge gaps:
=== PAGE 97 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 77 
 
 
 
 
the environmental impact of current practices, with particular focus on peri-urban systems; 
 
data on the number of dairy farmers, lactating animals and productivity, and understanding the 
dynamic between peri-urban and extensive pastoral systems; 
 
a comprehensive nationwide survey of existing micro- and larger enterprises processing milk; 
 
the nature and volume of market demand for fresh milk and other dairy products; 
 
consumers’ preferences and perception of risks and costs (economics) of zoonotic diseases that 
pose a risk to public health, since the emergence of MERS-CoV (a viral respiratory infection 
passed from animals to humans), as an example, is believed to have been driven by the 
development of peri-urban dairy farms; 
 
analysis of existing dairy state-level policies and regulatory frameworks and levels of 
enforcement. 
 
The key challenges identified in the most recent study cited above by the FAO-Investment Centre 
are: 
 
Competitive marketing needs improved business management skills. As competition 
intensifies, dairy farmers’ competitive advantage rests largely on their reputation for freshness, 
safety and quality. With a highly perishable product such as fresh milk, peri-urban farmers have 
to be increasingly skilled at managing price fluctuations and mitigating losses from low prices 
during the wet season. 
 
High cost of electricity prevents the establishment of a cold chain and hinders growth. Dairy 
farmers lack means to utilize renewable energy such as wind or solar systems. There are few 
peri-urban farmers who have their own cold storage as the majority have not been able to 
invest in such facilities and need to be able to sell their highly perishable product fast. 
 
Poor road networks and lack of cold chain transport facilities impact milk quality and lead to 
losses. The peri-urban diary businesses proximity to the city markets is an advantage. 
However, it comes at the cost of an increasing need for intensification during the dry season 
that leads to higher production costs. Moreover, relocating activities further from town 
markets in order to access free grazing is costly in terms of transport difficulties, delays, road 
taxes and blocks that often result in losses. 
 
Lack of financing limits needed investment for drilling to improve access to water. Like with 
other larger investments, many of the farmers lack the capital to be able to drill boreholes or 
provide other water management infrastructure. 
 
The lack of livestock technical extension and knowledge on modern and improved livestock 
management practices reduces productivity. The smaller peri-urban farmers, except the ones 
owned by big companies like Al-Maxruuf, lack basic marketing and food handling skills. In 
addition, livestock-rearing and farm management continues to follow traditional practices, with 
stagnant productivity. 
 
Under-developed fodder markets slow down intensification of dairy farming. Most available 
commercial fodder is from humanitarian emergency stocks or imported from Oman, with the 
subsector vulnerable to supply or price shocks outside of its control and at risk of being 
oligopolistic. The latter risk is increasingly visible, as competition among fodder importing 
companies remains very limited and there is little private to produce fodder locally. 
 
Deficient ability to control animal disease leaves the livestock sector highly vulnerable. There 
are very limited government veterinary services available to dairy farms for preventing and 
controlling disease outbreaks, while the quality of private veterinary clinics remains unclear. 
 
Institutional fragmentation of the sub-sector undermines the effectiveness of governments’ 
and donors’ interventions. There is no sector body such as an association or a dairy board that 
could advocate on behalf of and coordinate the subsector.
=== PAGE 98 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 78 
 
 
 
 
Lack of a clear legal and regulatory framework for dairy food safety poses a public health risk 
and impacts negatively growth potential. No quality assurance mechanisms are put in place 
and incidences of contamination or diseases transmitted through the milk by the animals 
remain high. 
 
Increasing price of peri-urban land prevents outsiders from being able to obtain title and 
invest in the sector. While the use of land is free under agreement between the local clan and 
members of the same, land title is a necessary requirement for the security of the investment in 
infrastructure and other facilities and assets, when land is to be exploited for an extended 
period. 
 
The peri-urban dairy sub-sector has high entry costs that prevent pastoral herders from 
entering this new type of business, which is more often than not in the hands of city 
businessmen owning other ventures, such as hotels and restaurants. Investors in dairy farms 
are mostly better off individuals, with more influence and with considerable financial means or 
access to financing. Therefore, the subsector does not seem benefit lower socio-economic and 
poverty profile groups, including pastoralists. 
 
Despite many challenges, the market-oriented peri–urban camel and cattle production system is 
emerging around many towns in across Somalia, where the sale of raw milk is already a very 
important economic activity. The growth opportunities for milk producers in the peri-urban 
context are based and depend upon: 
 
The remaining availability and accessibility to fertile rangeland in some peri-urban areas allows 
for further expansion of the sub-sector without the need for intensification and the transition 
to zero-grazing livestock systems, however this is context specific; 
 
Use of renewable energy like solar or wind power could help reduce energy costs and allow for 
expansion of cold chain and storage facilities that would improve outputs and economic 
performance; 
 
The availability of camels for sale allows newcomers to enter the business as long as they can 
secure access to peri-urban land and water; 
 
Strong traditional mechanism for management of use and access to land and rangeland prevent 
conflicts and secure investments; 
 
Increasing demand for meat offer new opportunities for diversification of farm income 
 
Improved livestock management and feeding practices that can impact productivity by reducing 
seasonal fluctuations. This requires locally available cheaper and more diversified sources of 
fodder is needed to avert risks of dependence on imports and minimize risks of monopoly; 
 
Professionalization of the camel dairy sector through improved herd management practices 
and improved animal husbandry techniques can positively impact productivity without need for 
expanding herd size. 
 
In addition to improving subsector specific knowledge, this LSDS recognizes the need to address 
the challenges listed above as well as to foster milk processing to develop this important sub- 
sector, by strengthening its organisation and management and drawing private investment for its 
expansion and modernization. This will enhance the subsector’s contribution to the socio-economic 
wellbeing, as well as the nutritional and health status, of the Somali population and enhance inter- 
sectoral links between food security and nutrition interventions. The immediate priority 
interventions proposed for the dairy sub-sector from the literature and the stakeholders are the 
following:
=== PAGE 99 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 79 
 
 
 
1. Improve veterinary and extension services to enable producers to control the spread of animal 
diseases, improve the health of the peri-urban herds, and meet also hygienic standards for milk 
production, handling and transportation and monitor the prevalence of zoonotic diseases; 
2. Strengthen government oversight and auditing of milk production, handling and transportation 
facilities to ensure food safety and quality standards are applied effectively, including by 
establishing quality control centers at milk collection points and markets; 
3. Support the establishment of a Dairy Board to represent peri-urban investors and serve as an 
entry point for discussion and private sector participation in policy dialogue; 
4. Adopt a two pronged approach to the sector’s development: promote higher raw fresh milk 
production and marketing for the rural population but with added public health safeguards (e.g. 
hygienic handling and retail outlets) and processed milk and higher value-added products, 
including yogurt made from camel milk, for urban and diaspora consumers (whose total 
demand currently may be small but set to grow); 
5. Foster widespread adoption, including by facilitating access to credit, of dairy preservation and 
processing techniques, such as cold chain infrastructure, technology, and business 
management—to support expansion of products with a long shelf-life (e.g. extended shelf life 
milk, ultra-pasteurized milk, milk powder, yogurt, ghee, cheese), which can reduce spoilage 
during the wet season when there is oversupply of fresh milk, enhance food safety and higher 
value addition, and reduce dairy imports. 
 
In the medium- and long-term, government, dairy farmers, processors, and the donor community 
should also: 
 
Design interventions in the dairy subsector to link with development plans for fodder, water, 
and transport; 
 
Adopt a gender strategy that protects and promotes the interest of female milk traders, as the 
peri-urban dairy subsector becomes more organized and shifting toward male-dominated 
employment; 
 
Design all development interventions by the government and by external partners to reflect the 
current role of youth in the sub-sector, and support its expansion; 
 
Support the establishment of commercially oriented dairy farmer’s association that can 
organize the value chain actors and improve information sharing and capacity-building; 
 
Maintain current policy of exempting needed agro-processing machinery from customs duties 
and consider other policy incentives such as preferential energy rates or reduced road taxes 
that can stimulate private investment in modernization and cold storage facilities; 
 
Pilot public private partnerships for the establishment of collection points with cold 
storage/cooling facilities using alternative energies to reduce running costs and improve 
economic viability 
 
Collect and publish statistics on dairy sector production and consumption, in addition to 
existing monitoring (by FSNAU) of milk retail prices; and 
 
Explore opportunities for the niche export of processed and powdered camel milk. 
 
6.3 
The Meat Subsector 
The meat value chain is still currently almost exclusively focused on supplying the local market 
(BORESHA, EU, 2018), but with increasing commercialization. Beyond traditional and widespread 
home-based slaughtering and meat production for shoats, commercial meat production for camels 
and cattle and meat trade in Somalia has been increasing, in response to growing domestic demand 
for fresh meat as well as for traditional meat products, such as mugmud and otka (popular dried 
meats in ghee). Open-slab slaughterhouses in some large towns, often owned by local governments 
and managed under often unclear PPP arrangements by private butchers’ associations, supply
=== PAGE 100 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 80 
 
 
41F 
 
carcasses to local butchers who retail their meat cuts directly in their shops or indirectly through a 
network of market sellers, predominantly women. Many small and medium-size  towns, 
furthermore, have no slaughterhouses at all. 
Most such slaughterhouses (and meat markets), however, are mostly unhygienic, in various states 
of disrepair, and lack drainage systems. In most cases, meat is not inspected because state and local 
governments’ veterinary departments either lack a legal framework to enforce (though meat 
hygiene legislation has been enacted in Somaliland and Puntland and the federal government is in 
the process of adopting the same, with assistance from the FAO and the EU), or they are unable to 
enforce regulations because of lack of meat inspectors. The poor hygiene conditions in most meat 
markets leave meat vulnerable to contamination. In addition to the lack or poor hygienic conditions 
of slaughterhouses and meat markets, other major challenges related to past failed export ventures 
of chilled meat were the high transport costs for export by air and the difficulty in meeting 
international standards. Only SoMeat, a private company equipped with two modern and hygienic 
slaughterhouses meeting international standards at the outskirts of Mogadishu, is exporting by air 
chilled and by sea frozen beef carcasses to Oman, while exploring also other markets, including 
Bahrain. 
Promoting meat production and expanding and upgrading local processing and value addition is 
not only needed to meet the growing domestic demand, but it would also contribute to improved 
food safety, to local revenue generation, and to employment creation, especially in urban and 
peri-urban areas, and including for women who dominate its domestic retail trade. Prospects can 
rely on growing domestic and regional markets for quality “grass-fed” meat from free-range 
livestock, and on the country’s proximity with Gulf states and other North-African (such as Egypt) 
and West Asian (such as Iran) markets. It should focus on building on existing positive experiences, 
such as the Burao slaughterhouse renovated under a public-private partnership (PPP) tripartite 
arrangement involving the municipality, which owns the facility, a  private  firm,  and  the 
community. 38 Similar developments under PPP arrangements are under way in Kismayo and Bosaso 
slaughterhouses. A subsector development strategy should also explore opportunities for expanding 
the marketing of traditional home-cured (or sun-dried) meat products, such as mugmud and otka, 
both within the country and outside. 
Only scattered and anecdotal statistics on meat production and consumption and information on 
stakeholders exist in Somalia, from local meat production facilities for the domestic market to 
commercial abattoirs for export. However, three meat processing companies  already  operate in 
the country. The oldest, the Somali Meat Co. (SoMeat) in Mogadishu has two modern 
slaughterhouses and exports frozen cattle carcasses to Oman (and in small quantities also to Qatar) 
in refrigerated containers, with plans to invest in the machinery required for producing and 
exporting cuts. The other two operations on the outskirts of Hargeisa (Hilibsan) and Galkayo (Beira 
Food Factory), processing and selling meat in the local markets in a hygienic way, have been recently 
established but have plans to expand, and in the case of Beira Food Factory also to can meat for  
both domestic consumption and export. There is also opportunity to sell both fresh and chilled meat 
to AMISOM troops in Mogadishu and other southcentral locations, which are currently unexploited 
mainly due to food safety concerns for local meat preparation, storage and transport. 
 
 
38 After its rehabilitation the Burao slaughterhouse has been handed over in 2018 to the private sector under a PPP agreement and soon 
will start exporting chilled meat to Malaysia. A similar arrangement has been agreed for rehabilitation and operation of the Kismayo 
slaughterhouse for camels.

The opportunities for exporting meat to Gulf States and in the broader East African region, where 
demand is projected to increase, have been highlighted in a recent study by the FAO (2017) and in 
other reports by external development agencies. More information on demand of livestock and 
livestock products in four of Somalia’s most important importing Arab countries is presented in table 
6.1. Other countries in the Gulf already importing frozen meat from SoMeat include Oman and 
Qatar, while Yemen remains an unexploited market. Other unexploited regional markets include the 
Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), Uganda, and most importantly Kenya, which is only about
=== PAGE 102 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 82 
 
 
 
70–75 percent self-sufficient in meat and whose import and re-export markets are also positioned to 
grow. 
Nonetheless, meat exports prospects need to be further analyzed, including quality perceptions, 
competitiveness and food safety issues of Somali meat in the Gulf, Middle East, North African, 
West and South-East Asian and regional markets, vis-à-vis other countries like Pakistan whose 
exports have already displaced Somali meat in the UAE market. It also imperative to understand 
better the issue of year-long availability of sufficient supplies to conquer these markets, addressing 
e.g. seasonality, domestic livestock prices, competition with live animal exports. 
Furthermore, an expansion of meat exports beyond Mogadishu (where frozen meat exports in 
refrigerated containers by sea are feasible) may not be cost-effective, due to high air cargo rates 
for chilled meat, except for frozen meat exports from Berbera, the only other container port at  
this time in the country. Any such export-oriented expansion, in any case, would require 
considerable technical and financial support to overcome the reasons for previous high investment 
failure rates, due to poor operational management, livestock supply constraints, and lack of 
compliance with Hazard Analysis Critical Control Point (HACCP) and other international quality and 
food safety standards. 
The LSDS recommends the following priority interventions for the meat subsector’s development: 
1. Approve and implement a Federal Meat Inspection Act, which has also an integral component 
of disease notification and related information; 
2. Support public and private investments and PPP arrangements for the establishment and 
management of modern hygienic slaughterhouses and cold chains in all urban centers, 
including to prevent the congregation of stray dogs and cats around slaughter sites and to 
remove and dispose properly of waste (bones, skulls, hides, skins, etc.) which litter slaughter 
sites and nearby beaches; 
3. Clarify roles and responsibilities of slaughterhouse investors, supervisors, customers under 
PPPs; 
4. Improve integration of traditional producers with local slaughterhouses and cold chain systems; 
5. Study the constraints and opportunities for developing a thriving offal industry; 
6. Offer training on proper food (meat) handling practices for stakeholders across the value chain. 
 
Other recommendations for the medium-term to support the development of the meat subsector 
are: 
 
Improve public services provided by government to meat handlers and processors, or reduce 
state and local taxes on meat processing; 
 
Maintain current policy of exempting needed agro-processing machinery from customs duties 
and consider other policy and tax incentives that can stimulate private investment in 
modernization, higher value addition (e.g. for meat cuts), cold storage facilities, and waste 
disposal; 
 
Strengthen government oversight of food (meat) safety practices to comply with international 
standards; 
 
Build capacity of the Somali veterinary system to ensure competency and credibility in (meat) 
certification and in meeting international trade standards and requirements; 
 
Offer technical support and facilitate access to credit for private companies already operating 
and new entrants in this subsector on: (i) environmental impact and handling of by-products,
=== PAGE 103 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 83 
 
 
 
(ii) alternative power sources to reduce the current high cost of electricity, (iii) producing cuts 
and boneless meat instead of only carcasses as at present, and (iv) waste disposal; 
 
Collect and publish comprehensive and consistent statistics on the meat subsector, beyond 
prices; and 
 
Study, and disseminate findings on, market demand and structure, competitiveness, and entry 
requirements in major Gulf and regional markets related to frozen and chilled meat exports 
 
6.4 The hides and skins subsector 
Hides and skins, cured mostly to sun-dried or dry-salted stages with only some processed to wet- 
blue stage, though of very poor quality, barely register today in the trade statistics because of the 
extremely low price they fetch. They are nonetheless still exported to Dubai, Pakistan, UAE, China, 
and Italy, suggesting potential international demand for a revival of exports from Somalia. Animal 
slaughter facilities, skills and flaying practices (of skins from the animals), however, are so poor that 
they result in poor quality of hides and skins, thus in a high rate of rejection by importers and/or in 
very low prices (e.g. US$0.3 per skin). Diseases such as sheep and goat pox, lumpy skin, mange and 
parasites including ticks also contribute to rejection rates as high as 50% due the pre-slaughter 
defects. The situation is worsened by pervasive cultural practices, including branding and coloring of 
animals after sale in markets which is destructive to their coats (Wanyoike F, 2018). These quality 
issues are largely attributed to the fact that hides and skins are handled by non-specialized labour, 
whether in small quantities at the household level or at larger scale in slaughterhouses and 
processing facilities that use mostly second-hand and poorly maintained equipment. 
To the challenges mentioned above, the sub-sector faces many others that negatively impact 
current prospects for international competitiveness and growth, related to slaughtering, 
processing to dry salted, wet blue stages and leather stages, handling associated chemical waste, 
and marketing. They include lack of a policy and regulatory framework to guide business 
investment; skilled manpower, technical knowhow, technologies, modern machineries, and 
financing to support commercial operations; and grading, quality assurance, certification and 
knowledge export markets requirements to help with marketing (FAO 2009). Furthermore, only 
scattered information exists on the hides and skins subsector in Somalia, including on the number of 
actors and production levels at household level, traders and processing facilities. The potential 
international market demand for leather and leather products from Somalia beyond the pre-war wet 
blue processing stage for its hides and skins in its traditional and in new (e.g. China) export markets 
is also neither documented nor understood. 
Nonetheless, improving the quality of current production and marketing of hides and skins would 
provide significant opportunities to support and sustain livelihoods, especially in rural areas, as 
was indeed the case before the war. In 2017, the FAO commissioned under the ESOLT Project three 
techno-economic assessments, one of which mapped value addition opportunities in the Somali 
livestock industry with a case study on hides and skins opportunities for Puntland. This assessment 
highlighted the importance of the subsector in all regions, and its high potential in generating 
economic benefits and employment, if skins were treated again as valuable commercial assets and if 
private stakeholders would establish suitable business structures.39 
 
 
 
 
39 The other two assessments covered: a) the livestock holding ground at the port of Mogadishu and b) animal shed for 
Hargeisa.
=== PAGE 104 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 84 
 
 
 
The LSDS priority recommendations for reviving this subsector combine both those from the 
recent FAO assessments and the October-November 2018 Somali public and private stakeholders, 
as follows: 
1. Undertake value chain studies that look at the economics of production and marketing of hides 
and skins, as well as assessing requirements and preferences in existing and potential export 
markets; 
2. Collect more statistical information about the subsector (starting under the planned livestock 
population census/survey), as well as about the constraints of attracting foreign investment by 
importing countries 
3. Formulate a policy and regulatory framework to guide both private investors and public 
regulators of a possible future tanning and leather industry, while also taking into account its 
environmental impacts; 
4. Formulate subsector standards that are based on international requirements, and ensure 
optimization of local product quality and improved pricing of products; 
5. Support and strengthen organizational capacity of hides and skins trading associations 
6. Encourage private investment in technology upgrading of existing and new slaughterhouses 
and tanneries, with government playing a facilitating role, including with tax incentives or 
rebates for modern equipment; 
7. Expand access to finance for traders and processors in hides and skins trade, using also targeted 
incentives. 
 
Other recommendations for the medium-term development of the subsector are to: 
 
Establish inspection and grading systems and enforce standards (by the federal and state 
ministries, in close collaboration with states’ chambers of commerce) to ensure that local 
suppliers and traders of raw hides and skins value and respect standards to meet foreign 
requirements; 
 
Provide training and extension services to value chain actors in appropriate techniques and 
practices, including modern flaying techniques and tannery technology to enhance product 
quality, value and international competitiveness; 
 
Encourage value addition through tanning of more hides and skins into finished leather and the 
manufacturing of leather products such as footwear for the domestic market; 
 
Assess international market demand for Somali hides and skins processed beyond wet blue 
before proposing public or private investments for advanced-stage leather exports. 
 
6.5 
The poultry subsector 
Poultry production and marketing in Somalia has been mostly neglected. The sub-sector today, 
despite potential, remains underdeveloped, with poor linkages between producers, market and 
consumers. At present the domestic poultry meat sector cannot compete on price with imports of 
frozen chicken meat in large urban markets, but there is a niche market due to strong preference by 
local consumers for indigenous breeds and more naturally-fed chicken meat. As demand is 
projected to continue to increase, several new businesses have invested in poultry farms (some 
broilers but mostly layers in the Benadir region, with the largest egg production in the country, and 
also in Lower and Middle Shabelle regions of southcentral Somalia to supply Mogadishu consumers; 
layers in Somaliland). Because of these recent developments, the poultry sector has gained a 
significant role in Somalia’s food security and protein availability. 
Both semi-intensive poultry farmers and these nascent large-scale poultry farms face many 
challenges with access to affordable feed, electricity, equipment, drugs/vaccines, cold storage,
=== PAGE 105 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 85 
 
 
 
packaging, transport, water, and modern production practices. Poor knowledge of good poultry 
farming techniques, limited availability of improved breeds adapted to the local environment, and 
poor marketing information were other constraints mentioned by stakeholders. In addition, there is 
no systematic or up-to-date analysis of the Somali poultry sector in the literature and thus the 
knowledge of this subsector is very poor. 
Poultry production and marketing in Somalia has been mostly neglected. The sub-sector today, 
despite potential, remains underdeveloped, with poor linkages between producers, market and 
consumers. However, because of an expanded commercial production and related consumption, 
poultry has become increasingly important to Somalia’s food security and protein availability. At 
present the domestic poultry meat sector cannot compete on price with imports of frozen chicken 
meat in large urban markets, but there is a niche market due to strong preference by local 
consumers for indigenous breeds and more naturally-fed chicken meat. As demand is projected to 
continue to increase, several new businesses have invested in poultry farms. These produce some 
broilers but mostly layers in the Benadir region, which has the largest egg production in the country, 
in Lower and Middle Shabelle regions to supply Mogadishu consumers, and in Somaliland to supply 
Hargeisa consumers. 
Both semi-intensive poultry farmers and these nascent large-scale poultry farms face many 
challenges. These relate to poor access to affordable feed, electricity, modern equipment, 
drugs/vaccines, cold storage, packaging, transport, water, and modern production practices. Poor 
knowledge of good poultry farming techniques, limited availability of improved breeds adapted to 
the local environment, and poor marketing information were other constraints mentioned by 
stakeholders during the October-November 2018 consultations.  In addition, there is no systematic 
or up-to-date analysis of the Somali poultry sector in the literature and thus understanding of this 
subsector is very poor. 
While the NDP references poultry as a possible growth sector, there are no targets or estimated 
investments allocated to it; and the fourth Peace-building and State-building Goal (PSG) among 
the economic foundations of the New Deal for Somalia agreed by the federal government with the 
donor community in the mid-2010s did not prioritize poultry as a growth sector. As a result, there 
has been limited government support to develop the business enabling environment for the poultry 
sector. Few poultry-specific services have been developed by the private sector, and there are few, if 
any, lead firms operating on a commercial scale, providing limited opportunities to play a facilitative 
role in implementation. Thus far, there has also been minimal donor support or research in the 
poultry sector to build upon and there is no reliable data to provide any evidence that the sector will 
be able to compete with imports in the near to medium term future, which risks that interventions 
promoted by the government may not be scalable. 
Despite the poor knowledge of the sector and notable challenges mentioned above, government 
and private stakeholders believe that the available high market demand underlies a strong 
potential for expanding and upgrading the poultry subsector. Such development will have 
widespread benefits, to include higher income for producers and traders; higher revenues for all 
levels of government and poor households; creation of employment opportunities for women; and 
improved food security, quality and safety for consumers. 
Amidst such major economic and knowledge challenges, to realize a vision for this subsector to 
play a big role in contributing to food security and to supplement traditional Somali livestock as a 
second form of livelihood, various interventions can nonetheless be recommended on the basis of
=== PAGE 106 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 86 
 
 
 
stakeholders’ suggestions and available studies (see Annexes 2-8; DFID/DAI 2016; and World Bank 
and FAO, 2018). These are mainly focused on modernizing and professionalizing the entire value 
chain, as follows: 
1. Create an enabling legal and regulatory environment for private investment in the sub-sector; 
2. Explore PPP arrangements for the development of a local chicken feed mill industry to reduce 
costs of feed; 
3. Promote investments in hatcheries, if it is determined that there is sufficient demand; 
4. Expand poultry health services, by working with regional specialists to provide advisory 
support; 
5. Develop appropriate policies, regulations and guidelines for more formalized poultry sector 
hygiene, production and packaging standards; 
 
In the medium-term, this LSDS recommends the following priority actions: 
 
Conduct a full-scale poultry sector mapping and feasibility study, in order to assess the 
potential economic, environmental and social sustainability of poultry sector development in 
Somalia (layer, broiler, and traditional system where appropriate), considering the recognized 
constraints to sector development caused by shortage of production inputs (including 
production of day-old-chicks, and compound feed), technical support and skilled labor, as well 
as the stiff competition faced by imports. 
 
Revive and expand the production of maize, sorghum and promote that of soya bean for 
poultry feed, as poultry development goes along with poultry feed production (grains and 
soybean meal) and improved breeds (semi-scavenging and/or commercial layers and broilers). 
 
6.6 
Honey production and beekeeping 
As already mentioned in chapter 3, there is only anecdotal evidence about beekeeping, which 
remains a small-scale side business for most beekeepers, and about honey, whose consumption is 
limited to local markets. There are no large-scale/modern honey production farms, but only a few 
small beekeeping operations in rural areas throughout the country that use traditional hives and 
honey harvesting kits. Small-scale beekeepers are not organized in associations, with limited 
knowledge of good beekeeping practices, and no access to affordable modern production and 
personal protection equipment. Other constraints are lack of feed (deforestation) and water (in dry 
season). 
Aggregation of honey through formation of honey producer groups/cooperatives coupled with 
standardized honey processing through refining and commercial marketing has potential to  
greatly   improve   the   local   and   national   economy.    A   rapid    assessment   of    the   impact 
of improved beekeeping interventions introduced by the FAO in Somalia over the last six years has 
demonstrated several socio-economic benefits of beekeeping, especially to rural women (FAO  
2015). Unlike other agricultural enterprises, beekeeping is less affected by climate-change and 
greatly reinforces women’s income sources from honey sales during drought periods when there is 
no other source of income. Beneficiaries use income from honey sales to purchase food, invest in 
education and other household items, thus diversifying their livelihoods options. 
Furthermore, honey is a popular product, widely believed to possess medicinal properties and 
thus locally in high demand. Prices for raw honey in recent years have been very high, thus 
profitable for beekeepers. Stakeholders’ recommendations for the development of this subsector 
are for the public sector to:
=== PAGE 107 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 87 
 
 
 
1. provide extension services to current and prospective beekeepers on good production, 
handling, and preservation practices as well as on market opportunities beyond nearby towns; 
2. facilitate their access to modern production and protective equipment; 
3. support the establishment by the private sector of honey refineries, to add value; and 
4. launch an awareness campaign to educate the wider public on beekeeping as a supplementary 
source of alternative income and livelihood, and of its benefits in pollinating trees and crops. 
 
6.7 
Domestic marketing of livestock products 
There is currently no comprehensive analysis done on the consumer markets across the country, 
including for livestock products, defining the qualitative and quantitative characteristics of the 
growing urban market demand or the formal and informal market supply network that provides 
for consumers across the rural areas. Livestock product markets, nonetheless, play a critical role in 
the Somali economy, affecting both the food security and livelihood of the population. In rural areas, 
pastoralists, agro-pastoralists and agriculturalists all depend on markets to varying degrees in  
gaining access to income and food. People rely on the sales of livestock and livestock products for a 
significant percentage of their cash income and to cover substantial parts of their food needs 
through locally and imported cereal purchases. 
Markets and trade (both domestic and foreign) have, in general, proven to be dynamic and 
resilient since the collapse of the Somali State in the early 1990s. While the trade in live animals is 
closely integrated with neighbouring countries and within the greater region, most trade in livestock 
products takes place locally and domestically. Transport and domestic marketing of livestock and its 
products are entirely private sector affairs, but state and local authorities as well as non-state actors 
intervene in collection of taxes and fees for services (such as health certification following 
inspections). Livestock and livestock products marketing, aside from supply challenges, is 
constrained mainly by the lack of necessary access roads, storage and market infrastructure, as well 
as by lack of financing. 
As the draft livestock sector strategy of 2004 already noted, taxes on livestock exports and milk 
and meat transport and sales are the major source of public revenue for the areas where there are 
functioning administrations at both regional and municipal levels (FAO et. al, 2004). Stakeholders, 
however, recently reported an appalling lack of budget for livestock ministries and related salaries 
and services. These administrations, thus, should be encouraged, perhaps through some of the 
financial instruments and agreements that the international community is expected to participate in 
to re–invest a percentage of these revenues into the livestock sector by funding important services 
that will promote efficient and smooth exporting and upgrading of public food safety services and 
facilities. Potential direct beneficiaries or future “managers” of these taxes would be a possible 
Somali Livestock Board, Chambers of Commerce, municipal slaughterhouses, professional 
associations. “Unofficial” taxes (roadblocks and checkpoints) imposed along major routes in areas 
where public administration is weak should be minimized and eliminated. 
Domestic marketing and trade of dairy, ruminants’ meat and poultry products are the priorities 
that need targeted support to enable producers to access the market and satisfy a growing 
national food demand, with the domestic market believed to be able absorb much higher dairy, 
ruminants’ meat and poultry production than at present. Major goals for such support are to foster 
competition at all levels of the market chain, while reducing risk and inefficiencies at all critical 
points. Other key objectives are to encourage more diversification of markets and especially an
=== PAGE 108 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 88 
 
 
 
increase of value-addition via processing. Among the priority interventions recommended are the 
following: 
1. Ensure coordination with relevant trade authorities, such as the ministries of trade and 
affiliated bodies in the collation of livestock and livestock products relevant trade data and 
research; 
2. Survey domestic demand trends and the current state of market infrastructure, including 
mapping of key markets, related facilities, main players (companies, agents, etc.), and 
investment needs; 
3. Review the extent of state and local taxes, as well as “unofficial” charges, on traders of livestock 
products, with a view at re-directing at least part of the official tax revenues from livestock to 
enhancing essential services and infrastructures for the sector, and eliminating all “unofficial” 
charges at roadblocks; 
4. Provide clear guidelines related to the establishment of public-private partnership 
arrangements for investment in and for management of critical market infrastructure and 
services; 
5. Support and facilitate the coordination of stakeholders, through the establishment of 
representative consultative bodies that can engage all types of traders. 
 
In the medium term, other recommendations to strengthen domestic marketing of livestock 
products are to: 
 
Conduct a comprehensive review of key imports that compete in domestic markets with local 
produce and where deemed viable consider introducing policy incentives towards import 
substitution; and 
 
Ensure strong linkage of strategies and interventions in production with strategies and 
investment in domestic marketing and market access improvements. 
 
6.8 
Quality standards and food safety of livestock products 
The biggest hurdle Somali livestock and livestock products face is compliance with food safety 
requirements. Regulations such as the Meat Hygiene and Inspection Act and Dairy Hygiene Act have 
been enacted, but these regulations are not enforced by the government through inspections or 
audits of food business’ food safety practices. A Food Safety Act, however, has yet to be drafted to 
provide guidance to businesses on applying effective control throughout the food chain and on 
ensuring withdrawal from markets of unsafe food. 
The responsibility of maintaining food safety and hygiene falls on the food industry which is 
however constrained by the lack of trained veterinarians and food scientists with the required 
expertise to manage their quality assurance departments. The exception is the Somali Meat 
Company (SoMeat), which is currently seeking ISO 2200 food safety certification for its meat 
processing facilities. None of the other food industry players in Somalia have gained any 
international food safety certification. With support from various donors, the emerging milk 
processors in and around Mogadishu are requesting their suppliers to provide milk free from 
contamination, by conducting themselves quality control tests to incoming milk. These processors, 
however, are still struggling to secure their supply chain and ensure the safety and quality of their 
milk sourcing practices. 
Questions asked by potential importers, for example during the 2018 Gulfood Expo, include 
availability of documentation as proof of the quality assurance of the Somali product, as well as 
HACCP product certification. Support services such as rebuttable certification body and ISO 17025
=== PAGE 109 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 89 
 
 
 
certified food testing laboratory, however, are non-existent in the country, thus depriving the Somali 
food industry of a system to verify the quality and safety of its products. 
To enhance the competitiveness of Somali livestock and livestock product in both local and 
international markets, the following interventions should be undertaken: 
1. Develop and enact a Food Law and establish National Food Safety Commission; 
2. Establish a Food Inspectorate to strengthen enforcement of food regulations; 
3. Establish a National Food Safety Laboratory to conduct both microbiological and chemical 
analysis; 
4. Increase supervision by the federal MoLFR over the activities of all private quarantine stations; 
5. Provide training to laboratory staff who should undergo proficiency testing; and 
6. Train food industry staff in the preparation and implementation of pre-requisite and HACCP. 
 
7.0 CROSS-CUTTING ISSUES 
 
7.1 Access to Finance 
 
Somalia’s financial sector is poorly developed except for its payment system, as many years of civil 
unrest and lack of functional government destroyed the foundation for financial intermediation. 
There are many limitations in financial, technical and managerial capacity to provide basic financial 
services, and most of the rural population is still unbanked, with overall penetration rates of banking 
services at 16 percent. So far, eight banks have been registered to offer financial services in Somalia, 
but three of them are yet to start operations as at January 2019. There are several microfinance 
institutions (MFIs) operating in Somalia either as affiliated subsidiaries of commercial banks or 
registered as nongovernmental organizations (NGOs). In addition to the six main MFIs, there are 
many other local and international NGOs providing microfinance products. Microfinance regional 
coverage seems to mirror affiliated commercials, while the NGO-based MFIs are usually 
concentrated in major urban centers such as Mogadishu, Hargeisa, Garowe and Kismayo. Both 
domestic and international payment services, however, are well developed, with over 67 percent of 
the population relying on money transfers via mobile network operations (MNOs) and 11 money 
transfer businesses (MTBs), the latter relying on agents spread all over the country including in rural 
areas and transacting over US$2.7 billions per month. 
Formal financial institutions in many developing, including neighboring, countries mostly exclude 
pastoralists from their services due to pastoralists’ inability to pledge collateral, and livestock 
insurance is not available at all. Furthermore, traditionally women in pastoral and agro-pastoral 
communities have faced even greater barriers than men in accessing finance. Pastoralists’ needs 
are unlikely to be accommodated by offering the same financial products as other groups of the 
community (Getu, 2017). Financial inclusion in pastoral areas through formal institutions is 
constrained by limited financial illiteracy, pastoralists’ mobility, high transaction costs, tribal 
conflicts, and limited financial capacity. On the other hand, interest of financial institutions to 
operate in rural areas (at least via their agent and outlets), expanding mobile and interest free 
banking services, and start of sedentary way of life promise better financial inclusion for livestock 
keepers (Getu, 2017). 
In fact, few financial services, with the exception of deposit taking and money transfers, are 
available to Somali livestock sector owners and traders, whether men or women. Those involved in 
livestock trade and other related business activities continue to rely on informal sources to fund 
their business activities, limiting the speed at which those businesses can grow. Informal groups and
=== PAGE 110 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 90 
 
 
 
institutions, often organized along clan lines, are less affected by cost considerations and other 
challenges that deter formal institutions from providing finance to livestock keepers and 
entrepreneurs. Groups can also provide a savings option – a service many micro-finance institutions 
may not offer – and groups can tailor loan sizes, procedures and terms to the preferences of their 
membership. However, institutions such as Amal Bank’s micro-finance section claim that they don’t 
provide loans for livestock related investments because of high risk of animal loss due to droughts 
and disease outbreaks. There is also limited understanding of loans and insurance services, which 
contributes to low levels of using such services.
=== PAGE 111 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 91 
 
 
 
Box 7.2 - Kenya Livestock Insurance Program (KLIP) 
Pastoralism plays an essential role in Kenya's ASAL region, but the region is vulnerable to recurrent 
drought. The livestock sector accounts for over 80% of household’s incomes in Northern Kenya. The region is 
exposed to recurrent droughts which decimate livestock herds, increase poverty and increase the incidence 
of stunting. During the catastrophic droughts in 2008 to 2011 the Kenyan economy lost an estimated US$12.1 
billion, over 70% of which in the livestock sector. 
Index Insurance solution. For the past four years, the Government of Kenya (GoK) partnered with the private 
sector insurance companies to rollout a livestock insurance program targeting vulnerable pastoralists using 
index insurance (KLIP). The Government pays insurance premiums for pastoralists, and private insurance 
companies develop the insurance policy and pay claims when they arise. KLIP was launched in 2015, has 
provided protection to over 31,930 households reaching over 200,000 people and has paid out three times a 
cumulative amount of US$7.2 million. KLIP now covers 8 counties and target all 14 pastoral counties by 2020. 
 
Simplicity, innovation and speed. KLIP insures pastoralists against the loss of their livestock in case of 
weather events such as drought. Insurance payout is triggered using satellite imaging to monitor the level of 
pasture on the ground. When the level falls below an historical average (as determined in the insurance 
contract), the payout is triggered automatically. KLIP provides protection starting at the beginning of a 
season, and if a drought strikes, payouts happen quickly to beneficiaries to allow them to buy water or animal 
feed to keep the livestock alive. It is all about prevention, rather than cure, and speed of intervention. Index 
insurance allows extending access to insurance services to people who are de facto excluded, as it eliminates 
the need for insurance agents to be out in the field monitoring forage and animals, which, given the remote 
regions involved, would make livestock insurance logistically and financially impossible to provide. KLIP also 
leverages from Kenya’s innovations in the financial sector with M-PESA (mobile money) and agent banking. 
Insurance companies make payment directly to the beneficiaries primarily by M-PESA, and by using the 
network of agents developed by some banks in the northern counties. 
 
Crowding-in private sector. By using the private sector insurance companies to manage the risk of payouts, 
KLIP is crowding-in private sector capital as an effective risk management tool. Instead of facing large and 
uncertain expenditure from their budget during drought years, GOK now pays a fixed annual premium and 
pass the unknown cost to the private sector, who are better equipped to manage. 
 
Significant Impact. KLIP has successfully responded to the recent El Niño drought in northern Kenya counties 
stretching from October 2015 to 2019. An impact study of KLIP released in March 2018 showed that KLIP had 
a significant impact on communities. Many beneficiaries reported using the payouts on household needs 
such as buying food for household or paying for school fees (94%) and on their livestock needs such as buying 
supplementary feed, medicine, water and restocking (82%). Sharing of payouts with community members or 
spending them for digging a well for the entire community was reported. Another beneficiary used part of 
the payout to buy a male goat to serve the entire village by mating their female goats. 
 
Lessons learned. Success was due to: i) government supporting premium payments; ii) private insurers 
processing and paying approved claims quickly to beneficiaries; iii) existing payment infrastructure. Going 
forward, awareness creation of pastoralists needs to be stepped up, to stimulate the voluntary uptake of the 
product and ensure commercial sustainability. Other countries (Rwanda, Uganda, Ethiopia) are now 
interested in rolling out a similar program. 
 
Providing inclusive financial services to all livestock and livestock products stakeholders in 
Somalia, including the poor, is an important element for tackling poverty and bringing about 
inclusive development. Access to finance would open opportunities for pastoralists to diversify their 
livelihood options by facilitating restocking of lost livestock, purchasing inputs, establishing small 
livestock-related microenterprises, and considering other sedentary economic activities, including 
value addition. There is a need to develop an appropriate strategy to increase financial access 
throughout the sector’s value chains.
=== PAGE 112 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 92 
 
 
 
Opportunities for improving access to finance in the livestock sector include: 
1. Increased interest by financial institutions and mobile money providers to expand their 
business. Banking services and mobile money usage in Somalia is increasing rapidly, providing 
opportunity to bring more people and businesses to formal financial services. The systems for 
providing appropriate financial services are developing smoothly. Such development is 
important for general access to financial services including the livestock sector. There exists 
deliberate move to sort the challenges that limits access to finance including issuance of digital 
Identification Cards which will facilitate opening and managing bank accounts. However, a 
deliberate effort to support financial institutions and mobile money operators is necessary 
including grants for supporting financial innovations. 
2. Improving financial literacy and awareness creation. The collapse of financial services in the late 
1980s (with the collapse of the bankrupt and corrupt Commercial Bank of Somalia, the only 
retail banking institution in the country) left the majority of the population with limited option 
on financial intermediation leading to reliance on informal financial mechanism. Investment by 
hawale companies and mobile money providers after the civil war has made deposit and 
payment services available again, but people are still reluctant to start trusting and using formal 
financial services. 
3. Digital financial services provision. While the usage of mobile money reaching 76 percent of the 
population provides a huge opportunity for digital financial services (DFS), they are currently 
dominated by deposit taking and money transfers with very limited or no other financial 
services like savings, credit and insurance. With financial literacy and awareness creation, the 
saving and borrow culture could be stimulated creating market for such products. 
4. Adoption of risk transfer instruments like livestock insurance. Risk inherent in livestock 
production limit access to financial services, especially credit. Livestock sector is highly exposed 
to drought shocks which destroy the foundation for income generation. Risk transfer 
mechanism is proving to be useful tool for managing drought shocks in pastoral areas. For 
example, the Kenya Livestock Insurance Program (KLIP), a livestock insurance scheme 
implemented under private public partnership pays the insured before drought become 
disasters (see Box 7.2). The insured households have financial resources in opportune moment 
to take active measures to protect their livestock. Under the request of the FGS, the World 
Bank is exploring the technical, operational and financial viability of such scheme for Somalia, 
as well as the need for extensive education of potential beneficiaries on insurance, from basics 
to specific available options. 
 
7.2 Human Resources Development 
Building and strengthening capacity of both public and private actors, including educational policy, 
regulations and institutions, is a key element of the livestock sector strategy. However, as 
elaborated in a background paper by Mohamud and Keilie (2018) that reviewed the state of 
livestock-related education and training across the country, technical secondary and higher 
veterinary and animal production related education lack institutional regulations and standards 
usually set by governmental authorities responsible for education at the local, state or federal level. 
Neither the federal government nor its federal member states have a higher education commission 
currently in operation. Higher education in Somalia currently lacks the institutional infrastructure, 
resources and systemic governmental and institutional checks and balances that ensure effective 
institutional operations. As a result, the quality of teaching and learning is relatively weak and 
inadequate to meet the educational demands of the Somali society. The education system lacks the 
necessary resources to train or hire qualified instructors, secure adequate infrastructure.
=== PAGE 113 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 93 
 
 
 
The Somali National Education Act is currently under review and consideration in the Federal 
Parliament and may take some time before it is enacted. There is thus no effective education policy 
that guides livestock educational institutions’ management and administration, curriculum 
development, student enrolment and qualification of faculty. 
Most schools of veterinary sciences and animal husbandry lack the human, budgetary, and 
physical capacities to prepare qualified graduates with skills, field training and laboratory 
experience necessary to function independently. As a result, the vast majority of the professionals 
overseeing today veterinary clinics, quarantine stations, vaccination and treatment programs, 
disease surveillances, poultry and dairy farms are former veterinary technicians trained under the 
pre-civil war government of Somalia. 
Unlike other fields of education, veterinary schools have the added disadvantage of not attracting 
large enrolments, and especially female students. Veterinary school concentration in urban centers 
adds to the existing disconnection between the pastoral and agro-pastoral communities and 
veterinary schools training technicians or treating their animals. Fostering female enrolment and 
bringing veterinary schools and clinics closer to the pastoral and agro-pastoral communities will 
close existing gaps in veterinary service delivery. 
Regarding further qualifications, the ESOLT project, with technical support by the FAO and funding 
support by the European Union, supported the training on quarantine management practices and 
operations as well as laboratory diagnosis and interpretation of results of common transboundary 
animal diseases. This targeted Somali veterinary personnel from federal and state line ministries, 
export quarantines, diagnostic laboratories and university veterinary graduates and has contributed 
to improved knowledge of professionals on sanitary, food safety and quality standards. In addition, 
under the ESOLT project, 120 butchers and meat traders all over Somalia were trained on quality 
improvement of hides, skins, leather and leather products 
The following challenges need to be addressed: 
 
inadequate facilities such as classrooms, laboratories, libraries and research centers; 
 
limited qualifications and professional and academic experience of the teaching staff; 
 
absence of technical veterinary institutes or colleges in rural community areas; 
 
rampant unemployment for veterinarians graduating from most universities; 
 
large number of academically unprepared university students graduating from secondary 
schools; 
 
inadequate veterinary clinics for field practice; 
 
limited budgetary resources for veterinary educational programs; 
 
insufficient number of internal and external student scholarships, and especially so for 
veterinary sciences and animal husbandry studies; and 
 
poor information sharing, coordination and networking among livestock-related educational 
institutions 
 
To improve the skills of the officials, professionals and technicians involved in the livestock sector, 
this LSDS offers the following priority recommendations in order to address the above- 
summarised current constraints: 
1. Enact national education legislation and establish higher-education commissions at the federal 
and state levels to strengthen the role of government in education and enhancing the quality of 
programs offered, including in veterinary science and animal husbandry. 
2. Standardize the academic curriculum; 
3. Enhance monitoring, evaluation and enforcement of regulations over educational institutions; 
4. Build capacity of faculty to improve quality of teaching and learning;
=== PAGE 114 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 94 
 
 
43F 
44F
 
5. Provide professional development opportunities and enhance veterinary faculty networking 
among Somali institutions to improve teaching and research staff and graduating student 
knowledge; 
 
Other recommendations for the medium-term are to: 
 
Strengthen employment capacity in both the public and private parts of the livestock sector, 
including by establishing modern veterinary research institutes, enhancing the capacity of 
veterinary laboratories for disease investigation, diagnostics and research, and promoting 
modern veterinary clinics, and field practice sites with animals; 
 
Establish new veterinary technical secondary and promote veterinary colleges near pastoral 
and agro-pastoral communities, to reduce high concentration of veterinary programs in urban 
centers; and 
 
Establish veterinary education associations for information sharing, networking, research and 
professional development and a central library with technical and scientific books on veterinary 
science and technology. 
 
7.3 External Assistance for Sector Development 
This section summarizes either the evaluations or the summary self-assessments of project and 
program experience by major international development partners, in an attempt to provide an 
overview of the focus, objectives, relevance, implementation challenges, and lessons learned from 
external development assistance during the last decade. The organizations listed below contributed 
either project or program evaluations, or summary self-assessments  based  on  a  common 
template: 40
