7.3.1 
Project Objectives, Focus, and Outcomes 
External assistance during the past decade was concentrated on Somaliland, Puntland, and the 
federal government, with only one project covering southcentral Somalia. 41 The projects aimed 
mostly on strengthening animal health systems, the policy and regulatory environment, and 
private sector development, contributing to building resilience, introducing technical innovations, 
facilitating successful emergency responses, enhancing supply chains, and aiding in access of 
markets. The main topical areas were animal health and disease surveillance and control 
 
 
40 A template with general questions about the project, including project overview, outcomes, and lessons learned, among a few others, 
was sent out to the major project implementers. Only three responded using the template while the others sent their own evaluation 
reports that were completed previously. Thus, seven program self-assessments and evaluations were collected and reviewed, with IGAD- 
ICPALD sending an evaluation that covered various country and regional projects and the FAO sending a 10-year review of 60 livestock or 
livestock related projects that it implemented in Somalia. 
41 However, the FAO program review did not provide information on the geo-political areas covered.
=== PAGE 115 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 95 
 
 
 
(IGAD/ICPALD, RHAS), trade and market linkages (IGAD/ICPALD, GEEL, ESOLT) the feed and fodder 
subsector (GEEL, LIP), livestock productivity (LIP), emergency response to disasters (LIP), resilience 
(LIP, ESOLT, MVC, WALP), improving compliance of standards, increased certification, and expanding 
and upgrading small-scale infrastructure (e.g. RHAS, ESOLT, WALP). 
Activities to accomplish these goals included training sessions, workshops, knowledge and 
technology sharing, bringing together stakeholders, capacity building, creation of cooperatives, 
and trade facilitation. Projects often provided technical and financial support to ministries to 
strengthen linkages and collaboration with livestock professional associations, training 
establishments and institutions, and the private sector to enhance service delivery and training. 
Other financial support often included infrastructure or equipment for trade facilitation, central and 
satellite veterinary laboratories or inspection posts, cold chain infrastructure like solar based village 
milk cooling facilities, new livestock holding grounds and quarantine facilities, and infrastructure for 
better water management. For example, the MVC project strengthened the dairy sector through 
improved governance and management of 25 dairy cooperatives (of which 13 were newly created) 
through trainings, and improved market linkages through trainings on milk hygiene and cold chain 
facility operation and management, along with the establishment of 6 satellite cooler centres/milk 
hygiene units. 
While evaluations and self-assessments often cited outputs such as development of small-scale 
infrastructure, they also listed intermediate outcomes such as improved standard methods and 
procedures and compliance, strengthened disease early detection and surveillance, and improved 
trust and transparency across borders. They did not claim however, achieving broader final 
outcomes such as improved market access and linkages, strengthened government capacity, 
increased trade and incomes. The intermediate outcomes were due to strengthened capacities of 
public institutions to deliver and regulate animal health services through animal disease contingency 
plans, veterinary boards, animal health strategies, public, private and community partnerships in 
animal health services delivery and improved surveillance and control systems, and improved access 
to water. The WALP project, for example, facilitated the Government building of eight water sources 
that reached an estimated over 42,000 people with “improved water sources” and capable of 
providing water to 200,000 livestock, with the additional achievement of building key institutional 
capacity through technical, administrative, and community mobilization skills. 
Other general outcomes include more private sector engagement through capital investment, 
strengthened dairy and feed and fodder sectors, and community-led resilience building through 
innovative programs such as a camel leasing model (GEEL), improved market access, and increased 
incomes from improved production and sales of livestock. Other outcomes include improved 
livelihoods of beneficiaries through education, knowledge sharing, and workshops as well as 
increased collaborations among pastoralists and commercially-oriented private sector enterprises. 
For example, the ESOLT project cited enhanced networking, negotiation skills, and trade linkages for 
115 Somali livestock value chains across all project areas. 
While evaluations boasted success in several objectives, however, most of the projects focused 
only on certain areas (i.e., Somaliland and Puntland) and often on only a few districts within those 
states. Thus, while general federal and state level capacities have indeed improved, benefits have 
reached only a very limited number of pastoralist communities.
=== PAGE 116 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 96 
 
 
 
7.3.2 
Relevance and Sustainability of Program Assistance 
The objectives of external assistance outlined above were and are indeed still relevant to the 
development needs of the country, the livestock sector, and the targeted beneficiaries. When 
surveyed, stakeholder views on relevance were also in agreement. However, some major binding 
constraints have remained unaddressed. For instance, while many projects focused on animal 
health or market linkages, they did not address environmental issues such as degradation and 
deterioration of pastures and rangelands, even though these issues were widely recognized across 
projects. Similarly, few (none in southcentral Somalia) projects focused on rehabilitating watering 
facilities or fostering commercial feed supplies and markets. 
External development partners also express high confidence in the sustainability of outcomes of 
their interventions. However, given the many daunting implementation issues faced and the lack 
of rigorous impact evaluations and slow progress on domestic resource mobilization, the 
sustainability of project benefits remains unclear. Donors’ optimism is mostly related to private 
sector partner engagement, overall goodwill from state and federal authorities, gradual though slow 
improvements in the quality and numbers of ministerial staff, and good security in the northern 
states and in the capital city. Often projects had specific components that focused on creating 
ownership, e.g. training of cooperatives and individual members on long-term business strategies or 
handing over plans of specific components, thus strengthening their sustainability. The WALP 
project, for instance, had capacity building and creating ownership by getting government ministries 
to lead key aspects of the project. as two of its objectives. Similarly, many projects included 
components for developing public-private partnerships (PPPs) to ensure effective and sustainable 
management of the assets handed over. 
Most evaluations, reviews and self-assessments, however, qualify this optimistic view about the 
sustainability of external interventions as subject to continuing adequate financial support being 
available, which is in fact hardly ever assured. External financial assistance for productive sectors, 
including the livestock sector, has been and continues to be very limited and subject to wide 
fluctuations over time. Moreover, domestic resource mobilization at all government levels is still 
minimal and mostly earmarked for security and administrative needs, rather than for providing other 
essential services and public investments in the productive sectors. 
 
7.3.3 
Implementation Challenges 
A variety of challenges presented themselves during project implementation, some more specific 
to certain projects than others. The impact of interventions was often weakened due to weak 
capacity, poor human resources, narrow project design, inadequate governance arrangements, 
including stakeholders’ engagement and institutional and donor coordination, and insecurity and 
lack of infrastructures. Below is a summary of the main issues cited in project evaluations and 
program reviews undermining their effectiveness and sustainability. 
 
Weak Institutional Capacity. This was the most highlighted and binding constraint to project success. 
There are overall poor communication and coordination between federal and state ministries, 
largely due to a lack of resources, incentives and institutional knowledge (with many staff being 
relatively new). Inadequate cost recovery for services provided and no agreement on revenue 
sharing among federal, regional, and port authorities result in high dependence by the sector on 
external donor support, resulting in vulnerability to shocks when support is inconsistent or is 
abruptly cut off.
=== PAGE 117 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 97 
 
 
 
Poor Education/Skills Development. There has been a high staff turnover after training or 
workshops, thus resulting in recently acquired knowledge and skills moving outside the livestock 
sector. There is also often lack of qualified technicians for maintenance and implementation. 
Moreover, very low levels of information among stakeholders on animal husbandry best practices 
(e.g. ruminant feeding), and on food storage and hygiene (e.g., for milk and meat) have undermined 
interventions to improve animal health and food safety. Finally, there is only limited and fluctuating 
awareness among regulators of animal and products’ standards required by the OIE and importing 
countries, thus limited ability to ensure traders’ compliance to avoid import restrictions. 
Narrow Project Design and Limited Focus on Hand-Over and Management. Historically, a piecemeal 
approach to the sector and lack of advance attention and clear plans for post-investment hand-over 
and management of assets provided by donors and multilateral development agencies, such as 
fodder sheds, dairy markets, slaughterhouses, fish-processing plants and cold chains in the northern 
regions, have resulted in long delays in their operations and in some cases have undermined 
sustainability of initially positive outcomes. Focusing interventions solely on some segments of the 
supply chain, moreover, results in poor or limited upstream and downstream linkages. 
Poor Donor Coordination. There is in general poor communication and collaboration across experts, 
project designers and managers, and donors in the sector, resulting often in different projects 
addressing similar segments and gaps. There needs to be a more holistic multi-donor and multi- 
disciplinary approach to development of the livestock sector in Somalia. 
Limited Beneficiary Involvement. Another common challenge was the limited ownership of projects’ 
activities and assets by beneficiaries, which undermined the achievement of the interventions’ 
objectives and hampered long term sustainability. Lack of input by beneficiaries and inadequate 
involvement of communities at the design stage resulted often in poor identification of needs and 
solutions. Furthermore, multiple, parallel projects (as a result of poor coordination) resulted in 
limited beneficiary or stakeholder engagement in any single one. 
Poor Sector Governance. Inadequate policies, strategies, and legal/regulatory frameworks 
compound the weak human resource base. Also, the FMSs should recognize and respect the 
coordinating role of federal authorities, including on international negotiations, regulatory 
enforcement, supervision, and health certifications. Low or no budgetary allocations for staff 
compensation, moreover, results in high staff turnover, minimal or no service delivery and extension 
staff, and poor or ineffective outreach to remote rural areas and communities. 
Insecurity and Dilapidated Infrastructure. Security and access remain problematic, especially in 
southcentral Somalia. Often activities cannot be implemented because of lack of access, due to 
insecurity, exacerbated by insufficient public sector infrastructure and lack of vehicles and 
communication equipment for field activities. All such factors also often hampered information 
sharing among project implementing partners. 
 
7.3.4 
Lessons Learned 
The following are other lessons learned based on a review of donor assessments reviewed: 
 
There needs be always a major skills and capacity building component in all projects.
 
Long procurement delays and slow delivery should be expected, but local procurement and 
project implementation, though even slower, should be encouraged wherever possible, for 
capacity building.
=== PAGE 118 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 98 
 
 
 
 
Using local consultants, sourcing in-country expertise and engaging private stakeholders and 
local authorities in project design and implementation also enhances ownership and chances for 
project success.
 
Since insecurity often restricts what public officials and foreign personnel of donor organizations 
can do and where they can go, partnering with private sector and local community organizations 
is often necessary for delivering key services and monitoring project implementation and results.
 
A collaborative approach among different institutions that can leverage different expertise and 
strengths and enhance technical coordination is also critical for building synergies with other 
country and regional projects, while avoiding duplication of interventions, and thus for project 
success.
 
Value chains are complex and often require good understanding of social dynamics, as 
relationships among producers, traders and retailers can be more valued by industry 
stakeholders than pure economic gains.
 
Selection of project sites must be transparent, evidence-based and technical criteria-driven. 
Criteria ought to include access, existing facilities and resources, and realistic chances to utilize 
new ones.
 
There needs to be a continuing strong awareness of regulators and other key value chain actors 
of SPS and quality standards prescribed by the OIE and/or required by importing countries, to 
avoid trade restrictions.
 
A pooled and programmatic approach that layers different activities, with long timeframes, 
broad scope and high flexibility, is essential in fragile and complex environments such as 
Somalia.
 
Hand-over and management issues after construction of facilities, especially those of productive 
and commercial nature as mentioned above, should always be considered and planned for 
during project design;
 
Shocks and disasters should be expected and addressed in project design and resource 
allocation.
 
Multi-sectoral approaches and interventions to strengthen resilience and disaster management 
are more effective than just a one-sector (livestock-only) approach.
 
Suitable technology that is geographically, environmentally, climatically, economically and 
culturally appropriate should be used to ensure resilience, reduce risk and increase 
sustainability.
 
Local species, while less productive, are better adapted to survive the conditions of the local 
environment than foreign breeds, for which required inputs are often not available or 
sustainable.
=== PAGE 119 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 99 
 
 
 
8.0 
CONCLUSIONS AND NEXT STEPS 
 
As elaborated in chapters 2 and 3, the livestock sector remains key to economic growth and 
poverty reduction, but its performance and prospects are undermined by considerable structural 
weaknesses, severe exposure to downside risks, and vulnerability to shocks from climate change 
that can impose major damages and losses and from animal health emergencies that can lead to 
import bans. The main constraints for the sector’s development were advanced in a summary form 
in section 3.8, chapter 3 and elaborated in more details in the subsequent chapters 4-7. These can  
be grouped around seven major themes: 
1. Weak institutional and policy framework, including limited and largely informal organization of 
the livestock value chain; lack of coordination and integration among its actors; weak human 
capacity; minimal or zero budgetary resources; lack of enforcement of enforcement of existing 
rules and regulations; and poor linkages and ineffective negotiations and agreements with its 
main trading partners that also sharply limit the country’s potential in the livestock 
commodities export trade; 
2. Dilapidated water and transport infrastructure, causing water shortages due to insufficient and 
irregular and inadequately harvested rainfall and higher transport and marketing costs than 
necessary, all of which affect livestock health and survival, availability of natural fodder, and 
undermine delivery of high-quality animals to domestic and foreign markets and expansion of 
dairy production and processing; 
3. Lack of access to good quality pasture, due to land degradation and reduction of productive 
land, which is in turn the result of deforestation, soil erosion, invasion of unpalatable plant 
species, spread of private enclosures, and climate change, which has been brining increasingly 
frequent recurrence of natural shocks such as drought and floods; 
4. Poor veterinary health services and transboundary animal disease control, with occasional 
flare-up of epidemics, other livestock diseases, and pests, compounded by limited availability of 
quality veterinary education and technical training facilities in pastoral areas; 
5. Overreliance on only a few export markets and on live animal exports; 
6. Financial constraints – with very low government budgetary allocations to livestock and lack of 
private investment in processing of livestock products (dairy, meat and hides and skins mainly) 
for value addition, partly due to poor access to credit and no access to insurance, and lack of 
incentives and encouragement by governments; and 
7. Persistent insecurity in large parts of southcentral Somalia. 
 
Despite current daunting challenges, there is in fact strong scope for the sector’s recovery and 
growth in the medium- and long-term, if managed properly, in both live animals and livestock 
products. Success in tackling the sector’s constraints and harnessing its potential, however, will 
require a clear vision of the priority policy reforms and interventions needed to alleviate the most 
binding constraints and concerted action across federal and state levels to mobilize and guide public 
and private investment, with strong, coordinated support by its development partners. 
 
This livestock sector development strategy (LSDS) reflects the key needs and strategic priorities of 
the Federal Government of Somalia, its Federal Member States, and the sector’s key stakeholders 
for medium- and long-term inclusive sector growth, trade, food safety and food security beyond 
those related to the sector’s humanitarian emergency needs and short-term recovery from the 
recent 2016/2017 drought. To implement this LSDS, further work is necessary to establish 
quantitative and qualitative targets related to the objectives and recommendations discussed in the
=== PAGE 120 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 100 
 
 
 
preceding sections of chapters 4 through 7, and to explore the institutional mechanisms, 
procedures, interventions and monitoring and evaluation framework for their implementation. The 
key goals this LSDS has endorsed are to: 
 
1. Strengthen the production and marketing systems for live animals, via improving 
environmental stewardship of the country’s fragile natural resources, and in particular of its 
rangelands, including to adapt to an increasingly threatening climate change; health services, 
including the credibility of health certifications; and feed and fodder availability; 
2. Develop the many value-added opportunities available in livestock products, via promoting 
investments in processing and quality improvements, while ensuring food safety. 
 
The MoLFR has already taken a step in the direction of implementing this LSDS by identifying a 
number of preliminary operational objectives (milestones, reproduced below) and activities with 
timeframes and cost estimates, as its inputs to the National Development Plan Roadmap: 
 
Construction and rehabilitation of livestock export centers, two fodder storage facilities, genetic 
banking and artificial insemination and animal feeding centers; 
 
Enhancing livestock trade by targeting 4 million animals to be exported and completing 
vaccination of 28 million animals annually; 
 
Develop animal production through capacity building (training and equipment); 
 
Construction of national quarantine station for livestock, animal health centers, and completion 
and furnishing of the central veterinary laboratory; 
 
Development of forestry resources, wildlife protection and reforestation of degraded land by 
planting 1 million trees at national level (2019 – 2020), with 200,000 trees planted by the end of 
2019; 
 
Ending charcoal exports and reducing charcoal use in the country; and 
 
Development of rangelands and water for livestock. 
 
The FAO has made already advanced plans for piloting a proposed methodology for estimating a 
new animal population baseline through a combination of aerial and ground surveys in three 
districts in Somalia. Based on an improved methodology yet to be validated by the FGS and the 
FMSs, a full nationwide animal population survey would follow in 2020, depending on the availability 
of needed financial support from donors. 
A comprehensive and ambitious Master Plan for the sector’s development, however, needs to be 
developed in order to operationalize this LSDS’ priority and medium- and long-term term 
recommendations. Quantifiable targets for the achievement of the LSDS objectives, moreover, are 
also needed, as well as specific interventions (policy and institutional changes, technical assistance 
and investment projects), with estimated costs, proposed timelines, and roles of government and 
external development partners, that would be a more concrete translation of the often-general 
recommended interventions in chapters 4-7 of this LSDS. Under the recently approved “Water for 
Agro-pastoral Productivity and Resilience or Biyoole Project, the World Bank is providing financial 
support as of July 2019 to the MoLFR’s for the preparation of such a Master Plan and related project 
proposals.
=== PAGE 121 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 101 
 
 
 
These interventions should clarify and address the following areas: 
 
Overall sector governance and institutional reform, including assignment of responsibilities 
among all levels of governments; capacity building for the Federal Government and Federal 
Member States; participation in policy formulation, institutional reforms, and public investment 
project design and implementation by all stakeholders’, including women, youth and 
disadvantaged groups; and effective aid coordination and partnership by all levels of 
government with international donors and development agencies; 
 
Natural resource management, including of the rangelands and including land tenure, and 
climate change adaptation and mitigation; 
 
Animal production and marketing systems, including effective rangeland management; animal 
health services (and credible health certifications for exports), nutrition, and welfare; and 
necessary data and analyses for evidence-based decision making, among others; 
 
Production and marketing of dairy, meat, hides and skins (for exports), poultry and beekeeping 
products, to promote value added and ensure food security and safety; 
 
Access to modern financial services, including insurance; 
 
Human resource development, including capacity building for the public and private 
stakeholders and for technical and higher educational institutions in veterinary, food and 
animal husbandry sciences. 
 
Finally, to attract external funding to carry out such intervention, the FGS and FMSs also need to 
clarify effective implementation mechanisms and a monitoring and evaluation framework, that 
can ensure and assess the effectiveness of interventions (outputs and outcomes) and the 
performance of implementing agencies, domestic and external. Considering the many fragmented 
efforts in the livestock sector by various development partners and the limited capacity of federal 
and state ministries, the establishment of an advisory technical committee would help in the 
preparation and implementation of such a Master Plan, including via remote links. To carry out all 
such necessary next steps, and also to implement much-needed animal population survey and feed 
inventory and balance, the MoLFR is seeking both financial assistance from its development partners 
for a new technical assistance activity during the second half of 2019 and 2020. This will also ensure 
adequate input for the most important sector of the Somali economy into the National Development 
Plan 2020-2024 currently under preparation.

   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 102

Main Annex 1: Summary Overview of October-November State-Level Consultations 
 
Introduction 
 
From October 14th to November 6th, 2018, the federal Ministry of Livestock, Forestry and Range 
(MoLFR), the FAO and the World Bank carried out consultation workshops with public and private 
stakeholders from the Benadir region and all six federal member states, as part of the joint 
background preparation for a Livestock Sector Strategy (LSS) for the next 10 years.42 The workshops 
took place as listed below: 
 
Regions/States

The objectives of these consultations were to: 
 
build ownership by the federal and state governments of the development of the LSS, as well as 
strengthen the leadership of the federal government and the federal-state dialogue and 
coordination in the process
 
capture federal and state-level long-term (2030) ‘visions’ regarding the development trajectory 
for the livestock sector
 
identify critical performance issues, challenges, opportunities, and development needs of state- 
level livestock production, marketing, and processing systems (be it extensive or intensive), 
including the priority value chains (VCs) in each state
 
identify sources and gaps of knowledge and public and private sector stakeholders in each state, 
and collect available qualitative and quantitative information.
 
For each state’s 2-day long workshop, World Bank and FAO staff made five presentations to share 
with participants analytical findings from recent or ongoing studies and to guide the subsequent 
group discussions and presentations by the participants. Furthermore, senior FMS livestock ministry 
officials (federal in the case of the Benadir workshop) gave either a Powerpoint or an oral overview 
presentation of the sector’s performance, challenges and needs. This was followed by 
identification/prioritization of value chains/subsectors as summarized below (with the exception of 
Benadir and SW State), and by breakout groups’ discussion and presentations (mostly in Somali) for 
the top value chains (VCs)/subsectors, that in most cases were live animal, feed/fodder, dairy and 
meat. Poultry, hides and skins, and beekeeping followed in order of economic importance and 
potential at considerable distance.

such cases state officials and stakeholders were flown to Mogadishu.

Cross-sectoral General Findings 
While the output of the consultation workshops can be found in the Online Annexes for each state 
and for each of the main VC/subsectors, the general cross-cutting findings of these consultations 
were that: 
1. Despite the many challenges, the livestock sector has big potential across all sub-sectors, 
because of a growing local population and returning diaspora, increasing global demand for 
animal source foods, very low current production and productivity levels, and minimal 
processing; 
2. To realize this potential, what is required is: 
A. significant capacity building of public sector institutions, including for: 
i. policy, 
ii. development and enforcement of regulations, 
iii. 
ensuring quality of veterinary and animal husbandry education, and 
iv. 
data collection and analysis (via an animal population and production survey 
and a feed inventory and balance assessment); 
B. large public investments for: 
i. expansion of public services (with an emphasis on strengthening the 
veterinary services, including disease control, meeting international sanitary 
and phytosanitary standards and ensuring veracity and credibility of health 
certifications), and 
ii. rehabilitation and expansion of critical infrastructures such as for water 
catchment and road transport (Malabo Declaration), 
C. security and access to finance for the private sector to expand its current minimal 
processing of livestock products; 
3. The sector’s development and growth also needs:
=== PAGE 136 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 116 
 
 
 
a. addressing the many and vast knowledge gaps; 
b. adopting a commercialization and market orientation mindset, right down to the 
livestock owner level; 
c. moving towards a resilience (plan and prepare for shocks) and development 
(growth) focus of external assistance, in addition to ensuring emergency 
interventions at times of crises; 
d. fostering appropriate and innovative technology; 
e. ensuring coordination and collaboration among all stakeholders and external 
partners, including clarification on roles and responsibilities under PPPs; and 
f. 
devoting adequate resources to the sector. 
 
For the four main VCs/subsectors that workshop participants identified in almost all states, 
the key cross-state findings are summarized below. 
 
Live Animals: General Findings 
Current situation 
• 
Traditional production systems (and associated limitations incl. fodder, water, diseases) 
• 
Increased population of animals (but high vulnerability to shocks) 
• 
Reduced exports due to droughts and bans from KSA and UAE (exception of Hajj) 
• 
Current export market is Oman, Yemen and small shipments to Egypt. 
Constraints and gaps 
• 
Insufficient veterinary services and oversight including on certification 
• 
Failure to meet international standards and requirements of importing countries 
• 
Insufficient knowledge and data 
• 
Inconsistent implementation of regulations and policies 
• 
Overdependence on traditional production systems and limited export markets 
Opportunities and potential 
• 
Existing and growing demand for grass-fed livestock in local and international markets 
• 
Somalia's’ strategic location and proximity to the Gulf markets. 
Recommendations from state participants 
• 
Provide extension services to livestock producers (on health and animal husbandry) 
• 
Rehabilitate holding grounds and other critical infrastructure such as roads (role of public vs 
private?). 
• 
Improve laboratory and diagnostic services 
• 
Establish credible government oversight of health certification processes 
• 
Conduct comprehensive, reliable census/survey of animal population and production.

   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy

Meat: General Findings 
Current Situation 
• 
Only  one  private  sector  international  standard  abattoir  is  operational  (SoMEAT  only  to 
Oman). 
• 
Increased local market for consumption of fresh meat 
• 
Many slaughterhouses in poor state or repair 
Constraints and Gaps 
• 
Recurrent droughts 
• 
Most abattoirs and slaughterhouses not functioning properly. 
• 
Difficulty in meeting importer country standards 
• 
High transport costs for exports 
Opportunities and potential 
• 
Growing global, regional and domestic markets for quality meat 
• 
Large "grass-fed“ livestock population 
• 
Well positioned for Gulf states and other markets. 
Recommendations from state participants 
• 
Build modern slaughterhouses in cities and establish PPPs for their management 
• 
Strengthen government oversight and auditing of meat safety to comply with international 
standards 
• 
Reduce taxes on meat or improve services provided by government 
• 
Collect and publish statistics on the meat subsector, beyond FSNAU retail prices. 
 
Dairy: General Findings 
Current situation 
• 
Milk production mainly from traditional pastoral system (subsistence, poor commercial) 
• 
Camel milk is the most in demand (+ cow and also some goat milk) 
• 
Large-scale importation of milk and milk products 
• 
Lack of finance means very little processing or storage of milk. 
Constraints and gaps 
• 
Low and very seasonal production levels under pastoral system 
• 
Limited disease control and surveillance 
• 
Lack of feeds and feed formulation 
• 
Low levels of knowledge on commercial milk production 
• 
High transport costs from remote production areas 
• 
Low Access to credit and finance. 
Opportunities and potential 
• 
Large number of milk producing animals 
• 
Commercialization has started 
• 
High and growing domestic demand, from increasing urbanization and higher incomes. 
Recommendations from state participants 
• 
Improve veterinary and extension services to producers (incl. milk hygiene and 
management) 
• 
Support private sector investment in milk processing (incl. cold chains) 
• 
Strengthen government oversight and auditing of milk (and in general food) safety, including 
by establishing quality control services at collection centers and markets 
• 
Add value to milk especially during the wet season when there is plenty of supply

   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 118 
 
 
 
• 
Aim to reduce milk imports rather than become an exporter (with possible exception of 
processed or powdered camel milk) 
• 
Collect and publish statistics on the dairy sector, beyond FSNAU retail prices. 
 
Animal Feeding and Fodder: General Findings 
Current situation 
• 
Traditional grazing system, (minimal input and seasonal shortages of pasture compounded 
by breakdown of traditional management institutions) 
• 
Limited commercial feed/fodder production 
• 
Continuous overgrazing and reduction in edible biomass 
• 
Deforestation resulting in erosion and desertification. 
Constraints and gaps 
• 
Little or no effective grazing management (defunct National Range Agency) 
• 
Insecurity and low access to rangelands in parts 
• 
Expansion of enclosures (and failure to control) 
• 
Low levels of knowledge on fodder production and management. 
• 
Low access to fodder seeds and other production inputs 
• 
Poor infrastructure (storage, road, water) 
• 
Land tenure issues. 
Opportunities and potential 
• 
Increased demand of fodder and feed supplies (Commercialization, improved breeds) 
• 
Existing large-scale crop production in some states 
• 
Plenty of suitable land and water available in some states (e.g. SWS, Jubaland) 
• 
Previous rangeland investments (e.g water catchments) only need rehabilitation 
Recommendations from state participants 
• 
Carry out a feed inventory and feed balance and develop a feed and rangeland management 
strategy 
• 
Support training on feed and fodder production 
• 
Rehabilitate and manage rangelands by reestablishing a rangeland management agency and 
enforcing policy and laws (including traditional systems where relevant) 
• 
Support private sector and cooperative investment in feed and fodder production.

Main Annex 2: Summary Overview of Validation Workshop, June 17-19, 2019 
 
Livestock plays a major role in Somalia; almost half of the population living in rural areas rely on it 
and it remains the largest contributor to Somali GDP and livelihoods, economic growth and poverty 
reduction. The sector’s performance and prospects, however, are undermined by various  
production constraints such as poor animal nutrition, diseases, eroded genetic resources, and poor 
natural resource management besides other structural and institutional weaknesses. 
 
This event marks the latest in a broad-based and broadly-inclusive consultative process that was 
built on consensus across all levels of the GoS around a single vision and a set of growth and 
development priorities for the livestock sector. The outcomes of these combined activities will 
inform and guide near-term interventions toward recovery and medium- to long-term programming, 
policy reforms, and investments for growth of competitive and sustainable Somali livestock sector. 
 
The World Bank in collaboration with FAO is engaged in supporting the FGS in the development of a 
multi-year livestock sector strategy that will contribute to the improvement of local livelihoods 
based on a climate resilient and sustainable growth of the sector. 
 
The workshop which took place during the period of 17-19 June 2019 at Mogadishu’s Aden Adde 
International Airport (MIA) with the participation of a broad spectrum of sector stakeholders 
including federal and state government officials, private sector leaders, professional/industry 
associations, non-governmental organizations, academia/research institutes, livestock development 
partners and international donor community. A total number of 160 participated the validation 
workshop. 
 
The overall objective of the workshop was to present, discuss, and validate a draft document of 
Livestock Sector Development Strategy (LSDS) and to initiate a forward discussion on how to 
mobilize its implementation. The workshop drew participants from all the states and Federal 
government of Somalia and was officially opened by The Prime Minister of the Federal Republic of 
Somalia HE. Hassan Ali Kheire. He commended the organizers for their effort in organizing such a 
well-attended and all- inclusive workshop and requested for workable solutions emanating from the 
workshop to drive the livestock sector to a notch higher. Because of the importance to livestock to 
Somalis, the Prime Minister requested from Minister of Livestock to table the outcome of the 
workshop and LSDS document at upcoming Ministerial cabinet meeting. 
 
The FGS Minister of Livestock, Forestry and Range (MoLFR), Hon. Hussein Mohamud Sheikh, the 
World Bank country Representative to Somalia, Mr. Hugh Riddell, and the FAO Representative to 
Somalia Mr. Serge Tissot in their opening remarks all highlighted the importance of livestock to 
economic development of Somalia. They lamented on the drawbacks from drought to livestock 
development and impacts on livelihoods, especially loss of GDP and livelihoods. They commended 
the development of the Livestock Strategy document as a significant milestone given that its 
actualization will have far reaching impacts in overall development of the country in terms of 
improving livestock sector more sustainable and competitive in the global markets, competitive 
against a backdrop of other emerging challenges such as climate change and increased cross- 
boundary diseases and new requirements in the international livestock markets. Furthermore, they 
pleaded with the donor community to assist mobilize resources to actualize the strategy across the 
different value chains. Finally, they recommended that the strategy would require development of 
workable implementation frameworks along these chains and be integrated into the National 
Development Program and called for all to share their experiences.
=== PAGE 140 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 120 
 
 
 
 
Highlights of strategy based on sectoral presentations 
The MoLFR/FGS technical team presented on the ministry’s milestones in the National Economic 
Development Roadmap of the FGS. Key gaps for which donor community/donors could assist were 
highlighted. Areas covered included Animal Production including Feed Resource development; 
Animal Health including certification for Somalia Livestock Export and National Livestock Quarantine 
improvement; Livestock Genetic Resources; Value-addition of Livestock Products; Livestock 
Marketing; Rangelands development; Livestock census, Livestock Infrastructure development and 
Capacity development 
 
Despite current daunting challenges, there is in fact strong scope for the sector’s recovery and 
growth in the medium- and long-term, if managed properly, in both live animals and livestock 
products. Success in tackling the sector’s constraints and harnessing its potential, however, will 
require a clear vision of the priority policy reforms and interventions needed to alleviate the most 
binding constraints and concerted action across federal and state levels to mobilize and guide public 
and private investment, with strong, coordinated support by its development partners. 
 
This livestock sector development strategy (LSDS) reflects the key needs and strategic priorities of 
the Federal Government of Somalia, its Federal Member States, and the sector’s key stakeholders 
for medium- and long-term inclusive sector growth, trade, food safety and food security beyond 
those related to the sector’s humanitarian emergency needs and short-term recovery from the 
recent 2016/2017 drought. 
 
To implement this LSDS, further work is necessary to establish quantitative and qualitative targets 
related to the objectives and recommendations discussed in the preceding sections of chapters 4 
through 7, and to explore the institutional mechanisms, procedures, interventions and monitoring 
and evaluation framework for their implementation. The key goals this LSDS has endorsed are to: 
 
1) Strengthen the production and marketing systems for live animals, via  improving 
environmental stewardship of the country’s fragile natural resources, and in particular of its 
rangelands, including to adapt to an increasingly threatening climate change; health services, 
including the credibility of health certifications; and feed and fodder availability; 
 
2) Develop the many value-added opportunities available in livestock products, via promoting 
investments in processing and quality improvements, while ensuring food safety.
=== PAGE 141 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 121 
 
 
 
Main Annex 3: Somalia’ Livestock Genetic Resources 
 
Somali livestock are of a broad range of breeds that are well adapted to a nomadic way of life, 
limited feed resources and intermittent water supply. Because Somali people live in several 
countries other than geographical Somalia, some of their traditional livestock breeds are also found 
in Djibouti, Ethiopia and Kenya. 
 
Camel breeds. Somalia, one of the world’s largest dromedary camel populations are kept by 
nomadic pastoralists under traditional management. Surveys conducted during the years 1982/86 
listed two main types of camels in Somalia: (i) northern camels and (ii) southern camels. The 
northern type is divided into three main sub-types: a) Coastal camels -of northern Somalia; b) Guban 
camels -of the mountain areas of Somalia.; and c) Hawd camels -of the Nugaal area. The southern 
type is also divided into three main sub-types: d) Hoor of central Somalia, e) Siif-da’ar of south 
central Somalia and f) Eydimo of western Somalia. 
 
Cattle breeds. The cattle of Somalia are mainly the East African Zebu type of which the following 
types are recognized, the Somali Boran, Dauara and Surqo (Rege and Tawah 1999; Nur 2005). The 
Somali Boran are believed to be a descendant of the first introduction of zebu into Africa from West 
Asia and are thought to have evolved following the migration of Ethiopian cattle into Somalia and 
Jubaland along the Somalia-Ethiopia border (Rege and Tawah 1999). The Surqo breed is a zenga 
breed (Rege and Tawah 1999; Nur 2005). The zenga breeds are breeds that resulted from zebu- 
sanga crosses that came about following the introduction of zebu cattle into Africa from Asia. The 
Surqo breed is a crossbred of the Boran of Somalia or Ethiopia with an unknown sanga population 
(Rege and Tawah 1999). The Zebu cattle of Somalia mainly belong to a group designated as “Small 
Zebus of the Somalia”. The main “breeds” are North Somali, Gasara and Garre. Boran cattle similar 
to those of southern Ethiopia and northern Kenya are found along Somalia’s border with these 
countries. The Jiddu or Tuni is an Intermediate Sanga/Zebu type with long horns and possibly has a 
certain degree of tolerance of trypanosomosis. 
 
Goat breeds. The main types of goats found in Somalia, are the Long-eared Somali goat, the Short- 
eared Somali goat, and, to a lesser extent, the Somali Arab goat. The Somali Long-eared goat is 
thought to be a descendent of the Somali, Arab goats, which were introduced form Arabia (FARM- 
Africa 1996). The Somali Arab goat, as the name implies, was introduced into Africa by Arabian 
traders. Almost all Somali goats are short eared. Breeds of this type are classed as “Somali” In the 
traditional Somali descriptive systems the ‘short eared/ degiar’ is smaller than the ‘degwein’ and has 
short prick ears in contrast to the forward inclined Pendent ears of the latter. The goat is of small to 
medium size with males weighing 30–50 kg and females 25–40 kg. A further short eared goat is the 
Arab (which in this context is a Somali clan name). It is the smallest of the Somali goats and is kept by 
coastal dwellers in the vicinity of towns. The goat is prolific with many twin and not a few triplet 
births being the rule. Some long eared (‘deguein’) milk goats are kept in villages and towns. These 
goats are usually known as Benadir and mainly occur in the southern half of Somalia. 
 
Sheep breeds. The Somali or Black Head Somali is the main sheep breed. This fat–rumped meat 
breed is found throughout Somalia. It is highly appreciated as a meat animal by people in the 
countries to which Somalia exports livestock. Somali sheep are the immediate ancestor of the Black 
Head Persian which was developed in South Africa in the late nineteenth and early twentieth 
century’s and that has been widely used for crossbreeding in many parts of Africa and elsewhere in 
the tropics. The classic color, as suggested by the name, is white with a black head. The fat rump is 
the most characteristic feature.
=== PAGE 142 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 122 
 
 
 
Main Annex 4: Livestock Sector Statistics 
 
The last livestock census in Somalia was done in 1975. Until the late 1980s, the government conducted 
reliable sample surveys of the livestock population, which was estimated at about 40 million for 1988 
(Somalia 1989; FAOSTAT). Since then, animal population estimates have been based largely on 
projections and extrapolations by external agencies with very limited current information or access to 
many parts of the country. One estimate suggested a livestock population of 39 million in 1998 (FAO, 
World Bank and EU 2004). 
 
New baseline estimates for 2014 were prepared by the FSNAU in October 2017 and agreed to as pre- 
drought baseline figures by the representatives of MoLFR, the federal Ministry of Planning and 
International Cooperation, the livestock ministries of FMSs, and their international partners for the 
Drought Impact Needs Assessment (Somalia 2018a). These desk estimates are reproduced in Statistical 
Annex SA2.1 of this report (LSDS). They placed the country’s livestock in 2014 at 52.9 million head, 
including 28.7 million goats, 13.6 million sheep, 6.6 million camels, and 3.9 million head of cattle. The 
FSNAU figures for goats were much larger and the figures for camels and cattle somewhat smaller than 
previous estimates by other sources and their regional distribution is also different (see World Bank-FAO 
2018b, section 5.7, pp 56-58). 
 
In addition to livestock exports which are easier to monitor, a significant number of animals are marketed 
domestically, generating employment for the local population, especially women, who often sell 
domestic meat and produce animal byproducts, including milk, soaps, and jewelry from bones. In the 
early 2000s, the FAO estimated offtake percentages at 27.3 percent for sheep, 23.3 percent for goats, 
11.3 percent for cattle, and 1.6 percent for camels (FAO 2004). Livestock product guess-estimates are  
also available in Somalia 2018 and chapter 5 of World Bank-FAO 2018. 
 
All such point estimates, however, are highly unreliable, given the high uncertainties about demographic 
parameters, recurrent droughts (and associated mortalities), and frequent but unpredictable cycles of 
destocking and restocking. They are also mostly inconsistent with previous ones, as their methodology 
cannot be used to generate plausible time series. For instance, the October 2017 point estimate by 
FSNAU of livestock population were derived by taking very disaggregated estimates of human population 
and household sizes (from UNFPA 2014 and from NDP 2017-2019) and multiplying household size by the 
average number of animals per household, as found in various livelihood surveys of pastoral households 
in limited geographical areas conducted at different times over the past decade (FSNAU and FEWS-NET 
2016). Applying the same parameters and assumptions to smoothly growing estimates of human 
population would result in equally smoothly growing estimates for livestock, without regards to higher 
mortality due to recurrent shocks like droughts or higher asset accumulation due to unpredictable export 
bans. 
 
The FAOSTAT database shows figures, including those for livestock products, annually over a long time, 
but the economic team working on World Bank-FAO 2018b found them unreliable and implausible. They 
often appear arbitrary and/ or smoothly extrapolated or flat. Furthermore, no FAOSO staff has any 
knowledge of who in government has been reporting these figures to FAOSTAT in Rome or how these 
figures have been validated. They seem to have been reported by (former) government officials directly 
to FAOSTAT in Rome even during the civil war years when no (recognized) government was in power. 
Other livestock (e.g. TLU) estimates in any other national or international databases are equally 
unreliable. 
 
Between February and March 2016, the World Bank in collaboration with Somali statistical authorities 
conducted a first wave of the Somali High Frequency Survey (HFS) in all accessible areas of nine regions 
within Somaliland, Puntland and southern Somalia (World Bank 2016). The survey interviewed 2,882 
urban households, 822 rural and 413 households in Internally Displaced People (IDP) settlements. The 
sample was drawn randomly based on a multi-level clustered design. The second wave was conducted in
=== PAGE 143 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 123 
 
 
 
2018 and its overall analysis has become available in early 2019 (World Bank 2019; Questionnaire for 
Wave 2 is available online). This dataset includes comprehensive information on assets and consumption, 
to allow estimation of poverty rates. Its questionnaire also included 39 questions related to livestock 
(Section G), but whose responses save two have not yet been analyzed. 
 
A Pilot Livestock Survey limited to two districts was undertaken by the FAO during 2008-2009. It had 
been planned to cover two contrasting districts, Oodweyn in Somaliland and Jowhar in southern Somalia, 
but escalating insecurity and cost-saving considerations forced a suspension of activities in Jowhar and 
forced a shift of the second district to Borama, also in Somaliland. The pilot survey combined use of 
aerial and ground survey teams, with the former designed to estimate the number of households/ bomas 
in the districts plus the numbers of animals. 
 
Through the implementation of this pilot project that undertook wet and dry season surveys in two 
contrasting districts, the methodology for data collection, entry, analysis and evaluation was tested; 
qualified Somali experts were integrated into the implementation team; government authorities (i.e. 
ministries of livestock and planning and local district authorities from Borama and Oodweyn ) took part in 
the implementation of the project; a total of 47 personnel as ground teams were identified and trained to 
undertake the ground surveys and thereby building capacity for future such surveys; questionnaires were 
developed and tested during the pilot study; and data analysis and evaluation of the methodology was 
conducted. Finally, stakeholder meetings were also held in Hargeisa, Mogadishu and Nairobi to present 
the findings and obtain feedback. The pilot survey’s final report and its annexes do highlight the strengths 
and weaknesses of the aerial and ground surveys and how both can be utilized for a more 
comprehensive, national baseline animal population survey. 
 
The general lessons from the Pilot Survey’s final report that will contribute to an improved and more 
robust methodology in the design of a future national survey were as follows (for original lessons, see 
FAO 2010, p. 17): 
 
 
Unforeseen possible deterioration in security conditions requires that future project’s scope, 
methodology and timelines be flexible, robust, and realistic;
 
Advance simulation is needed to determine the sample size for the aerial survey for better accuracy 
of estimating animal numbers and smaller gaps in comparison with ground survey estimates;
 
Given the shortage of skilled workers in the country, including trained personnel on data collection, 
entry, analysis and evaluation at all levels, detailed training of field personnel (ground teams) is 
needed for a better understanding of data collection methodology;
 
Adequate field-testing of the questionnaires is important.
 
Data analysis is to be standardized for better understanding of results presentation.
 
Funding should be adequate and cost-sharing arrangements should be clarified to carry out all the 
necessary steps to finally have reliable animal numbers.
More specifically, other lessons recommended by the University of Bristol to improve quality of 
responses and data from ground surveys and which would make use of more advanced statistical 
methods a possibility were as follows: 
 
Some questions from the ground surveys will need re-writing and it is particularly important that the 
livestock number questions are thoroughly tested before they are used in the future.
 
It is essential that respondents and fieldworkers understand questions and range of expected 
responses.
 
Fieldworkers and data processors should be fully trained to check for data inconsistencies and the 
entry of data should be thoroughly checked before being sent for analysis.
 
Much time and effort were invested in removing the many errors from the data. Some errors could 
be avoided by investing more time in the design of the database, for example, with more drop-down 
boxes and validation rules incorporated. To reduce the time required to clean the data and to allow 
for random checks where validation is not possible, the person analyzing the data should receive
=== PAGE 144 ===
   Somalia Livestock Sector Development Strategy 
 
- 124 
 
 
 
copies of the completed paper survey forms. One particular error was the usual survey problem of 
confusing missing values with zero. While some missing values were recorded as zero, the main 
problem for this pilot study was that many zeros had been recorded as missing values. Training and a 
code book is needed for the data processors containing the possible responses to questions under 
different scenarios.
